Tesler identities for wreath Macdonald polynomials
Marino Romero, Joshua Jeishing Wen

Abstract
. We give an explicit formula for an operator that sends a wreath Macdonald polynomial to the delta function at a character associated to its partition. This allows us to prove many new results for wreath Macdonald polynomials, especially pertaining to reciprocity: Macdonald–Koornwinder duality, evaluation formulas, etc. Additionally, we initiate the study of wreath interpolation Macdonald polynomials, derive a plethystic formula for wreath ( q , t ) (q,t) -Kostka coefficients, and present series solutions to the bispectral problem involving wreath Macdonald operators. Our approach is to use the eigenoperators for wreath Macdonald polynomials that have been produced from quantum toroidal and shuffle algebras.



2020 Mathematics Subject Classification
Primary: 05E05, 81R10; Secondary: 33D52, 81R12.

1.
Introduction

Let
\Lambda
be the ring of symmetric functions (in infinitely many variables), and set
\Lambda_{q,t}\coloneq\mathbb{C}(q,t)\otimes\Lambda
.
The
modified Macdonald polynomials

\{H_{\lambda}\}
are a distinguished basis of
\Lambda_{q,t}
that have played a large role in algebraic combinatorics, algebraic geometry, integrable systems, knot theory, probability, quantum algebra, and other fields.
Macdonald polynomials satisfy an interesting and useful duality that is hinted at by
Macdonald reciprocity
.
In terms of
H_{\lambda}
, it may be expressed as follows.
For a partition
\lambda
, let us view
\lambda
as its set of boxes in the French convention; we assign each box a coordinate in
\mathbb{Z}^{2}
, with the box in the bottom-left corner assigned to
(0,0)
.
Write

\displaystyle B_{\lambda}\coloneq\sum_{(a,b)\in\lambda}q^{a}t^{b}

and

\displaystyle D_{\lambda}\coloneq(1-q)(1-t)B_{\lambda}-1.

Reciprocity then takes the following form: for partitions
\lambda
and
\mu
,

(1.1)

\frac{H_{\lambda}[D_{\mu}]}{H_{\lambda}[-1]}=\frac{H_{\mu}[D_{\lambda}]}{H_{\mu}[-1]}.

Here, the evaluations should be interpreted plethystically.
Equation (
1.1
) was proved by Koornwinder; a proof can also be found in
[
Mac15
]
.

The perspective we will take in accessing (
1.1
) and its deeper aspects comes from work of Garsia–Haiman–Tesler
[
GHT99
]
, which developed and refined ideas underlying the Garsia–Tesler proof of
Macdonald integrality

[
GT96
]
.
In
[
GHT99
]
, the authors prove what we call the
Tesler identity
.
Namely, let

\displaystyle\Omega[X]

\displaystyle\coloneq\exp\left(\sum_{k>0}\frac{p_{k}}{k}\right),

\displaystyle\TT(f)

\displaystyle\coloneq f[X+1],

\displaystyle\nabla H_{\lambda}

\displaystyle\coloneq\left(\prod_{(a,b)\in\lambda}(-q^{a}t^{b})\right)H_{\lambda},

\displaystyle\mathsf{V}

\displaystyle\coloneq\nabla\Omega\left[\frac{X}{(1-q)(t-1)}\right]\TT\nabla.

The identity is then

(1.2)

\Omega\left[\frac{XD_{\lambda}}{(1-q)(t-1)}\right]=\mathsf{V}\left(\frac{H_{\lambda}}{H_{\lambda}[-1]}\right).

Conceptually, the left-hand-side of (
1.2
) is a delta function with respect to the
modified Macdonald pairing

\langle-,-\rangle_{q,t}^{\prime}
on
\Lambda_{q,t}
:

\left\langle\Omega\left[\frac{XD_{\lambda}}{(1-q)(t-1)}\right],f\right\rangle_{q,t}^{\prime}=f[D_{\lambda}].

The reciprocity (
1.1
) is a combination of (
1.2
) and the fact that
\mathsf{V}
is self-adjoint with respect to
\langle-,-\rangle_{q,t}^{\prime}
:

\left\langle\frac{H_{\lambda}}{H_{\lambda}[-1]},\mathsf{V}\left(\frac{H_{\mu}}{H_{\mu}[-1]}\right)\right\rangle_{q,t}^{\prime}=\left\langle\mathsf{V}\left(\frac{H_{\lambda}}{H_{\lambda}[-1]}\right),\frac{H_{\mu}}{H_{\mu}[-1]}\right\rangle_{q,t}^{\prime}.

Playing similar games with
\mathsf{V}
and
\langle-,-\rangle_{q,t}^{\prime}
, one can also prove
Macdonald–Koornwinder duality
: introducing an extra variable
u
,

(1.3)

\frac{H_{\lambda}[1+uD_{\mu}]}{\displaystyle\prod_{(a,b)\in\lambda}(1-uq^{a}t^{b})}=\frac{H_{\mu}[1+uD_{\lambda}]}{\displaystyle\prod_{(a,b)\in\mu}(1-uq^{a}t^{b})}.

Finally, we note that setting
\mu=\varnothing
in (
1.3
) yields the
evaluation formula
:

(1.4)

H_{\lambda}[1-u]=\prod_{(a,b)\in\lambda}(1-uq^{a}t^{b}).

These are but a small fraction of the results one can tease out of the Tesler identity (
1.2
).

Beyond type
A
, an analogue of (
1.1
) was proved by Cherednik
[
Che95
]
.
Cherednik worked in the finite-variable setting, and he provided an underlying conceptual structure that explains the duality: the spherical
double affine Hecke algebra
(DAHA) and its automorphism
\varphi
.
To describe this, let us highlight two ways
f\in\Lambda_{q,t}
can act on
\Lambda_{q,t}
: (1) via multiplication and (2) via its
Delta operator

\Delta[f]
, which acts diagonally on the basis
\{H_{\lambda}\}
by setting

\Delta[f](H_{\lambda})=f[D_{\lambda}]H_{\lambda}.

In the unmodified, finite-variable setting, the Delta operators are related to the
Macdonald operators
.
The spherical subalgebra of the DAHA can be viewed as the algebra generated by multiplication and Macdonald operators, and
\varphi
sends the former to the latter (but not vice-versa).
From this perspective,
\mathsf{V}
also plays a role (cf.
[
GM19
]
): for
f\in\Lambda_{q,t}
,

(1.5)

\Delta[f]\mathsf{V}=\mathsf{V}f,

where on the right-hand-side, we view
f
as its multiplication operator.
Thus, commuting past
\mathsf{V}
right-to-left performs a version of
\varphi
in infinitely many variables.
In the infinite-variable setting, the spherical DAHA is replaced with the
elliptic Hall algebra
(EHA) and
\varphi
is replaced with the
Miki automorphism

[
SV13
]
.
Quite interestingly, shadows of the EHA are all over
[
GHT99
]
, a decade before the explosion of interest for this algebra in the 2010s.
This is because the EHA is the algebraic structure built out of Jing’s vertex operators for Macdonald polynomials
[
Jin94
]
, and these vertex operators are a key ingredient in
[
GHT99
]
.

1.1.
Wreath Macdonald polynomials

This paper is concerned with generalizing the picture above to the
wreath Macdonald polynomials
defined by Haiman
[
Hai03
]
.
Fix an integer
r\geq 1
.
The wreath Macdonald polynomials are elements of the tensor power
\Lambda_{q,t}^{\otimes r}
.
They are still indexed by a single partition and reduce to usual modified Macdonald polynomials when
r=1
.
We will therefore denote them again by
\{H_{\lambda}\}
.
An important ingredient of their definition is the
core-quotient decomposition
of a partition, which yields a bijection:

\displaystyle\{\hbox{partitions}\}

\displaystyle\leftrightarrow\{\hbox{$r$-core partitions}\}\times\{\hbox{$r$-tuples of partitions}\}

\displaystyle\lambda

\displaystyle\mapsto\left(\mathrm{core}(\lambda),\mathrm{quot}(\lambda)\right).

We review this decomposition in
2.1
below.
The decomposition depends on
r
, but as
r
is fixed throughout, we will drop it from the notation.
Fixing an
r
-core
\alpha
,
\{H_{\lambda}\,|\,\mathrm{core}(\lambda)=\alpha\}
gives a basis of
\Lambda_{q,t}^{\otimes r}
.
The existence of
H_{\lambda}
was first proved by
[
BF14
]
, which also proved an analogue of
Macdonald positivity
.

The notion of “colors” permeates wreath Macdonald theory.
First, we index the tensorands of
\Lambda_{q,t}^{\otimes r}
with
\mathbb{Z}/r\mathbb{Z}
and denote by
p_{n}[X^{(i)}]
the element with
p_{n}
in the
i
th tensorand, i.e. the color
i
power sum. With this indexing,
X^{(i)}=X^{(j)}
if
i=j\hbox{ mod }r
.
A general element
f\in\Lambda_{q,t}^{\otimes r}
depends on all
r
families of variables, and so we denote it by
f[X^{\bullet}]
.
Plethysm in this setting takes on a richer character because we can take nontrivial linear maps across different colors.
Some important examples of this are the following:

\displaystyle p_{n}[\iota X^{(i)}]

\displaystyle\coloneq p_{n}[X^{(-i)}],

\displaystyle p_{n}[\sigma X^{(i)}]

\displaystyle\coloneq p_{n}[X^{(i+1)}],

\displaystyle p_{n}[(1-q\sigma)X^{(i)}]

\displaystyle\coloneq p_{n}[X^{(i)}]-q^{n}p_{n}[X^{(i+1)}],

\displaystyle p_{n}\left[\frac{X^{(i)}}{(1-q\sigma)}\right]

\displaystyle\coloneq\frac{\sum_{j=0}^{r-1}q^{j}p_{n}[X^{(i+j)}]}{1-q^{nr}}=p_{n}[(1-q\sigma)^{-1}X^{(i)}].

Likewise, we have a translation operator
\TT
for each variable color, which we denote by
\TT[X^{(i)}]
.

To each box
\square=(a,b)
in a partition, we assign its color to be
\bar{c}_{\square}\coloneq b-a\hbox{ mod }r
.
In French convention, this is the SW-to-NE diagonal that the box lies on, modulo
r
.
Correspondingly, we assign the color
b-a\hbox{ mod }r
to the character
q^{a}t^{b}
, and set

\displaystyle B_{\lambda}^{(i)}

\displaystyle\coloneq\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\square=(a,b)\in\lambda\\
\bar{c}_{\square}=i\end{subarray}}q^{a}t^{b},

\displaystyle B_{\lambda}^{\bullet}

\displaystyle\coloneq\sum_{i\in\mathbb{Z}/r\mathbb{Z}}B_{\lambda}^{(i)},

\displaystyle D_{\lambda}^{(i)}

\displaystyle\coloneq(1+qt)B^{(i)}-qB^{(i+1)}-tB^{(i-1)}-\delta_{i,0},

\displaystyle D_{\lambda}^{\bullet}

\displaystyle\coloneq\sum_{i\in\mathbb{Z}/r\mathbb{Z}}D_{\lambda}^{(i)}=(1-q)(1-t)B_{\lambda}^{\bullet}-1.

When performing plethystic evaluations, we will assign
X^{(i)}
to characters of color
i
.
For instance,

f[D_{\lambda}^{\bullet}]\coloneq f[X^{\bullet}]\,\bigg|_{X^{(i)}\mapsto D_{\lambda}^{(i)}}.

Finally, color also plays a role in our notion of nabla operators.
Recall that the wreath Macdonald polynomials give a basis of
\Lambda_{q,t}^{\otimes r}
only after restricting to partitions with a fixed
r
-core
\alpha
.
For
\lambda
with
\mathrm{core}(\lambda)=\alpha
, we then define

\nabla_{\alpha}H_{\lambda}=\left(\prod_{\begin{subarray}{c}\square=(a,b)\in\lambda\backslash\alpha\\
\bar{c}_{\square}=0\end{subarray}}(-q^{a}t^{b})\right)H_{\lambda}

and then define
\nabla_{\alpha}f
by linearity.
Our notion of the nabla operator is significantly simpler than the one posed in
[
OS23
]
.

1.1.1.
Main result

Let

\mathbb{E}_{\lambda}=\Omega\left[\sum_{i\in\mathbb{Z}/r\mathbb{Z}}X^{(i)}\left(\frac{D_{\lambda}^{\bullet}}{(1-q)(t-1)}\right)^{(i)}\right],

where in assigning colors to
D_{\lambda}^{\bullet}/(1-q)(1-t)
, we expand it into a power series assuming
|q|,|t|<1
.
There is a natural analogue of the modified Macdonald pairing, which we also denote by
\langle-,-\rangle_{q,t}^{\prime}
, and
\mathbb{E}_{\lambda}
plays the role of a delta function (Corollary
4.10
):

\langle\mathbb{E}_{\lambda},f\rangle_{q,t}^{\prime}=f[X^{\bullet}]\,\bigg|_{X^{(i)}\mapsto D_{\lambda}^{(-i)}}\eqcolon f[\iota D_{\lambda}^{\bullet}].

The main theorem of this paper is the following wreath analogue of (
1.2
):

Theorem 1.1
.

Fix
r>2
.
For an
r
-core
\alpha
, let

\mathsf{V}_{\alpha}\coloneq\nabla_{\alpha}\Omega\left[\frac{X^{(0)}}{(1-q\sigma^{-1})(t\sigma-1)}\right]\TT[X^{(0)}]\nabla_{\alpha}.

We then have
\nabla_{\mathrm{core}(\lambda)}H_{\lambda}=H_{\lambda}[\iota D_{w_{0}\mathrm{core}(\lambda)}^{\bullet}]H_{\lambda}
and

(1.6)

\mathbb{E}_{\lambda}=\mathsf{V}_{\mathrm{core}(\lambda)}\left(\frac{H_{\lambda}}{H_{\lambda}[\iota D_{w_{0}\mathrm{core}(\lambda)}^{\bullet}]}\right).

1.1.2.
Symmetry of nabla

The
w_{0}\mathrm{core}(\lambda)
appearing in the denominator on the right-hand-side of (
1.6
) comes from an action of the symmetric group
\Sigma_{r}
on partitions, defined in
2.1.4
; the element
w_{0}\in\Sigma_{r}
is the longest element.
Another permutation that plays well with our machinery is the long cycle
\sigma
.
For any permutation
w
, we have
w\mathrm{core}(\lambda)=\mathrm{core}(w\lambda)
.

Concerning nabla, there are two significant new features in the wreath case:

•

Besides
1
, every eigenvalue of
\nabla_{\alpha}
is degenerate.

•

\nabla_{\alpha}
is not self-adjoint with respect to
\langle-,-\rangle_{q,t}^{\prime}
; its adjoint is
\nabla_{w_{0}\alpha}
.

The second feature is basically responsible for
w_{0}\mathrm{core}(\lambda)
appearing in (
1.6
).
The first feature gives room to produce variations on (
1.6
).
For instance, for
k\in\mathbb{Z}/r\mathbb{Z}
, we have the following (Lemma
5.6
):

\nabla_{\sigma^{-k}\mathrm{core}(\lambda)}H_{\lambda}[\sigma^{-k}X^{\bullet}]=\left(\prod_{\begin{subarray}{c}\square=(a,b)\in\lambda\backslash\alpha\\
\bar{c}_{\square}=0\end{subarray}}(-q^{a}t^{b})\right)H_{\lambda}[\sigma^{-k}X^{\bullet}].

Thus, we can compensate for the
\sigma^{-k}
plethysm by also changing the core parameter on nabla; we emphasize that this is a nontrivial symmetry.
This leads to the following shifted variant of Theorem
1.1
:

Theorem 1.2
.

Fix
r>2
and
k\in\mathbb{Z}/r\mathbb{Z}
.
Let

\mathbb{E}_{\lambda}^{(k)}=\Omega\left[\sum_{i\in\mathbb{Z}/r\mathbb{Z}}X^{(i)}\left(\frac{D_{\lambda}^{\bullet}}{(1-q)(t-1)}\right)^{(i+k)}\right].

For a
r
-core
\alpha
, we set

\mathsf{V}_{\alpha}^{(k)}\coloneq\nabla_{\sigma^{-k}\alpha}\Omega\left[\frac{X^{(-k)}}{(1-q\sigma^{-1})(t\sigma-1)}\right]\TT[X^{(-k)}]\nabla_{\sigma^{-k}\alpha}.

We then have

(1.7)

\mathbb{E}_{\lambda}^{(k)}=\mathsf{V}_{\mathrm{core}(\lambda)}^{(k)}\left(\frac{H_{\lambda}[\sigma^{-k}X^{\bullet}]}{H_{\lambda}[\iota D_{\mathrm{core}(w_{0}\lambda)}^{\bullet}]}\right).

Let us note that
\mathbb{E}_{\lambda}^{(k)}
is also some form of delta function (Proposition
5.2
):

\langle\mathbb{E}_{\lambda}^{(k)},f\rangle_{q,t}^{\prime}=f[X^{\bullet}]\,\bigg|_{X^{(i)}\mapsto D_{\lambda}^{(k-i)}}\eqcolon f[\sigma^{k}\iota D_{\lambda}^{\bullet}].

1.1.3.
Applications

As in
[
GHT99
]
, Theorem
1.1
and its shifted generalization, Theorem
1.2
, have many immediate applications.
In the wreath case, however, these all yield new results.
We cover these consequences in Section
6
.
Here is a list:

(1)

We prove an analogue of Macdonald–Koornwinder duality (
1.3
).
For
k\in\mathbb{Z}/r\mathbb{Z}
and partitions
\lambda
and
\mu
with
\mathrm{core}(\mu)=w_{0}\sigma^{-k}\mathrm{core}(\lambda)
, this takes the following form:

(1.8)

\frac{H_{\lambda}[1+u\sigma^{k}\iota D_{\mu}^{\bullet}]}{\displaystyle\prod_{\begin{subarray}{c}\square=(a,b)\in\lambda\backslash\mathrm{core}(\lambda)\\
\bar{c}_{\square}=k\end{subarray}}(1-uq^{a}t^{b})}=\frac{H_{\mu}[1+u\sigma^{k}\iota D_{\lambda}^{\bullet}]}{\displaystyle\prod_{\begin{subarray}{c}\square=(a,b)\in\mu\backslash\mathrm{core}(\mu)\\
\bar{c}_{\square}=k\end{subarray}}(1-uq^{a}t^{b})}.

Note that if we fix
\lambda
,
\mathrm{core}(\mu)
may change as we vary
k
.

(2)

Setting
\mu=w_{0}\sigma^{-k}\mathrm{core}(\lambda)
in (
1.8
), we obtain the evaluation formula:

(1.9)

H_{\lambda}[1+u\sigma^{k}\iota D_{w_{0}\sigma^{-k}\mathrm{core}(\lambda)}^{\bullet}]=\prod_{\begin{subarray}{c}\square=(a,b)\in\lambda\backslash\mathrm{core}(\lambda)\\
\bar{c}_{\square}=k\end{subarray}}(1-uq^{a}t^{b}).

This proves an evaluation conjecture used in
[
AD24
]
.

(3)

Taking
u\rightarrow\infty
in (
1.9
), we obtain

H_{\lambda}[\sigma^{k}\iota D_{w_{0}\sigma^{-k}\mathrm{core}(\lambda)}^{\bullet}]=\prod_{\begin{subarray}{c}\square=(a,b)\in\lambda\backslash\mathrm{core}(\lambda)\\
\bar{c}_{\square}=k\end{subarray}}(-q^{a}t^{b}).

Note that the case
k=0
gives a formula for the evaluation
H_{\lambda}[\iota D_{w_{0}\mathrm{core}(\lambda)}^{\bullet}]
appearing in the denominator of (
1.6
) and (
1.7
).
It also coincides with the eigenvalue of
\nabla_{\mathrm{core}(\lambda)}
at
H_{\lambda}
.

(4)

Taking
u\rightarrow\infty
in (
1.8
), we obtain a shifted reciprocity: if
\mathrm{core}(\mu)=w_{0}\sigma^{-k}\mathrm{core}(\lambda)
,

\frac{H_{\lambda}[\sigma^{k}\iota D_{\mu}^{\bullet}]}{H_{\lambda}[\sigma^{k}\iota D_{\mathrm{core}(\mu)}^{\bullet}]}=\frac{H_{\mu}[\sigma^{k}\iota D_{\lambda}^{\bullet}]}{H_{\mu}[\sigma^{k}\iota D_{\mathrm{core}(\lambda)}^{\bullet}]}.

(5)

We define an analogue of the Fourier pairing from
[
Che95
]
and prove an analogue of
[
Che97
, Theorem 1.2]
: for
\lambda
and
\mu
with
\mathrm{core}(\mu)=w_{0}\mathrm{core}(\lambda)
,

\displaystyle\left\langle\TT[X^{(0)}]\nabla_{\mathrm{core}(\mu)}H_{\mu},\TT[X^{(0)}]\nabla_{\mathrm{core}(\lambda)}H_{\lambda}\right\rangle_{q,t}^{\prime}

\displaystyle=H_{\lambda}[\iota D_{\mu}^{\bullet}]H_{\mu}[\iota D_{\mathrm{core}(\lambda)}^{\bullet}]=H_{\mu}[\iota D_{\lambda}^{\bullet}]H_{\lambda}[\iota D_{\mathrm{core}(\mu)}^{\bullet}].

(6)

We define wreath analogues of
interpolation Macdonald polynomials
(cf.
[
Sah96
,
Kno97
]
) and give an explicit plethystic formula for them.

(7)

Given an
r
-tuple of partitions
\vec{\gamma}=(\gamma^{(0)},\gamma^{(1)},\ldots,\gamma^{(r-1)})
, one can define the
multi-Schur function

s_{\vec{\gamma}}\coloneq s_{\gamma^{(0)}}[X^{(0)}]s_{\gamma^{(1)}}[X^{(1)}]\cdots s_{\gamma^{(r-1)}}[X^{(r-1)}].

The set
\{s_{\vec{\gamma}}\}
forms a basis of
\Lambda_{q,t}^{\otimes r}
.
The
wreath
(q,t)
-Kostka coefficients

\{K_{\vec{\gamma},\lambda}\}
are then the expansion coefficients in

H_{\lambda}=\sum_{\vec{\gamma}}K_{\vec{\gamma},\lambda}s_{\vec{\gamma}}.

We give a plethystic formula for
K_{\vec{\gamma},\lambda}
in the spirit of
[
GT96
,
GHT99
]
.

(8)

Finally, we define a wreath analogue of the
global spherical function
from
[
Che97
]
(cf.
[
Sto14
]
).
In a similar vein, for each
r
-core
\alpha
, we give the formula in terms of wreath Macdonald polynomials for a series
\mathcal{F}_{\alpha}[X^{\bullet},Y^{\bullet}]
that satisfies

\displaystyle\mathcal{F}_{\alpha}[X^{\bullet},Y^{\bullet}]

\displaystyle=\mathcal{F}_{w_{0}\alpha}[Y^{\bullet},X^{\bullet}]

\displaystyle\mathcal{F}_{\alpha}[X^{\bullet},\iota D_{\lambda}^{\bullet}]

\displaystyle=H_{\lambda}\hbox{ if $\mathrm{core}(\lambda)=\alpha$}.

1.2.
Methods

Notably missing in our discussion of the wreath case thus far are the wreath analogues of Delta operators.
They do indeed play a major role in this paper, but introducing them requires some machinery.
Hinted at in the work of Varagnolo–Vasserot
[
VV99
]
and made precise in
[
Wen19
]
, the replacement for the EHA in the wreath setting is the
quantum toroidal algebra

U_{\mathfrak{q},\mathfrak{d}}(\ddot{\mathfrak{sl}}_{r})
.
Namely, let
Q
be the
A_{r-1}
root lattice.

U_{\mathfrak{q},\mathfrak{d}}(\ddot{\mathfrak{sl}}_{r})
acts on the space
\mathcal{W}\coloneq\mathbb{C}(q^{\frac{1}{2}},t^{\frac{1}{2}})\otimes\Lambda^{\otimes r}\otimes\mathbb{C}[Q]
– the so-called
vertex representation

[
Sai98
]
.
For
\alpha\in Q
, we denote by
e^{\alpha}\in\mathbb{C}[Q]
the corresponding generator.
In
2.1
, we specify a bijection

\{\hbox{$r$-cores}\}\leftrightarrow Q.

We will abuse notation and treat
\mathrm{core}(\lambda)\in Q
.
It is then natural to situate all the wreath Macdonald polynomials in
\mathcal{W}
as

H_{\lambda}\mapsto H_{\lambda}\otimes e^{\mathrm{core}(\lambda)},

where they form a basis.

In
[
Wen19
]
, the second author proved that when
r\geq 3
, a large commutative subalgebra of
U_{\mathfrak{q},\mathfrak{d}}(\ddot{\mathfrak{sl}}_{r})
acts diagonally on
H_{\lambda}\otimes e^{\mathrm{core}(\lambda)}
.
The work
[
OSW22
]
then produced explicit formulas for some of these operators; when specialized to finitely-many variables, they yield wreath analogues of the Macdonald operators.
These formulas are almost always very complicated.
We catalogue in
3.5
the operators used in this paper.
It is interesting that while in the original
r=1
case, one can write down eigenoperators for
H_{\lambda}
without knowledge of the EHA, it seems much less likely that one would organically stumble upon our eigenoperators in the
r>1
case
1
1

1

We expect the formulas for the eigenoperators in
3.5
to still hold when
r=2
. When
r=2
, the presentation for
U_{\mathfrak{q},\mathfrak{d}}(\ddot{\mathfrak{sl}}_{r})
is different. One needs to establish analogues of
[
Tsy19
]
and
[
Wen19
]
, which at this point should require more precision than ingenuity.
.
Some calculations that are trivial in the
r=1
case become far more complicated in the wreath setting (cf.
4.3.4
).

Almost all of our departures from
[
GHT99
]
are somehow related to the wreath Delta operators.
We highlight some of them below.

1.2.1.
Dual polynomials

As hinted by the fact that
\nabla_{\alpha}
is not necessarily self-adjoint with respect to
\langle-,-\rangle_{q,t}^{\prime}
, the
H_{\lambda}
are no longer orthogonal under the pairing.
There seems to be no sensible way to fix this, and thus one must introduce dual versions

H^{\dagger}_{\lambda}\coloneq H_{\lambda}[-\iota X^{\bullet};q^{-1},t^{-1}].

that satisfy, for
\lambda
and
\mu
with
\mathrm{core}(\lambda)=\mathrm{core}(\mu)
,

\langle H^{\dagger}_{\lambda},H_{\mu}\rangle_{q,t}^{\prime}=0

if and only if
\lambda\not=\mu
.
We note that in the case
r=1
,
H^{\dagger}_{\lambda}
is a scalar multiple of
H_{\lambda}

[
GH96
]
.

Correspondingly, in addition to the
\Delta
-operators,
\Delta[f]
, which act diagonally on
H_{\lambda}\otimes e^{\mathrm{core}(\lambda)}
, one has adjoint
\Delta^{\dagger}
-operators,
\Delta^{\dagger}[f]
, acting diagonally on
H^{\dagger}_{\lambda}\otimes e^{w_{0}\mathrm{core}(\lambda)}
.
This is responsible for the appearance of
w_{0}
in our reciprocity statements.
By writing
\nabla_{\alpha}
in terms of
\Delta[f]
for some
f
, we are able to compute its adjoint and show that it is
\nabla_{w_{0}\alpha}
.
In particular, this shows that
\nabla_{w_{0}\alpha}
has an exotic diagonal basis given by
\{H_{\lambda}^{\dagger}\,|\,\mathrm{core}(\lambda)=\alpha\}
.
One can try to define other versions of nabla by using the characters of boxes with color
k\not=0
, but these seem to lack this adjunction property (or something compensating for its lack).
Finally, we note that the analogue of (
1.5
) roughly takes the form

\Delta^{\dagger}[f]\mathsf{V}=\mathsf{V}f.

1.2.2.
The series
\mathbb{D}

In
[
GHT99
]
, a significant role is played by the series of operators
D
: applied to
f\in\Lambda
, we obtain

D(f)\coloneq f\left[X+(1-q)(1-t)z^{-1}\right]\Omega[-zX]=\sum_{k}z^{k}D_{k}(f),

as a series in the variable
z
. This

D
is a modified version of the vertex operator from
[
Jin94
]
.
In particular,
D_{0}
is a
\Delta
-operator.
A critical technical result in
[
GHT99
]
is its Theorem 2.1: that any
f\in\Lambda_{q,t}
can be written in terms of iterated applications of
e_{1}
(by multiplication) and
D_{1}
to
1
.

Here, an analogous role is played by our series
\mathbb{D}
(cf.
3.4.6
), which is now a series in
r
variables
z_{0},z_{1},\ldots,z_{r-1}
.
To specify an operator, one now feeds it a vector of degrees
\vec{k}=(k_{0},k_{1},\ldots,k_{r-1})\in\mathbb{Z}^{r}
to obtain an operator
\mathbb{D}_{\vec{k}}
.
Certain instances yield
\Delta
-operators, while others yield the adjoint
\Delta^{\dagger}
-operators.
In proving the shifted Tesler identity (Theorem
1.2
), we introduce shifted
\Delta
- and
\Delta^{\dagger}
-operators, and
\mathbb{D}
also yields instances of those as well.
The analogue of
[
GHT99
, Theorem 2.1]
is Theorem
4.6
.

1.2.3.
A Pieri-free proof

A significant obstacle in the development of wreath Macdonald theory is the lack of Pieri rules.
Multiple pathways to approach them seem to indicate that they can be very difficult to access
[
OSW22
,
Wen24
]
.
The proofs in
[
GHT99
]
utilize certain degree 1 Pieri rules.
We are able to sidestep this, which gives a novel approach even in the
r=1
case.
Instead, in
4.2
, we study the action of certain higher order
\Delta^{\dagger}
-operators on the span of delta functions
\{\mathbb{E}_{\lambda}\}
.
In the
r=1
case, these
\Delta^{\dagger}
-operators correspond under duality to
e_{n}
- and
g_{n}
-Pieri rules
[
Mac15
]
.
Our strategy is guided by Anton Mellit’s original ideas for rethinking the
r=1
case.

1.3.
Outline

The paper is organized as follows:

•

Section
2
introduces the basic definitions for wreath Macdonald polynomials and their structures.
We review the core-quotient decomposition, discuss matrix plethysm (cf.
[
OS23
, 2.2]
), define the wreath Macdonald polynomials, and introduce the pairing
\langle-,-\rangle_{q,t}^{\prime}
.

•

Section
3
introduces
U_{\mathfrak{q},\mathfrak{d}}(\ddot{\mathfrak{sl}}_{r})
and its corresponding shuffle algebra.
The goal is to review the framework of
[
Wen19
,
OSW22
]
for producing
\Delta
- and
\Delta^{\dagger}
-operators.
In
3.4
and
3.5
, we introduce
\mathbb{D}
and the various eigenoperators used in this paper.
Equipped with the formulas for these operators, we hope that others can work on wreath Macdonald polynomials without reference to
U_{\mathfrak{q},\mathfrak{d}}(\ddot{\mathfrak{sl}}_{r})
.

•

Section
4
proves Theorem
1.1
.
We are very much intellectually indebted to
[
GHT99
]
, but as stated in the introduction, the new setting forces significant technical departures.

•

Section
5
proves variations of Theorem
1.1
.
This includes its shifted version, Theorem
1.2
.
We also prove a “star” version involving inverse characters, i.e.
q^{-a}t^{-b}
.

•

Section
6
proves the consequences of Theorem
1.2
, as outlined in
1.1.3
.

2.
Wreath Macdonald theory

Throughout this paper, we fix
r>0
.

2.1.
Partitions

We begin by setting the notation for partitions and reviewing the core-quotient decomposition.

2.1.1.
Basic notation

A partition
\lambda=(\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2},\cdots)
is a descending sequence of nonnegative integers such that
\lambda_{k}=0
for some
k
.
Denote by
\ell(\lambda)
the greatest index such that
\lambda_{\ell(\lambda)}\not=0
.
Setting

|\lambda|=\sum_{i=1}^{\ell(\lambda)}\lambda_{i},

we say
\lambda\vdash n
if
|\lambda|=n
.
We can also record a partition
\lambda
using a sequence

(1^{m_{1}},2^{m_{2}},\ldots,)

where

m_{k}\coloneq\#\left\{i\,|\,\lambda_{i}=k\right\}.

The
transposed
partition
{}^{t}\lambda
has parts

{}^{t}\lambda_{i}\coloneq\#\left\{k\,|\,m_{k}\geq i\right\}.

For
\lambda,\mu\vdash n
,
\lambda\leq\mu
will denote dominance order: namely that

\lambda_{1}+\cdots+\lambda_{i}\leq\mu_{1}+\cdots\mu_{i}

for all
i
.

To
\lambda
, we associate its
Young diagram
, which we draw following the French convention.
For example,
\lambda=(4,2,2)
has the Young diagram

We assign Cartesian coordinates to the boxes in the Young diagram such that the box at the lower left corner has coordinate
(0,0)
.
Thus,
(i,j)\in\lambda
is in the
(i+1)
-th column and
(j+1)
-th row.
The
content
of
\square=(i,j)
is the quantity

c_{\square}\coloneq j-i

Graphically, it is the SW-NE diagonal that the box lies on.
Note that the diagram of
{}^{t}\lambda
is obtained by reflecting
\lambda
across the content 0 line.
We call the class of
c_{\square}
modulo
r
its
color
and denote it by
\bar{c}_{\square}
.

Next, we introduce some notation involving
r
.
We will be concerned with
r
-tuples of partitions:

\vec{\lambda}=(\lambda^{(0)},\ldots,\lambda^{(r-1)}).

To avoid confusion, we will index the coordinates within the tuple by superscripts.
It will be useful to view the indexing set of the tuple as
\mathbb{Z}/r\mathbb{Z}
, so that
\lambda^{(i)}=\lambda^{(j)}
if
i=j\mod r
.
We denote the size of a tuple by

|\vec{\lambda}|\coloneq\sum_{i\in\mathbb{Z}/r\mathbb{Z}}|\lambda^{(i)}|

and write
\vec{\lambda}\vdash n
if
|\vec{\lambda}|=n
.
Concerning a single partition
\lambda
, a
ribbon
of length
r
is a set of
r
contiguous boxes on the outer (NE) edge of
\lambda
whose removal leaves behind another partition. (This is often referred to as a rim-hook in other contexts.)
An
r
-core partition is a partition with no ribbons of length
r
.
An addable corner of a partition is a cell
\square
for which
\lambda\cup\square
is a partition. Similarly, a removable corner is a cell
\square
for which
A-\square
is a partition.
We denote by
A_{i}(\lambda)
and
R_{i}(\lambda)
the addable and removable boxes of color
i
.

Finally, we discuss characters of cells and partitions.
For
\square=(a,b)
, we denote by
\chi_{\square}=q^{a}t^{b}
the character of the cell.
Denote by

B_{\lambda}^{(i)}\coloneq\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\square\in\lambda\\
\bar{c}_{\square}=i\end{subarray}}\chi_{\square}

the generating function of characters of boxes with color
i
.
The sum over all boxes will be denoted

B_{\lambda}^{\bullet}=\sum_{i\in\mathbb{Z}/r\mathbb{Z}}B_{\lambda}^{(i)}.

These notations will interact with matrix plethysms in
2.2.4
below.

2.1.2.
Young–Maya correspondence

Another visual representation of a partition is its
Maya diagram
.
A Maya diagram is a function
m:\mathbb{Z}\rightarrow\{\pm 1\}
such that

m(n)=\begin{cases}-1&n\gg 0\\
1&n\ll 0\end{cases}.

We can visualize
m
as a string of black and white beads indexed by
\mathbb{Z}
: the bead at position
n
is black if
m(n)=1
and white if
m(n)=-1
.
These beads will be arranged horizontally with the index increasing towards the
left
.
Between indices
0
and
-1
, we draw a notch and call it the
central line
.
Notice that beads left of the central line (nonpositive values of
n
) will tend to be white while beads right of the central line (negative values of
n
) will tend to be black.
The
charge

c(m)
is the difference in the number of discrepancies:

c(m)=\#\left\{n\geq 0\,|\,m(n)=1\right\}-\#\left\{n<0\,|\,m(n)=-1\right\}.

The
vacuum diagram
is the Maya diagram with only white beads left of the central line and only white beads right of the central line.

To a partition
\lambda
, we obtain a Maya diagram
m(\lambda)
via its
edge sequence
—we call this the
Young–Maya correspondence
.
Namely, we tilt
\lambda
by
45
degrees counterclockwise and draw the content lines (which are now vertical).
We label by
n
the gap between content lines
n
and
n+1
.
Within the gap labeled by
n
, the outer (formerly NE) edge of
\lambda
either has slope
1
or
-1
; and
m(\lambda)(n)
takes the value of the slope of that segment.
Outside of
\lambda
, we assign the default limit assignments (or we can also use a more generous definition of outer edge).
For example, Figure
1
gives the Young–Maya correspondence for
\lambda=(4,2,2)
.

\cdots
3
2
1
0
-1
-2
-3
-4
-5
\cdots

Figure 1.
The Maya diagram for the partition
(4,2,2)
.

Proposition 2.1
.

The Young–Maya correspondence is a bijection between partitions and charge
0
Maya diagrams.

2.1.3.
Cores and quotients

The decomposition of a partition into its
r
-core and
r
-quotient is a basic ingredient in the definition of wreath Macdonald polynomials.
We describe it here in terms of the Maya diagram.
Given
\lambda
and
0\leq i\leq r-1
, we take the following
quotient subdiagrams
of
m(\lambda)
:

m_{i}(\lambda)(n)\coloneq m(\lambda)(i+nr).

We call a bead in
m(\lambda)
that goes to
m_{i}(\lambda)
an
i
-bead
.
This subdiagram may possibly have nonzero charge,
c_{i}
.
We move the notch in
m_{i}(\lambda)
to the left by
c_{i}
units (right by
|c_{i}|
units if
c_{i}<0
) and treat it as the central line, obtaining a charge zero Maya diagram.
By Proposition
2.1
, this corresponds to a partition
\lambda^{(i)}
, and we set

\mathrm{quot}(\lambda)=(\lambda^{(0)},\ldots,\lambda^{(r-1)}).

In Figure
2
, we show that for
r=3
and
\lambda=(4,2,2)
,
\mathrm{quot}(\lambda)=(\varnothing,\varnothing,(1,1))
. Here, we have drawn the central line coming from
m(\lambda)
using solid notches and the
c_{i}
-shifted central lines using dashed notches.

m(\lambda)
:
\cdots
3
2
1
0
-1
-2
-3
-4
-5
\cdots
m_{0}(\lambda):
\cdots
3
0
-3
\varnothing
-6
\cdots
m_{1}(\lambda):
\cdots
4
\varnothing
1
\cdots
m_{2}(\lambda):
\cdots
5
2
-1
-4
-7
\cdots

Figure 2.
The quotient decomposition for
\lambda=(4,2,2)
when
r=3
.

The
r
-core
\mathrm{core}(\lambda)
is the result of removing ribbons of length
r
from
\lambda
until it is no longer possible.
We can obtain it from
m(\lambda)
by changing all
m_{i}(\lambda)
into the vacuum diagrams centered at the
c_{i}
-shifted central lines and then reconstituting the total Maya diagram.
For
r=3
and
\lambda=(4,2,2)
we obtain
\mathrm{core}(\lambda)=(1,1)
:

m(\lambda)
:
\cdots
3
2
1
0
-1
-2
-3
-4
-5
\cdots
\downarrow
\cdots
2
1
0
-1
-2
-3
-4
-5
\cdots

We can obtain any
r
-core in this way, and so they are in bijection with the tuples of charges
(c_{0},\ldots,c_{r-1})
.
Note that we have

c_{0}+\cdots+c_{r-1}=0

due to
m(\lambda)
being charge zero.
Thus, we naturally view
(c_{0},\ldots,c_{r-1})
as an element of the
A_{r-1}
root lattice
Q
.

Proposition 2.2
.

We have the following:

(1)

The core-quotient decomposition gives a bijection

\{\hbox{\rm partitions}\}\leftrightarrow\{\hbox{\rm$r$-core partitions}\}\times\left\{\hbox{\rm$r$-multipartitions}\right\}

(2)

The map
\lambda\mapsto(c_{0},\ldots,c_{r-1})
restricts to a bijection between
r
-core partitions and the
A_{r-1}
root lattice
Q
.

(3)

The number of addable minus removable boxes of each color can be read from the charges:

(2.1)

\#A_{i}(\lambda)-\#R_{i}(\lambda)=\delta_{i,0}+c_{i-1}-c_{i}

(4)

Adding an equal number boxes of each color does not change the core.

2.1.4.
Maya symmetries

We can obtain new partitions by applying permutations to the quotient Maya diagrams.

Definition 2.3
.

Given a partition
\lambda
with quotient Maya diagrams
\left(m_{0}(\lambda),m_{1}(\lambda),\ldots,m_{r-1}(\lambda)\right)
, we view
\pi\in\Sigma_{r}
as a permutation of
\{0,1,\dots,r-1\}
and define
\pi\lambda
by setting

\left(m_{0}(\pi\lambda),m_{1}(\pi\lambda),\ldots,m_{r-1}(\pi\lambda)\right)\coloneq\left(m_{\pi(0)}(\lambda),m_{\pi(1)}(\lambda),\ldots,m_{\pi(r-1)}(\lambda)\right).

In particular, the longest element
w_{0}\in\Sigma_{r}
gives

\left(m_{0}(w_{0}\lambda),m_{1}(w_{0}\lambda),\ldots,m_{r-1}(w_{0}\lambda)\right)\coloneq\left(m_{r-1}(\lambda),m_{r-2}(\lambda),\ldots,m_{0}(\lambda)\right).

Put another way, we have

m_{i}(w_{0}\lambda)\coloneq m_{-i-1}(\lambda).

For the long cycle
\sigma=(0~1~\cdots~r-1)
, we have

\left(m_{0}(\sigma\lambda),m_{1}(\sigma\lambda),\ldots,m_{r-1}(\sigma\lambda)\right)\coloneq\left(m_{r-1}(\lambda),m_{0}(\lambda),m_{1}(\lambda),\ldots,m_{r-2}(\sigma\lambda)\right).

Obviously, the two actions from
w_{0}
and
\sigma
send
r
-cores to
r
-cores, and the charges
(c_{0},\ldots,c_{r-1})
undergo the same transformation with respect to their coordinates.
We leave it to the reader to see that in our running example of
r=3
and
\lambda=(4,2,2)
, we have

\displaystyle w_{0}\lambda=(6,4),

\displaystyle\sigma\lambda=(6,4,1),

and

\displaystyle\sigma^{2}\lambda=(5,3).

Thus,
w_{0}\lambda\not={}^{t}\lambda
, and it is not exactly straightforward to describe the boxes of
w_{0}\lambda
in terms of those in
\lambda
.
Nonetheless, we have the following.

Proposition 2.4
.

For any
\pi\in\Sigma_{r}
, the partitions
\lambda
and
\pi\lambda
have the same set of color
0
boxes. Furthermore, if
\{\pi(0),\pi(1),\dots,\pi(k-1)\}=\{0,1,\dots,k-1\}
, then
\lambda
and
\pi\lambda
have the same color
k
boxes.

In particular,

(1)

The partitions
\lambda
and
w_{0}\lambda
have the same set of color
0
boxes.

(2)

For any
k\in\mathbb{Z}/r\mathbb{Z}
,
\lambda
and
\sigma^{k}\lambda
have the same set of color
0
boxes.

(3)

For any
k\in\mathbb{Z}/r\mathbb{Z}
,
\lambda
and
w_{0}\sigma^{-k}\lambda=\sigma^{k}w_{0}\lambda
have the same set of color
k
boxes.

Proof.

Divide
m(\lambda)
into contiguous segments of length
r
, starting from a
0
-bead on the right and ending with an
(r-1)
-bead on the left. In terms of the Young diagram of
\lambda
, we are considering the sequence of
r
boundary segments between two content lines with values divisible by
r
. Starting from the SE, such a sequence is a list of north and west steps,
a=a_{0}a_{1}\cdots a_{r-1}
, starting on the content line
(d-1)r
for some
d
, and ending on the content line
dr
.

The action of
\pi\in\Sigma_{r}
is given by taking all such segments and sending them to
\pi a=a_{\pi(0)}\cdots a_{\pi(r-1)}
, which has the same number of north and west steps as
a
. Since this is true for every such segment, we have that
a
in
\lambda
and
\pi a
in
\pi\lambda
start and end at the same point.
But these endpoints determine the boxes of color
0
in their respective content lines, so
\lambda
and
\pi\lambda
have the same color
0
boxes.

If in particular
a_{0}\cdots a_{k-1}
has the same number of north and west steps as
a_{\pi(0)}\cdots a_{\pi(k-1)}
, then the endpoints of these two partial sequences are the same. But the end point after the step
a_{k-1}
determines the boxes of color
k
. So if
\{\pi(0),\dots,\pi(k-1)\}=\{0,\dots,k-1\}
, then
\lambda
and
\pi\lambda
have the same color
k
boxes.
∎

2.2.
Symmetric functions

Let
\Lambda
denote the ring of symmetric functions.
We recall some standard notation concerning
\Lambda

[
Mac15
]
:

•

p_{k}=\sum_{i}x_{i}^{k}
denotes the
k
th
power sum
;

•

e_{n}=\sum_{i_{1}<\dots<i_{n}}x_{i_{1}}\cdots x_{i_{n}}
denotes the
n
th
elementary symmetric function
and
e_{\lambda}\coloneq\prod e_{{}^{t}\lambda_{i}}
;

•

h_{n}\sum_{i_{1}\leq\dots\leq i_{n}}x_{i_{1}}\cdots x_{i_{n}}
denotes the
n
th
complete homogeneous symmetric function
and
h_{\lambda}\coloneq\prod h_{\lambda_{i}}
;

•

s_{\lambda}
denotes the
Schur function
associated to the partition
\lambda
.

•

The
Hall pairing

\langle-,-\rangle
on
\Lambda
has orthonomal basis
\{s_{\lambda}\}
.

Note here that, as opposed to more usual conventions,
e_{\lambda}
is not the product of the
e_{\lambda_{i}}
. There is a distinction for when the subscript is a partition
(k)
versus a number, so that

e_{(k)}=e_{1}^{k}\neq e_{k}=e_{(1^{k})}
. This will make our future triangularity results easier to write.

We denote by
\omega
the plethysm on
\Lambda
that sends

p_{k}[\omega X]\coloneq(-1)^{k+1}p_{k}[X].

It is an involution that exchanges
e_{n}
and
h_{n}
, and more crucial for us, we have

s_{\lambda}[\omega X]=s_{{}^{t}\lambda}[X].

2.2.1.
The Frobenius characteristic

The irreducible representations of the symmetric group
\Sigma_{n}
are indexed by partitions of
n
; given
\lambda\vdash n
, we denote by
V_{\lambda}
the corresponding irreducible.
Recall the following basic result about
\Lambda
(cf.
[
Mac15
]
):

Theorem 2.5
.

The map
s_{\lambda}\mapsto[V_{\lambda}]
gives an isomorphism

\displaystyle\Lambda

\displaystyle\cong\bigoplus_{n}\mathrm{Rep}(\Sigma_{n})

of rings,
where the right-hand-side is endowed with the induction product.
Moreover, this isomorphism sends the Hall pairing to the character pairing.

2.2.2.
Wreath products

Denote by

\Gamma_{n}\coloneq\Sigma_{n}\wr\mathbb{Z}/r\mathbb{Z}=\Sigma_{n}\ltimes\left(\mathbb{Z}/r\mathbb{Z}\right)^{n}

the wreath product of
\Sigma_{n}
with the cyclic group
\mathbb{Z}/r\mathbb{Z}
.
We will work with a fixed
r
throughout, so we omit it from our notation.

An analogous result to Theorem
2.5
holds for
\Gamma_{n}
, but now with the tensor product
\Lambda^{\otimes r}
.
The irreducble representations are now indexed by
r
-tuples of partitions with a total of
n
boxes.
For such an
r
-tuple
\vec{\lambda}
, we denote by
V_{\vec{\lambda}}
the corresponding irreducible representation.
On the other hand, given
\vec{\lambda}=(\lambda^{(0)},\ldots,\lambda^{(r-1)})
, we can define the
multi-Schur function

s_{\vec{\lambda}}=s_{\lambda^{(0)}}\otimes s_{\lambda^{(1)}}\otimes\cdots\otimes s_{\lambda^{(r-1)}}\in\Lambda^{\otimes r}.

We likewise define
e_{\vec{\lambda}}
and
h_{\vec{\lambda}}
.
We can endow
\Lambda^{\otimes r}
with the tensor product of the Hall pairing, which we will also denote by
\langle-,-\rangle
.
The multi-Schur basis
\{s_{\vec{\lambda}}\}
is orthonormal for this pairing.

Theorem 2.6

(
[
Mac15
, I.B]
)
.

The map
s_{\vec{\lambda}}\mapsto[V_{\vec{\lambda}}]
gives an isomorphism of rings

\displaystyle\Lambda^{\otimes r}

\displaystyle\cong\bigoplus_{n}\mathrm{Rep}(\Gamma_{n})

that intertwines the Hall pairing and the character pairing.

2.2.3.
Matrix plethysm

The usual plethystic substitution
f[E]
of a symmetric function
f\in\Lambda
at an expression
E(t_{1},t_{2},\dots)
is defined by first setting
p_{n}[E]=E(t_{1}^{n},t_{2}^{n},\dots)
and then extending algebraically, since
\{p_{n}\}
is a set of algebraically independent generators.

In the multisymmetric function case, we first index the tensorands in
\Lambda^{\otimes r}
using
\mathbb{Z}/r\mathbb{Z}
.
For any
f\in\Lambda
, and
i\in\mathbb{Z}/r\mathbb{Z}
, we denote by
f[X^{(i)}]\in\Lambda^{\otimes r}
the element with
f
in the
i
th tensorand and
1
elsewhere; note that
X^{(i)}=X^{(j)}
if
i=j\hbox{ mod }k
.
In particular, we have algebraically independent generators given by
p_{n}[X^{(i)}]
.
Plethystic substitution in
\Lambda^{\otimes r}
can be more complicated because we can take linear maps on
\{p_{n}[X^{(i)}]\}_{i\in\mathbb{Z}/r\mathbb{Z}}
.
Specifically, given an
r\times r
matrix
A=(A_{ij})
with entries in
\mathbb{C}(t_{1},t_{2},\dots)
, we index its coordinates by
\left(\mathbb{Z}/r\mathbb{Z}\right)^{2}
and define (cf.
[
OS23
, 3.2.2]
)

(2.2)

p_{n}[AX^{(j)}]\coloneq~\sum_{i\in\mathbb{Z}/r\mathbb{Z}}p_{n}[A_{ij}X^{(i)}]~=~\sum_{i\in\mathbb{Z}/r\mathbb{Z}}A_{ij}(t_{1}^{n},t_{2}^{n},\dots)p_{n}[X^{(i)}].

For an arbitrary
f\in\Lambda^{\otimes r}\otimes\mathbb{C}(q,t)
, we define its
matrix plethysm

f[AX^{\bullet}]
to be its image under the map generated by (
2.2
).

We will make heavy use of two particular examples:

\displaystyle p_{n}[\sigma X^{(i)}]

\displaystyle\coloneq p_{n}[X^{(i+1)}]\text{ and }

\displaystyle p_{n}[\iota X^{(i)}]

\displaystyle\coloneq p_{n}[X^{(-i)}].

Proposition 2.7
.

The following hold:

(1)

For any monomial
s
(in particular, for
s=q,t
), the plethysms
1-s\sigma
and
1-s\sigma^{-1}
are invertible, giving

\displaystyle p_{n}\left[\frac{X^{(i)}}{1-s\sigma}\right]

\displaystyle=\frac{p_{n}[X^{(i)}]+s^{n}p_{n}[X^{(i+1)}]+s^{2n}p_{n}[X^{(i+2)}]+\cdots+s^{(r-1)n}p_{n}[X^{(i-1)}]}{1-s^{rn}}~\text{ and }

(2.3)

\displaystyle p_{n}\left[\frac{X^{(i)}}{1-s\sigma^{-1}}\right]

\displaystyle=\frac{p_{n}[X^{(i)}]+s^{n}p_{n}[X^{(i-1)}]+s^{2n}p_{n}[X^{(i-2)}]+\cdots+s^{(r-1)n}p_{n}[X^{(i+1)}]}{1-s^{rn}}.

(2)

The transpose of
\sigma
is its inverse:
\sigma^{T}=\sigma^{-1}
.

2.2.4.
Colors and characters

Finally, recall our notation
B_{\lambda}^{(i)}
and
B_{\lambda}^{\bullet}
introduced at the end of
2.1.1
.
We will by default evaluate the variables
X^{(i)}
at
B_{\lambda}^{(i)}
, so that the notation

f[B^{\bullet}_{\lambda}]

denotes this color-respecting evaluation.
Similarly, when we evaluate a symmetric function at a (possibly infinite) sum of
q,t
-characters,
we treat the color of any
(q,t)
-monomial
q^{a}t^{b}
as having color
b-a
modulo
r
, evaluating with respect to its color.
Note that
1=q^{0}t^{0}
has color
0
.
With this set, when performing evaluations, we will include color-changing plethysms such as
f[\iota B_{\lambda}^{\bullet}]
to mean compute
f[\iota X^{\bullet}]
first then perform the color-respecting evaluation.

For any sum
S^{\bullet}
of characters, we denote by
S^{(i)}
the partial sum of characters of color
i
.
Thus, multiplication by
q
shifts the color down, whereas multiplication by
t
shifts the color up.
An important example of this is

D_{\lambda}^{\bullet}\coloneq(1-q)(1-t)B_{\lambda}^{\bullet}-1,

where, in particular, one has

(2.4)

D_{\lambda}^{(i)}=qt\sum_{\square\in R_{i}(\lambda)}\chi_{\square}-\sum_{\square\in A_{i}(\lambda)}\chi_{\square}.

In this paper, denominators of the form
(1-q^{k})^{-1}
and
(1-t^{k})^{-1}
will appear.
We will by default expand them into power series where
|q|,|t|<1
.

For a sum
S^{\bullet}
of characters split into colored summands
\{S^{(i)}\}
, we will denote by
S^{\bullet}_{*}
the sum where
q
and
t
are inverted:

S^{(i)}_{*}\coloneq S^{(i)}\bigg|_{(q,t)\mapsto(q^{-1},t^{-1})}

The key point here is that we determine the color of the characters
prior
to inversion.

Another useful substitution we will encounter looks like

\left(\frac{X^{\bullet}}{(1-q\sigma)(1-t\sigma^{-1})}\right)\Bigg|_{X^{(j)}\rightarrow S^{(j)}}.

Since

\displaystyle\left(\frac{X^{\bullet}}{(1-q\sigma)(1-t\sigma^{-1})}\right)\Bigg|_{X^{(j)}\mapsto S^{(j)}}

\displaystyle=\sum_{a,b\geq 0}q^{a}t^{b}X^{(i+a-b)}\Big|_{X^{(j)}\mapsto S^{(j)}}

\displaystyle=\sum_{a,b\geq 0}q^{a}t^{b}S^{(i+a-b)},

and
q^{a}t^{b}S^{(i+a-b)}
gives characters of color
i
, we have

(2.5)

\left(\frac{X^{\bullet}}{(1-q\sigma)(1-t\sigma^{-1})}\right)\Big|_{X^{(j)}\mapsto S^{(j)}}=\left(\frac{S^{\bullet}}{(1-q)(1-t)}\right)^{(i)}.

2.3.
Wreath Macdonald polynomials

Let
\Lambda_{q,t}^{\otimes r}\coloneq\Lambda^{\otimes r}\otimes\mathbb{C}(q,t)
.
To emphasize dependence on
(q,t)
, we may denote
f\in\Lambda_{q,t}^{\otimes r}
by
f[X^{\bullet};q,t]
.

2.3.1.
Definition of
H_{\lambda}

For two partitions
\lambda
and
\mu
, we will use
\lambda\leq_{r}\mu
to denote that
\lambda\leq\mu
and
\mathrm{core}(\lambda)=\mathrm{core}(\mu)
.
The following definition is equivalent to the original one proposed by Haiman
[
Hai03
]
.

Definition 2.8
.

For
\lambda
with
\mathrm{quot}(\lambda)\vdash n
, the
modified wreath Macdonald polynomial

H_{\lambda}=H_{\lambda}[X^{\bullet};q,t]
is characterized by the following conditions:

(1)

H_{\lambda}[(1-q\sigma^{-1})X^{\bullet}]
lies in the span of
\left\{s_{\mathrm{quot}(\mu)}\,\middle|\,\mu\geq_{r}\lambda\right\}
;

(2)

H_{\lambda}[(1-t^{-1}\sigma^{-1})X^{\bullet}]
lies in the span of
\left\{s_{\mathrm{quot}(\mu)}\,\middle|\,\mu\leq_{r}\lambda\right\}
;

(3)

when expanded in terms of
\{s_{\vec{\mu}}\}
, the coefficient of
s_{n}[X^{(0)}]
is
1
, i.e.
H_{\lambda}[1]=1
.

The existence of
\{H_{\lambda}\}
was first proved by Bezrukavnikov–Finkelberg
[
BF14
]
using geometry; an alternative proof is given in
[
Wen19
]
.
When
\lambda
is allowed to range over all partitions sharing the same core (so only the quotient varies), we obtain a basis for
\Lambda_{q,t}^{\otimes r}
.
One can view the core as giving an order on multipartitions by constituting them into a single partition and applying dominance order.

2.3.2.
Modified elementary and complete symmetric functions

We can rewrite conditions (1) and (2) of Definition
2.8
as stating that
H_{\lambda}
spans the intersection of two subspaces.
Specifically, define

\displaystyle\hat{e}_{n}^{(p)}

\displaystyle\coloneq e_{n}\left[\frac{X^{(p)}}{1-t^{-1}\sigma^{-1}}\right],

\displaystyle\hat{e}_{\vec{\lambda}}

\displaystyle\coloneq e_{\vec{\lambda}}\left[\frac{X^{\bullet}}{1-t^{-1}\sigma^{-1}}\right],

\displaystyle\hat{h}_{n}^{(p)}

\displaystyle\coloneq h_{n}\left[\frac{X^{(p)}}{1-q\sigma^{-1}}\right],\text{ and }

\displaystyle\hat{h}_{\vec{\lambda}}

\displaystyle\coloneq h_{\vec{\lambda}}\left[\frac{X^{\bullet}}{1-q\sigma^{-1}}\right].

Proposition 2.9

(
[
Wen19
, Proposition 2.12]
)
.

The intersection

\mathrm{span}\big\{\hat{h}_{\mathrm{quot}(\mu)}\,\big|\,\mu\geq_{r}\lambda\big\}\cap\mathrm{span}\big\{\hat{e}_{\mathrm{quot}(\mu)}\,\big|\,\mu\leq_{r}\lambda\big\}

is one-dimensional and spanned by
H_{\lambda}
.

2.3.3.
The Nabla operator

Fixing an
r
-core
\alpha
, we obtain a basis
\{H_{\lambda}\,|\,\mathrm{core}(\lambda)=\alpha\}
of
\Lambda^{\otimes r}_{q,t}
.
We can thus define the operator
\nabla_{\alpha}
as follows.
Let
d_{0}(\lambda)
be the number of color
0
boxes in
\lambda
.
For
\lambda
with
\mathrm{core}(\lambda)=\alpha
,

(2.6)

\displaystyle\nabla_{\alpha}H_{\lambda}

\displaystyle\coloneq(-1)^{|\mathrm{quot}(\lambda)|}\left(\prod_{\begin{subarray}{c}\square\in\lambda\backslash\alpha\\
\bar{c}_{\square}=0\end{subarray}}\chi_{\square}\right)H_{\lambda}

\displaystyle=(-1)^{|\mathrm{quot}(\lambda)|}\frac{e_{d_{0}(\lambda)}\left[B_{\lambda}^{(0)}\right]}{e_{d_{0}(\mathrm{core}(\lambda))}\left[B_{\mathrm{core}(\lambda)}^{(0)}\right]}H_{\lambda}

Remark 2.10
.

The nabla operator we use in this paper is far simpler than the one introduced in
[
OS23
, 4.5]
.
The latter is attributed to Haiman.
In particular, for
\alpha\not=\varnothing
, the eigenvalue of
\nabla_{\alpha}
is not related to the evaluation
H_{\lambda}[-1]
.

2.4.
Orthogonality

Recall that we denote the Hall pairing on
\Lambda^{\otimes r}
by
\langle-,-\rangle
.
Note that
\sigma
is
not
self-adjoint with respect to this pairing when
r>2
; it is adjoint to
\sigma^{-1}
.
It is thus advantageous to twist the pairing with
\iota
: we define
\langle-,-\rangle^{*}
by

\langle f,g\rangle^{*}\coloneq\langle f[\iota X^{\bullet}],g\rangle=\langle f,g[\iota X^{\bullet}]\rangle

The shift
\sigma
is then self-adjoint with respect to
\langle-,-\rangle^{*}
.

2.4.1.
Skewing

For any
f\in\Lambda_{q,t}^{\otimes r}
, we define the skewing operator
f^{\perp}
as the adjoint under
\langle-,-\rangle
of multiplication by
f
.
Namely,

\langle fg,h\rangle=\langle g,f^{\perp}h\rangle.

We then have

\left(p_{n}[X^{(i)}]\right)^{\perp}=n\frac{\partial}{\partial p_{n}[X^{(i)}]}\eqcolon p_{n}^{\perp}[X^{(i)}].

2.4.2.
The wreath Macdonald pairing

The following is an analogue of the modified Macdonald pairing:

Definition 2.11
.

The
modified wreath Macdonald pairing

\langle-,-\rangle_{q,t}^{\prime}
is defined by

\displaystyle\langle f,g\rangle_{q,t}^{\prime}

\displaystyle\coloneq\left\langle f,g\left[(1-q\sigma^{-1})(t\sigma-1)X^{\bullet}\right]\right\rangle^{*}

\displaystyle=\left\langle f[\iota X^{\bullet};q,t],g\left[(1-q\sigma^{-1})(t\sigma-1)X^{\bullet};q,t\right]\right\rangle

Because
\sigma
is self-adjoint with respect to
\langle-,-\rangle^{*}
, the pairing
\langle-,-\rangle_{q,t}^{\prime}
is symmetric.
A key feature (or bug) is that
\{H_{\lambda}\}
is no longer an orthogonal basis.
A dual orthogonal basis is given by

H_{\lambda}^{\dagger}\coloneq H_{\lambda}[-\iota X^{\bullet};q^{-1},t^{-1}].

Proposition 2.12
.

For
\lambda
and
\mu
with
\mathrm{core}(\lambda)=\mathrm{core}(\mu)
, we have

\langle H_{\lambda}^{\dagger},H_{\mu}\rangle_{q,t}^{\prime}\not=0

if and only if
\lambda=\mu
.

Proof.

If
\lambda\not\geq\mu
, then

\displaystyle\langle H^{\dagger}_{\lambda},H_{\mu}\rangle_{q,t}^{\prime}

\displaystyle=\left\langle H_{\lambda}[-X;q^{-1},t^{-1}],H_{\mu}\left[(1-q\sigma^{-1})(t\sigma-1)X^{\bullet}\right]\right\rangle/H_{\lambda}[-1;q^{-1},t^{-1}]

\displaystyle=\left\langle H_{\lambda}\left[(1-t\sigma^{-1})X^{\bullet};q^{-1},t^{-1}\right],H_{\mu}\left[(1-q\sigma^{-1})X^{\bullet}\right]\right\rangle/H_{\lambda}[-1;q^{-1},t^{-1}]

\displaystyle=0,

due to the multi-Schur functions that appear in the definition of
H_{\lambda}
.
The case
\lambda\not\leq\mu
is similar.
Finally,
\langle H_{\lambda}^{\dagger},H_{\lambda}\rangle_{q,t}^{\prime}\not=0
because
\langle-,-\rangle_{q,t}^{\prime}
is nondegenerate and
\{H_{\lambda}^{\dagger}\}
and
\{H_{\lambda}\}
are bases.
∎

Remark 2.13
.

Our pairing differs from the one in
[
OS23
]
by a plethystic minus.
In
loc. cit.
, the authors compute the value of the pairing
N_{\lambda}\coloneq\langle H_{\lambda}^{\dagger},H_{\lambda}\rangle_{q,t}^{\prime}
.

2.4.3.
Cauchy kernel

As usual, let
\Omega[X]
denote the following infinite sum in
\Lambda
:

\Omega[X]=\exp\left(\sum_{k>0}\frac{p_{k}[X]}{k}\right)=\sum_{n\geq 0}h_{n}[X].

The
Cauchy kernel
for the Hall pairing on
\Lambda
is then given by

\Omega[XY]=\exp\left(\sum_{k>1}\frac{p_{k}[X]p_{k}[Y]}{k}\right).

We then have the corresponding kernel for the
\iota
-twisted Hall pairing
\langle-,-\rangle^{*}
on
\Lambda^{\otimes r}
:

\displaystyle\Omega[X^{-\bullet}Y^{\bullet}]

\displaystyle\coloneq\Omega\left[X^{(0)}Y^{(0)}+X^{(-1)}Y^{(1)}+\cdots+X^{(-r+1)}Y^{(r-1)}\right]

\displaystyle=\Omega[X^{\bullet}Y^{-\bullet}].

Note that in this notation, we only take products of variables with negating colors.
When applying plethysm to this kernel, we do so on each summand above.
The Cauchy kernel for
\langle-,-\rangle_{q,t}^{\prime}
is then

\displaystyle\Omega_{q,t}[X^{-\bullet}Y^{\bullet}]

\displaystyle\coloneq\Omega\left[X^{-\bullet}\left(\frac{Y^{\bullet}}{(1-q\sigma^{-1})(t\sigma-1)}\right)\right]

Here, the denominators denote the application of the inverse of
(1-q\sigma^{-1})(1-t\sigma)
to the given family of variables.
The following is a standard result from the theory of such kernels.

Proposition 2.14
.

Set

N_{\lambda}\coloneq\langle H^{\dagger}_{\lambda},H_{\lambda}\rangle_{q,t}^{\prime}.

For a given
r
-core
\alpha
, we have

\Omega_{q,t}[X^{-\bullet}Y^{\bullet}]=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\lambda\\
\mathrm{core}(\lambda)=\alpha\end{subarray}}\frac{H_{\lambda}^{\dagger}[X^{\bullet}]H_{\lambda}[Y^{\bullet}]}{N_{\lambda}}=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\lambda\\
\mathrm{core}(\lambda)=\alpha\end{subarray}}\frac{H_{\lambda}[X^{\bullet}]H_{\lambda}^{\dagger}[Y^{\bullet}]}{N_{\lambda}}.

Moreover,
\Omega_{q,t}[X^{-\bullet}Y^{\bullet}]=\Omega_{q,t}[Y^{-\bullet}X^{\bullet}]
.

3.
Quantum toroidal and shuffle algebras

Now, we fix
r>2
.

3.1.
The quantum toroidal algebra

We will introduce two parameters
(\mathfrak{q},\mathfrak{d})
such that

(3.1)

\displaystyle q

\displaystyle=\mathfrak{q}\mathfrak{d},

\displaystyle t

\displaystyle=\mathfrak{q}\mathfrak{d}^{-1}

Let
\mathbb{F}\coloneq\mathbb{C}(\mathfrak{q}^{\frac{1}{2}},\mathfrak{d}^{\frac{1}{2}})
.

3.1.1.
The definition of
U_{\mathfrak{q},\mathfrak{d}}(\ddot{\mathfrak{sl}}_{r})

For
i,j\in\mathbb{Z}/r\mathbb{Z}
, define
a_{i,j}
and
m_{i,j}
to be

\displaystyle a_{i,i}

\displaystyle=2,

\displaystyle a_{i,i\pm 1}

\displaystyle=-1,

\displaystyle m_{i,i\pm 1}

\displaystyle=\mp 1,\hbox{ and }

\displaystyle a_{i,j}

\displaystyle=m_{i,j}=0\hbox{ otherwise.}

We then define

g_{i,j}(z)\coloneq\frac{\mathfrak{q}^{a_{i,j}}z-1}{z-\mathfrak{q}^{a_{i,j}}}.

The
quantum toroidal algebra

U_{\mathfrak{q},\mathfrak{d}}(\ddot{\mathfrak{sl}}_{r})
is a unital associative
\mathbb{F}
-algebra generated by

\{e_{i,k},f_{i,k},\psi_{i,k},\psi_{i,0}^{-1},\gamma^{\pm\frac{1}{2}},\mathfrak{q}^{\pm d_{1}},\mathfrak{q}^{\pm d_{2}}\}_{i\in\mathbb{Z}/r\mathbb{Z}}^{k\in\mathbb{Z}}.

Its relations are described in terms of currents: Let

\displaystyle e_{i}(z)

\displaystyle\coloneq\sum_{k\in\mathbb{Z}}e_{i,k}z^{-k},

\displaystyle f_{i}(z)

\displaystyle\coloneq\sum_{k\in\mathbb{Z}}f_{i,k}z^{-k},\text{ and }

\displaystyle\psi_{i}^{\pm}(z)

\displaystyle\coloneq\psi_{i,0}^{\pm 1}+\sum_{k>0}\psi_{i,\pm k}z^{\mp k}.

The relations may then be written as follows:

\displaystyle[\psi_{i}^{\pm}(z),\psi_{j}^{\pm}(w)]=0,~\,\gamma^{\pm\frac{1}{2}}\hbox{ are central},

\displaystyle\psi_{i,0}^{\pm 1}\psi_{i,0}^{\mp 1}=\gamma^{\pm\frac{1}{2}}\gamma^{\mp\frac{1}{2}}=\mathfrak{q}^{\pm d_{1}}\mathfrak{q}^{\mp d_{1}}=\mathfrak{q}^{\pm d_{2}}\mathfrak{q}^{\mp d_{2}}=1,

\displaystyle\mathfrak{q}^{d_{1}}e_{i}(z)\mathfrak{q}^{-d_{1}}=e_{i}(\mathfrak{q}z),~\,\mathfrak{q}^{d_{1}}f_{i}(z)\mathfrak{q}^{-d_{1}}=f_{i}(\mathfrak{q}z),~\,\mathfrak{q}^{d_{1}}\psi_{i}^{\pm}(z)\mathfrak{q}^{-d_{1}}=\psi_{i}^{\pm}(\mathfrak{q}z),

\displaystyle\mathfrak{q}^{d_{2}}e_{i}(z)\mathfrak{q}^{-d_{2}}=\mathfrak{q}e_{i}(z),~\,\mathfrak{q}^{d_{2}}f_{i}(z)\mathfrak{q}^{-d_{2}}=\mathfrak{q}^{-1}f_{i}(z),~\,\mathfrak{q}^{d_{2}}\psi_{i}^{\pm}(z)\mathfrak{q}^{-d_{2}}=\psi_{i}^{\pm}(z),

\displaystyle g_{i,j}(\gamma^{-1}\mathfrak{d}^{m_{i,j}}z/w)\psi_{i}^{+}(z)\psi_{j}^{-}(w)=g_{i,j}(\gamma\mathfrak{d}^{m_{i,j}}z/w)\psi_{j}^{-}(w)\psi_{i}^{+}(z),

\displaystyle e_{i}(z)e_{j}(w)=g_{i,j}(\mathfrak{d}^{m_{i,j}}z/w)e_{j}(w)e_{i}(z),

\displaystyle f_{i}(z)f_{j}(w)=g_{i,j}(\mathfrak{d}^{m_{i,j}}z/w)^{-1}f_{j}(w)f_{i}(z),

\displaystyle(\mathfrak{q}-\mathfrak{q}^{-1})[e_{i}(z),f_{j}(w)]=\delta_{i,j}\left(\delta(\gamma w/z)\psi_{i}^{+}(\gamma^{\frac{1}{2}}w)-\delta(\gamma z/w)\psi_{i}^{-}(\gamma^{\frac{1}{2}}z)\right),

\displaystyle\psi_{i}^{\pm}(z)e_{j}(w)=g_{i,j}(\gamma^{\pm\frac{1}{2}}\mathfrak{d}^{m_{i,j}}z/w)e_{j}(w)\psi_{i}^{\pm}(z),

\displaystyle\psi_{i}^{\pm}(z)f_{j}(w)=g_{i,j}(\gamma^{\mp\frac{1}{2}}\mathfrak{d}^{m_{i,j}}z/w)^{-1}f_{j}(w)\psi_{i}^{\pm}(z),

\displaystyle\mathrm{Sym}_{z_{1},z_{2}}[e_{i}(z_{1}),[e_{i}(z_{2}),e_{i\pm 1}(w)]_{\mathfrak{q}}]_{\mathfrak{q}^{-1}}=0,~\,[e_{i}(z),e_{j}(w)]=0\hbox{ for }j\not=i,i\pm 1,

\displaystyle\mathrm{Sym}_{z_{1},z_{2}}[f_{i}(z_{1}),[f_{i}(z_{2}),f_{i\pm 1}(w)]_{\mathfrak{q}}]_{\mathfrak{q}^{-1}}=0,~\,[f_{i}(z),f_{j}(w)]=0\hbox{ for }j\not=i,i\pm 1.

Here,
\delta(z)
denotes the delta function

\delta(z)=\sum_{k\in\mathbb{Z}}z^{k}

and
[a,b]_{\mathfrak{q}}=ab-\mathfrak{q}ba
is the
\mathfrak{q}
-commutator.

3.1.2.
Horizontal and vertical subalgebras

U_{\mathfrak{q},\mathfrak{d}}(\ddot{\mathfrak{sl}}_{r})
contains two subalgebras isomorphic to the quantum affine algebra
U_{\mathfrak{q}}(\dot{\mathfrak{sl}}_{r})
.
Omitting the grading element
D
of
U_{\mathfrak{q}}(\dot{\mathfrak{sl}}_{r})
, they are:

•

the subalgebra generated by the constant terms
\{e_{i,0},f_{i,0},\psi_{i,0}^{\pm 1}\}_{i\in\mathbb{Z}/r\mathbb{Z}}
, called the
horizontal subalgebra
and denoted
\dot{U}^{h}
;

•

the subalgebra generated by the currents indexed by
i=1,\ldots,r-1
, called the
vertical subalgebra
and denoted
\dot{U}^{v}
.

Let
{}^{\prime}\ddot{U}^{\prime}
denote the subalgebra obtained by omitting the elements
\{\mathfrak{q}^{d_{1}},\mathfrak{q}^{d_{2}}\}
.
The following beautiful construction of Miki gives an automorphism exchanging the “two loops” of the quantum toroidal algebra:

Theorem 3.1

(
[
Mik99
]
)
.

There exists an algebra automorphism
\varpi
of
{}^{\prime}\ddot{U}^{\prime}
such that
\varpi(\dot{U}^{h})=\dot{U}^{v}
.

Explicit formulas for the images of elements under
\varpi
are generally quite complicated.
Thanks to existing work,
\varpi
will only appear conceptually in this paper.

To end, let us note that the currents
\{\psi_{i,0}^{\mp 1}\psi_{i}^{\pm}(z)\}_{i\in\mathbb{Z}/r\mathbb{Z}}
together with
\gamma^{\frac{1}{2}}
generate a rank
r
Heisenberg subalgebra
\mathcal{H}
.
Specifically, if we define logarithmic generators
\{b_{i,n}\}
by

\psi_{i}^{\pm}(z)=\psi_{i,0}^{\pm 1}\exp\left(\pm(\mathfrak{q}-\mathfrak{q}^{-1})\sum_{n>0}b_{i,\pm n}z^{\mp n}\right),

then the
\{b_{i,n}\}
satisfy the relation

(3.2)

\left[b_{i,n},b_{j,n^{\prime}}\right]=\delta_{n,-n^{\prime}}\frac{\left(\gamma^{n}-\gamma^{-n}\right)\mathfrak{d}^{-nm_{i,j}}\left[na_{i,j}\right]_{\mathfrak{q}}}{(\mathfrak{q}-\mathfrak{q}^{-1})n},

where
[n]_{v}
denotes the quantum number

[n]_{v}=\frac{v^{n}-v^{-n}}{v-v^{-1}}.

We call
\mathcal{H}
the
vertical Heisenberg subalgebra
.
There are two suitable notions of
horizontal
Heisenberg subalgebras, namely
\varpi^{-1}(\mathcal{H})
and
\varpi(\mathcal{H})
.
They seem to be different, and both will appear in this paper.
To relate the two, consider the
\mathbb{C}(\mathfrak{q})
-linear algebra anti-involution
\eta
on
{}^{\prime}\ddot{U}^{\prime}
such that

\begin{gathered}\eta(\mathfrak{d})=\mathfrak{d}^{-1},\\
\eta(e_{i,k})=e_{i,-k},~\,\eta(f_{i,k})=f_{i,-k},~\,\eta(h_{i,k})=-h_{i,-k},\\
\eta(\psi_{i,0})=\psi_{i,0}^{-1},\,\text{ and }~\eta(\gamma^{\frac{1}{2}})=\gamma^{\frac{1}{2}}.\end{gathered}

Proposition 3.2
.

[
Mik99
]

The Miki automorphism
\varpi
satisfies
\eta\varpi^{-1}=\varpi\eta
.

3.2.
Representations

We will use two representations of
U_{\mathfrak{q},\mathfrak{d}}(\ddot{\mathfrak{sl}}_{r})
to study wreath Macdonald polynomials.

3.2.1.
Vertex representation

Recall that we denote the
A_{r-1}
root lattice by
Q
; denote by
Q^{\vee}
the coroot lattice and
(-,-):Q^{\vee}\times Q\rightarrow\mathbb{Z}
the pairing given by the Cartan matrix, so that

(h_{i},\alpha_{j})=a_{i,j},

where
\alpha_{1},\ldots,\alpha_{r}\in Q
are the simple roots and
h_{1},\ldots,h_{r}\in Q^{\vee}
are the simple coroots.
Additionally, we set

\displaystyle\alpha_{0}\coloneq-\sum_{i=1}^{r-1}\alpha_{i}

\displaystyle\text{ and }

\displaystyle h_{0}\coloneq-\sum_{i=1}^{r-1}h_{i}.

Finally, let
P
denote the weight lattice with fundamental weights
\Lambda_{1},\ldots,\Lambda_{r-1}
.
The set
\{\alpha_{2},\ldots,\alpha_{r-1},\Lambda_{r-1}\}
is a basis of
P
.

The
twisted group algebra

\mathbb{F}\{P\}
is the
\mathbb{F}
-algebra generated by
\{e^{\alpha_{j}}\}_{j=2}^{r-1}\cup\{e^{\Lambda_{r-1}}\}
satisfying the relations

\displaystyle e^{\alpha_{i}}e^{\alpha_{j}}=(-1)^{(h_{i},\alpha_{j})}e^{\alpha_{j}}e^{\alpha_{i}},

and

\displaystyle e^{\alpha_{i}}e^{\Lambda_{r-1}}=(-1)^{\delta_{i,r-1}}e^{\Lambda_{r-1}}e^{\alpha_{i}}.

For
\alpha\in P
, we write
\alpha=\sum_{j=2}^{r-1}m_{j}\alpha_{j}+m_{r}\Lambda_{r-1}
and set

e^{\alpha}=e^{m_{2}\alpha_{2}}\cdots e^{m_{r-1}\alpha_{r-1}}e^{m_{r}\Lambda_{r-1}}.

We then set
\mathbb{F}\{Q\}
to be the subalgebra generated by
e^{\alpha_{1}},\ldots,e^{\alpha_{r-1}}
, which has

\{e^{\alpha}\}_{\alpha\in Q}
as a basis.

The vertex representation is defined on the space
\mathcal{W}\coloneq\Lambda^{\otimes r}\otimes\mathbb{F}\{Q\}
.
To define it, we need to introduce the following additional operators:

\displaystyle\TT\left[\pm X^{(i)}z\right]

\displaystyle\coloneq\exp\left(\pm\sum_{k>0}p_{k}^{\perp}[X^{(i)}]\frac{z^{k}}{k}\right),

\displaystyle\partial_{\alpha_{i}}(e^{\alpha})

\displaystyle\coloneq(h_{i},\alpha)e^{\alpha},\text{ and }

\displaystyle z^{H_{i,0}}(e^{\alpha})

\displaystyle\coloneq z^{(h_{i},\alpha)}\mathfrak{d}^{\frac{1}{2}\sum_{j=1}^{r-1}(h_{i},m_{j}\alpha_{j})m_{i,j}}e^{\alpha},

where
\alpha=\sum_{j=1}^{r-1}m_{j}\alpha_{j}
.
Because of the exponential in
\TT[X^{(i)}z]
, we can consolidate products of such operators using plethystic notation:

\TT\left[X^{(i)}z\right]\TT\left[X^{(j)}w\right]\eqcolon\TT\left[X^{(i)}z+X^{(j)}w\right].

As usual with plethystic notation, a minus sign will not denote a substitution
z\mapsto-1
.

Theorem 3.3

(
[
Sai98
]
)
.

Let
\vec{\mathfrak{c}}=(\mathfrak{c}_{0},\ldots,\mathfrak{c}_{r-1})\in(\mathbb{F}^{\times})^{r}
.
The following define a
{}^{\prime}\ddot{U}^{\prime}
-action on
\mathcal{W}
:

\displaystyle\rho_{\vec{\mathfrak{c}}}(e_{i}(z))

\displaystyle=\mathfrak{c}_{i}\Omega\left[\mathfrak{q}^{-\frac{1}{2}}X^{(i)}z\right]\TT\left[-\mathfrak{q}^{-\frac{1}{2}}\left(-\mathfrak{d}^{-1}X^{(i-1)}+(\mathfrak{q}+\mathfrak{q}^{-1})X^{(i)}-\mathfrak{d}X^{(i+1)}\right)z^{-1}\right]e^{\alpha_{i}}z^{1+H_{i,0}},

\displaystyle\rho_{\vec{\mathfrak{c}}}(f_{i}(z))

\displaystyle=\frac{(-1)^{r\delta_{i,0}}}{\mathfrak{c}_{i}}\Omega\left[-\mathfrak{q}^{\frac{1}{2}}X^{(i)}z\right]\TT\left[\mathfrak{q}^{\frac{1}{2}}\left(-\mathfrak{d}^{-1}X^{(i-1)}+(\mathfrak{q}+\mathfrak{q}^{-1})X^{(i)}-\mathfrak{d}X^{(i+1)}\right)z^{-1}\right]e^{-\alpha_{i}}z^{1-H_{i,0}},

\displaystyle\rho_{\vec{\mathfrak{c}}}(\psi_{i}^{+}(z))

\displaystyle=\exp\left((\mathfrak{q}-\mathfrak{q}^{-1})\sum_{k>0}\frac{[k]_{\mathfrak{q}}}{k}\left(-\mathfrak{d}^{-k}p_{k}^{\perp}[X^{(i-1)}]+(\mathfrak{q}^{k}+\mathfrak{q}^{-k})p_{k}^{\perp}[X^{(i)}]-\mathfrak{d}^{k}p_{k}^{\perp}[X^{(i+1)}]\right)z^{-k}\right)\mathfrak{q}^{\partial_{\alpha_{i}}},

\displaystyle\rho_{\vec{\mathfrak{c}}}(\psi_{i}^{-}(z))

\displaystyle=\exp\left(-(\mathfrak{q}-\mathfrak{q}^{-1})\sum_{k>0}\frac{[k]_{\mathfrak{q}}}{k}p_{k}[X^{(i)}]z^{k}\right)\mathfrak{q}^{-\partial_{\alpha_{i}}},\text{ and }

\displaystyle\rho_{\vec{\mathfrak{c}}}(\gamma^{\frac{1}{2}})

\displaystyle=\mathfrak{q}^{\frac{1}{2}}.

Remark 3.4
.

To reconcile our formulas above with the usual way the vertex representation is presented, we note

(3.3)

\rho_{\vec{\mathfrak{c}}}(b_{i,k})=\begin{cases}\displaystyle\frac{[k]_{\mathfrak{q}}}{k}p_{|k|}[X^{(i)}]&k<0\\
\vskip-10.00002pt\\
\displaystyle\frac{[k]_{\mathfrak{q}}}{k}\left(-\mathfrak{d}^{-k}p_{k}^{\perp}[X^{(i-1)}]+(\mathfrak{q}^{k}+\mathfrak{q}^{-k})p_{k}^{\perp}[X^{(i)}]-\mathfrak{d}^{k}p_{k}^{\perp}[X^{(i+1)}]\right)&k>0\end{cases}.

Together with
\rho_{\vec{\mathfrak{c}}}(\gamma^{\frac{1}{2}})
, these indeed satisfy the Heisenberg relations (
3.2
).

3.2.2.
Fock representation

The second representation is commonly called the Fock representation.
Let
\mathcal{F}
be the
\mathbb{F}
-vector space with basis indexed by partitions
\{|\lambda\rangle\}
.
The corresponding dual basis vectors will be denoted
\{\langle\lambda|\}
.
We recall that for a given partition
\lambda
,

•

d_{i}(\lambda)
is the number of boxes in
\lambda
with color
i
;

•

A_{i}(\lambda)
and
R_{i}(\lambda)
denote the addable and removable boxes of
\lambda
with color
i
, respectively; and

•

\bar{c}_{\square}
is the color of
\square\in\lambda
.

Theorem 3.5

(
[
FJMM13
,
Wen19
]
)
.

Let
v\in\mathbb{F}^{\times}
.
The following matrix elements define an action
\tau_{v}
of
{}^{\prime}\ddot{U}^{\prime}
on
\mathcal{F}
:

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} \langle\lambda|\tau_{v}\left(e_{i}(z)\right)|\lambda+\square\rangle&=\delta_{\bar{c}_{\square},i}(-\mathfrak{d})^{d_{i+1}(\lambda)}\delta\left(\frac{z}{\chi_{\square}v}\right)\frac{\displaystyle\prod_{\blacksquare\in R_{i}(\lambda)}\left(\chi_{\square}-\mathfrak{q}^{2}\chi_{\blacksquare}\right)}{\displaystyle\prod_{\begin{subarray}{c}\blacksquare\in A_{i}(\lambda)\\
\blacksquare\not=\square\end{subarray}}\left(\chi_{\square}-\chi_{\blacksquare}\right)},\\
\langle\lambda+\square|\tau_{v}\left(f_{i}(z)\right)|\lambda\rangle&=\delta_{\bar{c}_{\square},i}(-\mathfrak{d})^{-d_{i+1}(\lambda)}\delta\left(\frac{z}{\chi_{\square}v}\right)\frac{\displaystyle\prod_{\begin{subarray}{c}\blacksquare\in A_{i}(\lambda)\\
\blacksquare\not=\square\end{subarray}}\left(\mathfrak{q}\chi_{\square}-\mathfrak{q}^{-1}\chi_{\blacksquare}\right)}{\displaystyle\prod_{\blacksquare\in R_{i}(\lambda)}\mathfrak{q}\left(\chi_{\square}-\chi_{\blacksquare}\right)},\\
\langle\lambda|\tau_{v}\left(\psi_{i}^{\pm}(z)\right)|\lambda\rangle&=\prod_{\blacksquare\in A_{i}(\lambda)}\frac{\left(\mathfrak{q}z-\mathfrak{q}^{-1}\chi_{\blacksquare}v\right)}{\left(z-\chi_{\blacksquare}v\right)}\prod_{\blacksquare\in R_{i}(\lambda)}\frac{\left(\mathfrak{q}^{-1}z-\mathfrak{q}\chi_{\blacksquare}v\right)}{\left(z-\chi_{\blacksquare}v\right)},\text{ and }\\
\langle\lambda|\tau_{v}(\gamma^{\frac{1}{2}})|\lambda\rangle&=1.\end{aligned}

It will be useful to compute the following:

Lemma 3.6
.

For
k>0
,the Heisenberg generators
\{b_{i,\pm k}\}
act on
\mathcal{F}
as

\displaystyle\langle\lambda|\tau_{v}(b_{i,k})|\lambda\rangle

\displaystyle=\frac{v^{k}[k]_{\mathfrak{q}}}{\mathfrak{q}^{k}k}\left(\sum_{\blacksquare\in A_{i}(\lambda)}\chi_{\blacksquare}^{k}-\sum_{\blacksquare\in R_{i}(\lambda)}(qt\chi_{\blacksquare})^{k}\right)=\frac{v^{k}[k]_{\mathfrak{q}}}{\mathfrak{q}^{k}k}p_{k}[-D_{\lambda}^{(i)}]\text{ and }

\displaystyle\langle\lambda|\tau_{v}(b_{i,-k})|\lambda\rangle

\displaystyle=\frac{\mathfrak{q}^{k}[k]_{\mathfrak{q}}}{v^{k}k}\left(\sum_{\blacksquare\in A_{i}(\lambda)}\chi_{\blacksquare}^{-k}-\sum_{\blacksquare\in R_{i}(\lambda)}(qt\chi_{\blacksquare})^{-k}\right)=\frac{\mathfrak{q}^{k}[k]_{\mathfrak{q}}}{v^{k}k}p_{k}\left[-(D_{\lambda}^{(i)})_{*}\right].

3.2.3.
Tsymbaliuk isomorphism

The representation
\tau_{v}
on
\mathcal{F}
is generated as a module by
|\varnothing\rangle
, whereas on the other hand,
\rho_{\vec{\mathfrak{c}}}
is generated by
1\otimes 1\in\mathcal{W}
.
Both can be considered vacuum vectors for their respective representations.
The following theorem was proved by Tsymbaliuk.

Theorem 3.7

(
[
Tsy19
,
Wen19
]
)
.

Let

v=\frac{(-1)^{\frac{(r-2)(r-3)}{2}}\mathfrak{q}}{\mathfrak{d}^{\frac{r}{2}}\mathfrak{c}_{0}\cdots\mathfrak{c}_{r-1}}.

The vacuum-to-vacuum map

(3.4)

\mathcal{F}\ni|\varnothing\rangle\mapsto 1\otimes 1\in\mathcal{W}

induces an isomorphism
\mathrm{T}:\mathcal{F}\rightarrow\mathcal{W}
between the
representation
\tau_{v}
and the
\varpi
-twisted representation
\rho_{\vec{\mathfrak{c}}}\circ\varpi
.

Recall that in Proposition
2.2
, we give a bijection between
r
-cores and
Q
.
We will abuse notation and write
\mathrm{core}(\lambda)\in Q
.
The following result provides more details on the Tsymbaliuk isomorphisms:

Theorem 3.8

(
[
Wen19
]
)
.

For a nonzero scalar
c_{\lambda}
, we have:

\mathrm{T}\left(|\lambda\rangle\right)=c_{\lambda}H_{\lambda}\otimes e^{\mathrm{core}(\lambda)}.

Therefore,
\{H_{\lambda}\otimes e^{\mathrm{core}(\lambda)}\}
is a diagonal basis for
(\rho_{\vec{\mathfrak{c}}}\circ\varpi)(\mathcal{H})
.

3.2.4.
Plethysm for
\mathcal{T}
-operators

Note that

(3.5)

\mathcal{T}\left[\pm X^{(i)}z\right]\left(f[X^{(j)}]\right)=\begin{cases}f[X^{(i)}\pm z]&i=j\\
f[X^{(j)}]&i\not=j\end{cases},

which can be checked on power sums.
We can extend the matrix plethysm notation
2.2.3
to
\mathcal{T}
-operators:

\mathcal{T}\left[AX^{(j)}\right]\coloneq\mathcal{T}\left[\sum_{i\in\mathbb{Z}/r\mathbb{Z}}A_{ij}X^{(i)}\right].

Below is a lemma that makes this plethystic notation more viable to work with.

Lemma 3.9
.

For two
r\times r
matrices
A
and
B
with entries in
\mathbb{C}(q,t)
and
f\in\Lambda_{q,t}^{\otimes r}
, we have

\mathcal{T}\left[AX^{(j)}z\right]\Omega\left[BX^{(k)}w\right]=\Omega\left[\left(A^{T}B\right)_{jk}zw\right]\Omega\left[BX^{(k)}w\right]\mathcal{T}\left[AX^{(j)}z\right]

Proof.

Writing things out, we have

\displaystyle\mathcal{T}\left[AX^{(j)}z\right]=\mathcal{T}\left[\sum_{i\in\mathbb{Z}/r\mathbb{Z}}A_{ij}X^{(i)}z\right]

and

\displaystyle\Omega\left[BX^{(k)}w\right]=\Omega\left[\sum_{i\in\mathbb{Z}/r\mathbb{Z}}B_{ik}X^{(i)}w\right].

Thus, applying (
3.5
), we have

\displaystyle\mathcal{T}\left[AX^{(j)}z\right]\Omega\left[BX^{(k)}w\right]

\displaystyle=\Omega\left[\sum_{i\in\mathbb{Z}/r\mathbb{Z}}B_{ik}\left(X^{(i)}+A_{ij}z\right)w\right]\mathcal{T}\left[AX^{(j)}z\right]

\displaystyle=\Omega\left[\sum_{i\in\mathbb{Z}/r\mathbb{Z}}B_{ik}A_{ij}zw\right]\Omega\left[BX^{(k)}w\right]\mathcal{T}\left[AX^{(j)}z\right]

\displaystyle=\Omega\left[(A^{T}B)_{jk}zw\right]\Omega\left[BX^{(k)}w\right]\mathcal{T}\left[AX^{(j)}z\right].\qed

3.2.5.
Rescaled vertex operators

Let us rescale
\mathcal{W}
by

p_{k}[X^{(i)}]\mapsto\mathfrak{q}^{-\frac{k}{2}}p_{k}[X^{(i)}].

This yields an isomorphic representation
\rho_{\vec{\mathfrak{c}}}^{+}
.
We will only be concerned with the action of the
e
-currents:

\displaystyle E_{i}(z)

\displaystyle\coloneq\rho_{\vec{\mathfrak{c}}}^{+}(e_{i}(z))

\displaystyle=\mathfrak{c}_{i}\Omega\left[X^{(i)}z\right]\TT\left[-\left(-q^{-1}X^{(i-1)}+(1+q^{-1}t^{-1})X^{(i)}-t^{-1}X^{(i+1)}\right)z^{-1}\right]e^{\alpha_{i}}z^{1+H_{i,0}}

\displaystyle=\mathfrak{c}_{i}\Omega\left[X^{(i)}z\right]\TT\left[-(1-q^{-1}\sigma^{-1})(1-t^{-1}\sigma)X^{(i)}z^{-1}\right]e^{\alpha_{i}}z^{1+H_{i,0}}.

Likewise, we define
\rho_{\vec{\mathfrak{c}}}^{-}
by rescaling

p_{k}[X^{(i)}]\mapsto\mathfrak{q}^{\frac{k}{2}}p_{k}[X^{(i)}].

We will only be interested in the action of the
f
-currents:

\displaystyle F_{i}(z)

\displaystyle\coloneq\rho_{\vec{\mathfrak{c}}}^{-}(f_{i}(z))

\displaystyle=\frac{(-1)^{r\delta_{i,0}}}{\mathfrak{c}_{i}}\Omega\left[-X^{(i)}z\right]\TT\left[\left(-tX^{(i-1)}+(1+qt)X^{(i)}-qX^{(i+1)}\right)z^{-1}\right]e^{-\alpha_{i}}z^{1-H_{i,0}}

\displaystyle=\frac{(-1)^{r\delta_{i,0}}}{\mathfrak{c}_{i}}\Omega\left[-X^{(i)}z\right]\TT\left[(1-q\sigma)(1-t\sigma^{-1})X^{(i)}z^{-1}\right]e^{-\alpha_{i}}z^{1-H_{i,0}}.

Since we have only rescaled homogeneous elements, in particular
\{H_{\lambda}\}
, the diagonalization statement of Theorem
3.8
still holds for
\rho_{\vec{\mathfrak{c}}}^{\pm}
.

With this rescaling, the components of
\rho_{\vec{\mathfrak{c}}}^{-}(f_{i}(z))
have nice adjoints under
\langle-,-\rangle^{\prime}_{q,t}
.

Lemma 3.10
.

For
f,g\in\Lambda^{\otimes r}
, we have the following adjunction relation:

\left\langle f,\Omega\left[-X^{(i)}z\right]g\right\rangle_{q,t}^{\prime}=\left\langle\TT\left[(1-q\sigma)(1-t\sigma^{-1})X^{(-i)}z\right]f,g\right\rangle_{q,t}^{\prime}.

Here, we view both sides as power series in the variable
z
.

Proof.

It suffices to show

\left\langle f,-p_{n}[X^{(i)}]g\right\rangle_{q,t}^{\prime}=\left\langle\left(-t^{n}p_{n}^{\perp}[X^{(-i-1)}]+(1+q^{n}t^{n})p_{n}^{\perp}[X^{(-i)}]-q^{n}p_{n}^{\perp}[X^{(-i+1)}]\right)f,g\right\rangle_{q,t}^{\prime}.

This in turn follows from

-p_{n}[\iota(1-q\sigma^{-1})(t\sigma-1)X^{(i)}]=-t^{n}p_{n}[X^{(-i-1)}]+(1+q^{n}t^{n})p_{n}[X^{(-i)}]-q^{n}p_{n}[X^{(-i+1)}].\qed

3.2.6.
Extending the pairing

We will extend the wreath Macdonald pairing
\langle-,-\rangle_{q,t}^{\prime}

\mathbb{F}
-linearly from
\Lambda_{q,t}^{\otimes r}
to
\mathcal{W}
as follows.
Viewing
\alpha\in Q
as
\alpha=(a_{0},\ldots,a_{r-1})\in\mathbb{Z}^{r}
, define its
reverse

w_{0}\alpha
by:

w_{0}\alpha\coloneq(a_{r-1},\ldots,a_{0})

Recall that in
2.1.4
, we have also defined reverse map
w_{0}\lambda
on partitions.
The two notions of reverse agree when we regard
\mathrm{core}(\lambda)\in Q
via its vector of charges
\mathrm{core}(\lambda)=(c_{0},\ldots,c_{r-1})
.

We now set

\langle f\otimes e^{\alpha},g\otimes e^{\beta}\rangle_{q,t}^{\prime}=\delta_{\alpha,w_{0}\beta}\langle f,g\rangle_{q,t}^{\prime}.

Thus,
\{H_{\lambda}\otimes e^{\mathrm{core}(\lambda)}\}
is a basis of
\mathcal{W}
with dual basis
\{H_{\lambda}^{\dagger}\otimes e^{\mathrm{core}(w_{0}\lambda)}\}
.
Recall that in Lemma
3.10
, the
\Omega
- and
\TT
-components of
F_{i}(z)
go to those of
F_{-i}(z^{-1})
under adjunction.
Our choice of extension does something similar for the
z^{H_{i,0}}
component:

Lemma 3.11
.

We have

\left\langle f\otimes e^{\alpha},\prod_{i=0}^{r-1}z_{i}^{H_{i,0}}(g\otimes e^{\beta})\right\rangle_{q,t}^{\prime}=\left\langle\prod_{i=0}^{r-1}z_{i}^{-H_{-i,0}}(f\otimes e^{\alpha}),g\otimes e^{\beta}\right\rangle_{q,t}^{\prime}.

Proof.

For
\beta=(b_{0},\ldots,b_{r-1})
, first observe that

z_{0}^{H_{0,0}}\cdots z_{r-1}^{H_{r-1,0}}(g\otimes e^{\beta})=z_{0}^{b_{r-1}-b_{0}}z_{1}^{b_{0}-b_{1}}\cdots z_{r-1}^{b_{r-2}-b_{r-1}}g\otimes e^{\beta}.

The only nontrivial part of this calculation is to check that the powers of
\mathfrak{d}
in the action of
H_{i,0}
cancel.
This is because we have exactly one term per color: if
\alpha=\sum_{j}m_{j}\alpha_{j}
, then

\sum_{i}\langle h_{i},m_{j}\alpha_{j}\rangle m_{i,j}=-\langle\alpha_{j-1},m_{j}\alpha_{j}\rangle+\langle\alpha_{j+1},m_{j}\alpha_{j}\rangle=0.

We likewise then have

z_{0}^{-H_{0,0}}z_{1}^{-H_{r-1,0}}\cdots z_{r-1}^{-H_{1,0}}(f\otimes e^{w_{0}\beta})=z_{0}^{b_{r-1}-b_{0}}z_{1}^{b_{0}-b_{1}}\cdots z_{r-1}^{b_{r-2}-b_{r-1}}f\otimes e^{w_{0}\beta}.\qed

Remark 3.12
.

We do not obtain that each
F_{i}(z)
is adjoint to
F_{-i}(z^{-1})
.
For this to hold, we would need to twist the pairing to invert
\mathfrak{d}
.
At best, we can make such a statement for products of
\{F_{i}(z)\}
that have an equal number of each color.
All the operators we consider in this paper are like this.

3.3.
Shuffle algebra

The shuffle algebra gives a different way to view
U_{\mathfrak{q},\mathfrak{d}}(\ddot{\mathfrak{sl}}_{r})
and its actions on
\mathcal{W}
and
\mathcal{F}
.
In particular, we will use it to work with elements of the horizontal Heisenberg subalgebras
\varpi(\mathcal{H})
and
\varpi^{-1}(\mathcal{H})
, which are otherwise inaccessible via generators and relations.

3.3.1.
Definition of
\mathcal{S}

For
\vec{n}=(n_{0},\ldots,n_{r-1})\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}^{r}
, consider the space of rational functions

\mathbb{F}\left(z_{i,a}\right)_{i\in\mathbb{Z}/r\mathbb{Z}}^{1\leq a\leq n_{i}}.

We will call the index
i
of
z_{i,a}
the
color
of the variable
x_{i,a}
.
Let
\Sigma_{\vec{n}}\coloneq\prod_{i=0}^{r-1}\Sigma_{n_{i}}
and consider its action on the above ring whereby the factor
\Sigma_{n_{i}}
only permutes the variables of color
i
.
We then set

\displaystyle\mathbb{S}_{\vec{n}}\coloneq\left[\mathbb{F}\left(z_{i,a}\right)_{i\in\mathbb{Z}/r\mathbb{Z}}^{1\leq a\leq n_{i}}\right]^{\Sigma_{\vec{n}}}

and

\displaystyle\mathbb{S}\coloneq\bigoplus_{\vec{n}\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}^{r}}\mathbb{S}_{\vec{n}}.

Thus,
\mathbb{S}_{\vec{n}}
is a space of
color-symmetric
rational functions.

We endow the direct sum
\mathbb{S}
with the
shuffle product
.
To define this product, we need the
mixing terms
: for
i,j\in\mathbb{Z}/r\mathbb{Z}
, set

\omega_{i,j}(z,w)\coloneq\begin{cases}\left(z-\mathfrak{q}^{2}w\right)^{-1}\left(z-w\right)^{-1}&i=j,\\
\left(\mathfrak{q}w-\mathfrak{d}^{-1}z\right)&i+1=j,\\
\left(z-\mathfrak{q}\mathfrak{d}^{-1}w\right)&i-1=j,\text{ and }\\
1&\hbox{otherwise.}\end{cases}

For
F\in\mathbb{S}_{\vec{n}}
and
G\in\mathbb{S}_{\vec{m}}
, we define the shuffle product
F\star G\in\mathbb{S}_{\vec{n}+\vec{m}}
to be

F\star G\coloneq\mathrm{Sym}_{\vec{n}+\vec{m}}\left(F\bigg(\{z_{i,a}\}_{a=1}^{n_{i}}\bigg)G\bigg(\{z_{j,b}\}_{b=n_{j}+1}^{n_{j}+m_{j}}\bigg)\prod_{i,j\in\mathbb{Z}/r\mathbb{Z}}\prod_{\begin{subarray}{c}1\leq a\leq n_{i}\\
n_{j}+1\leq b\leq n_{j}+m_{j}\end{subarray}}\omega_{i,j}(z_{i,a},z_{j,b})\right)

Here,
\mathrm{Sym}_{\vec{n}+\vec{m}}
denotes the symmetrization for the
\Sigma_{\vec{n}+\vec{m}}
action.

Finally, we consider the subspace
\mathcal{S}_{\vec{n}}\subset\mathbb{S}_{\vec{n}}
of functions
F
satisfying the following two conditions:

(1)

Pole conditions:

F
is of the form

(3.6)

F=\frac{f(\{z_{i,a}\})}{\displaystyle\prod_{i\in\mathbb{Z}/r\mathbb{Z}}\,\prod_{\begin{subarray}{c}1\leq a,b\leq n_{i}\\
a\not=b\end{subarray}}(z_{i,a}-\mathfrak{q}^{2}z_{i,b})}

for a color-symmetric Laurent polynomial
f
.

(2)

Wheel conditions:

F
has a well-defined finite limit when

\frac{z_{i,a_{1}}}{z_{i+\epsilon,b}}\rightarrow\mathfrak{q}\mathfrak{d}^{\epsilon}\hbox{ and }\frac{z_{i+\epsilon,b}}{z_{i,a_{2}}}\rightarrow\mathfrak{q}\mathfrak{d}^{-\epsilon}

for any choice of
i
,
a_{1}
,
a_{2}
,
b
, and
\epsilon
, where
\epsilon\in\{\pm 1\}
.
This is equivalent to specifying that the Laurent polynomial
f
in the formula (
3.6
) evaluates to zero.

Define
\mathcal{S}
to be the direct sum

\mathcal{S}\coloneq\bigoplus_{\vec{n}\in(\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0})^{r}}\mathcal{S}_{\vec{n}}.

Proposition 3.13
.

The product
\star
is associative and
\mathcal{S}
is closed under
\star
.

We call
\mathcal{S}
the
shuffle algebra of type
\hat{A}_{r-1}
.
The relation between
\mathcal{S}
and
U_{\mathfrak{q},\mathfrak{d}}(\ddot{\mathfrak{sl}}_{r})
is given by the following result of Negu

t

,

:

Theorem 3.14

(
[
Neg20
]
)
.

Let

•

\ddot{U}^{+}\subset U_{\mathfrak{q},\mathfrak{d}}(\ddot{\mathfrak{sl}}_{r})
denote the subalgebra generated by
\{e_{i,k}\}_{i\in\mathbb{Z}/r\mathbb{Z}}^{k\in\mathbb{Z}}
and

•

\ddot{U}^{-}\subset U_{\mathfrak{q},\mathfrak{d}}(\ddot{\mathfrak{sl}}_{r})
denote the subalgebra generated by
\{f_{i,k}\}_{i\in\mathbb{Z}/r\mathbb{Z}}^{k\in\mathbb{Z}}
.

\mathcal{S}
is generated by
\{z_{i,1}^{k}\}_{i\in\mathbb{Z}/\ell\mathbb{Z}}^{k\in\mathbb{Z}}
and the maps

\displaystyle\Psi_{+}\left(z_{i,1}^{k}\right)=e_{i,k}

and

\displaystyle\Psi_{-}\left(z_{i,1}^{k}\right)=f_{i,k}

define algebra isomorphisms
\Psi_{+}:\mathcal{S}\rightarrow\ddot{U}^{+}
and
\Psi_{-}:\mathcal{S}^{\mathrm{op}}\rightarrow\ddot{U}^{-}
.

Proposition 3.15

(
[
OSW22
]
)
.

For
F\in\mathcal{S}_{\vec{n}}
, we have

\Psi_{+}^{-1}\eta\Psi_{+}(F)=\Psi_{-}^{-1}\eta\Psi_{-}(F)=\left.F\left(\{z_{i,a}^{-1}\}\right)\prod_{i\in\mathbb{Z}/r\mathbb{Z}}\prod_{a=1}^{n_{i}}(-\mathfrak{d})^{n_{i+1}n_{i}}z_{i,a}^{n_{i+1}+n_{i-1}-2(n_{i}-1)}\right|_{\mathfrak{d}\mapsto\mathfrak{d}^{-1}}.

3.3.2.
Action on vertex representation

For a Laurent series
f
, denote by
\{f\}_{0}
its constant term.
Moreover, for a set of noncommuting operators
a_{1},\ldots,a_{n}
, we define the ordered products

\displaystyle\overset{\curvearrowright}{\prod_{i=1}^{n}}\coloneq a_{1}\cdots a_{n},

\displaystyle\text{ and }

\displaystyle\overset{\curvearrowleft}{\prod_{i=1}^{n}}\coloneq a_{n}\cdots a_{1}.

The following will allow us to compute the action of shuffle elements on
\mathcal{W}
.

Proposition 3.16

(
[
OSW22
]
)
.

For
F\in\mathcal{S}_{\vec{n}}
and
f\otimes e^{\alpha}\in\mathcal{W}
, we have

(\rho^{+}_{\vec{c}}\circ\Psi_{+})(F)(f\otimes e^{\alpha})=\left\{\frac{\displaystyle F\times\overset{\curvearrowright}{\prod_{i=0}^{r-1}}\,\overset{\curvearrowright}{\prod_{a=1}^{n_{i}}}E_{i}(z_{i,a})(f\otimes e^{\alpha})}{\displaystyle\left(\prod_{i\in I}\prod_{1\leq a<a^{\prime}\leq n_{i}}\omega_{i,i}(z_{i,a},z_{i,a^{\prime}})\right)\left(\prod_{0\leq i<j\leq r-1}\,\prod_{\begin{subarray}{c}1\leq a\leq n_{i}\\
1\leq b\leq n_{j}\end{subarray}}\omega_{i,j}(z_{i,a},z_{j,b})\right)}\right\}_{0},

where the rational function on the right hand side is expanded into a Laurent series assuming

\displaystyle|z_{i,a}|=1,

\displaystyle|\mathfrak{q}|>1,

\displaystyle\text{ and }

\displaystyle|\mathfrak{d}|=1.

On the other hand, we have

(\rho_{\vec{\mathfrak{c}}}^{-}\circ\Psi_{-})(F)(f\otimes e^{\alpha})=\left\{\frac{\displaystyle F\times\overset{\curvearrowleft}{\prod_{i=0}^{r-1}}\,\overset{\curvearrowleft}{\prod_{a=1}^{n_{i}}}F_{i}(z_{i,a})(f\otimes e^{\alpha})}{\displaystyle\left(\prod_{i\in\mathbb{Z}/r\mathbb{Z}}\prod_{1\leq a<a^{\prime}\leq n_{i}}\omega_{i,i}(z_{i,a},z_{i,a^{\prime}})\right)\left(\prod_{0\leq i<j\leq r-1}\,\prod_{\begin{subarray}{c}1\leq a\leq n_{i}\\
