f=\sum_{i=0}^{r-1}\tilde{p}_{1}^{(i)}A_{i}+\mathbb{D}^{*}_{-\epsilon_{i}}B_{i},

where
A_{i},B_{i}\in\Lambda_{q,t}^{\otimes r}
are of degree
n-1
.
It then suffices to show that for all
p\in\mathbb{Z}/r\mathbb{Z}
and
g\in\Lambda_{q,t}^{\otimes r}
with
\deg(g)=n-1
,

(4.45)

\displaystyle\left\langle\tilde{p}_{1}^{(p)}g\otimes e^{w_{0}\alpha},\nabla\Omega\left[\frac{X^{(0)}}{(1-q\sigma^{-1})(t\sigma-1)}\right](1\otimes e^{\alpha})\right\rangle_{q,t}^{\prime}

\displaystyle\overset{?}{=}\tilde{p}_{1}^{(p)}[\iota D_{\alpha}]g[\iota D_{\alpha}^{\bullet}],

(4.46)

\displaystyle\left\langle\mathbb{D}_{-\epsilon_{p}}^{*}\left(g\otimes e^{w_{0}\alpha}\right),\nabla\Omega\left[\frac{X^{(0)}}{(1-q\sigma^{-1})(t\sigma-1)}\right](1\otimes e^{\alpha})\right\rangle_{q,t}^{\prime}

\displaystyle\overset{?}{=}\mathbb{D}_{-\epsilon_{p}}^{*}(g\otimes e^{w_{0}\alpha})[\iota D_{\alpha}^{\bullet}].

Here, in the evaluation on the right-hand-side of (
4.46
), we send
e^{w_{0}\alpha}\mapsto 1
.

First, consider (
4.45
).
A consequence of Lemma
3.10
is that

\displaystyle\left(\tilde{p}_{1}^{(p)}\right)^{\dagger}

\displaystyle=\left(p_{1}\left[\frac{X^{(p)}}{(1-q\sigma^{-1})(1-t\sigma)}\right]\right)^{\dagger}

\displaystyle=p_{1}^{\perp}\left[-(1-q\sigma)(1-t\sigma^{-1})\iota\left(\frac{X^{(p)}}{(1-q\sigma^{-1})(1-t\sigma)}\right)\right]

\displaystyle=-p_{1}^{\perp}\left[X^{(-p)}\right].

Thus, starting with the left-hand-side of (
4.45
), taking adjoints and applying (
4.13
) and (
4.4
) yields

\displaystyle\left\langle\tilde{p}_{1}^{(p)}g\otimes e^{w_{0}\alpha},\nabla\Omega\left[\frac{X^{(0)}}{(1-q\sigma^{-1})(t\sigma-1)}\right](1\otimes e^{\alpha})\right\rangle_{q,t}^{\prime}

\displaystyle\hskip 20.00003pt=\left\langle g\otimes e^{w_{0}\alpha},-p_{1}^{\perp}[X^{(-p)}]\nabla\Omega\left[\frac{X^{(0)}}{(1-q\sigma^{-1})(t\sigma-1)}\right](1\otimes e^{\alpha})\right\rangle_{q,t}^{\prime}

\displaystyle\hskip 20.00003pt=\left\langle g\otimes e^{w_{0}\alpha},\nabla\mathbb{D}_{\epsilon_{-p}}\Omega\left[\frac{X^{(0)}}{(1-q\sigma^{-1})(t\sigma-1)}\right](1\otimes e^{\alpha})\right\rangle_{q,t}^{\prime}

\displaystyle\hskip 20.00003pt=\left\langle g\otimes e^{w_{0}\alpha},\nabla\Omega\left[\frac{X^{(0)}}{(1-q\sigma^{-1})(t\sigma-1)}\right]\left(\mathbb{D}_{\epsilon_{-p}}-\mathbb{D}_{\epsilon_{-p}-\epsilon_{0}}\right)(1\otimes e^{\alpha})\right\rangle_{q,t}^{\prime}.

Because
\mathbb{D}_{\epsilon_{-p}}
decreases degree, it acts by zero on
1\otimes e^{\alpha}
.
Corollary
3.24
then implies

\displaystyle\left\langle g\otimes e^{w_{0}\alpha},\nabla\Omega\left[\frac{X^{(0)}}{(1-q\sigma^{-1})(t\sigma-1)}\right]\left(\mathbb{D}_{\epsilon_{-p}}-\mathbb{D}_{\epsilon_{-p}-\epsilon_{0}}\right)(1\otimes e^{\alpha})\right\rangle_{q,t}^{\prime}

\displaystyle\hskip 20.00003pt=\left(\frac{D_{\alpha}^{\bullet}}{(1-q)(1-t)}\right)^{(-p)}\left\langle g\otimes e^{w_{0}\alpha},\nabla\Omega\left[\frac{X^{(0)}}{(1-q\sigma^{-1})(t\sigma-1)}\right](1\otimes e^{\alpha})\right\rangle_{q,t}^{\prime}

\displaystyle\hskip 20.00003pt=\tilde{p}_{1}^{(p)}[\iota D_{\alpha}^{\bullet}]g[\iota D_{\alpha}^{\bullet}],

where in the last step, we use the induction hypothesis.

We take a similar approach for (
4.46
).
Starting from the left-hand-side, Lemma
3.31
(1) allows us to take the adjoint of
\mathbb{D}_{-\epsilon_{p}}^{*}
, after which we apply (
4.15
):

\displaystyle\left\langle\mathbb{D}_{-\epsilon_{p}}^{*}\left(g\otimes e^{w_{0}\alpha}\right),\nabla\Omega\left[\frac{X^{(0)}}{(1-q\sigma^{-1})(t\sigma-1)}\right](1\otimes e^{\alpha})\right\rangle_{q,t}^{\prime}

\displaystyle\hskip 20.00003pt=\left\langle g\otimes e^{w_{0}\alpha},qt\mathbb{D}_{\epsilon_{-p}}^{*}\nabla\Omega\left[\frac{X^{(0)}}{(1-q\sigma^{-1})(t\sigma-1)}\right](1\otimes e^{\alpha})\right\rangle_{q,t}^{\prime}

(4.47)

\displaystyle\hskip 20.00003pt=qt\left\langle g\otimes e^{w_{0}\alpha},\nabla p_{1}^{\perp}[X^{(-p)}]\Omega\left[\frac{X^{(0)}}{(1-q\sigma^{-1})(t\sigma-1)}\right](1\otimes e^{\alpha})\right\rangle_{q,t}^{\prime}.

To commute the
p_{1}^{\perp}[X^{(-p)}]
further to the right, note that by Lemma
3.9
,

(4.48)

\TT[X^{(-p)}u]\Omega\left[\frac{X^{(0)}}{(1-q\sigma^{-1})(t\sigma-1)}\right]=\Omega\left[-C^{(-p)}u\right]\Omega\left[\frac{X^{(0)}}{(1-q\sigma^{-1})(t\sigma-1)}\right]\TT[X^{(-p)}u]

where

C^{(-p)}=(1-q^{r})^{-1}(1-t^{r})^{-1}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}0\leq i,j\leq r-1\\
j-i=-p\end{subarray}}q^{i}t^{j}=\left(\frac{1}{(1-q)(1-t)}\right)^{(-p)}

Taking the coefficient
u
in (
4.48
), we get

p_{1}^{\perp}[X^{(-p)}]\Omega\left[\frac{X^{(0)}}{(1-q\sigma^{-1})(t\sigma-1)}\right]=\Omega\left[\frac{X^{(0)}}{(1-q\sigma^{-1})(t\sigma-1)}\right]\left(p_{1}^{\perp}[X^{(-p)}]-C^{(-p)}\right)

Because
p_{1}^{\perp}[X^{(-p)}]
annihilates
1\otimes e^{\alpha}
, (
4.47
) becomes

\displaystyle-qtC^{(-p)}\left\langle g\otimes e^{w_{0}\alpha},\nabla\Omega\left[\frac{X^{(0)}}{(1-q\sigma^{-1})(t\sigma-1)}\right](1\otimes e^{\alpha})\right\rangle_{q,t}^{\prime}=-qtC^{(-p)}g[\iota D_{\alpha}^{\bullet}],

by induction.
It will be advantageous to rewrite the factor as

\displaystyle-qtC^{(p)}

\displaystyle=-qt\left(\frac{1}{(1-q)(1-t)}\right)^{(-p)}

\displaystyle=\frac{-qt}{(1-qt)}\left(\frac{1}{1-q}+\frac{t}{1-t}\right)^{(-p)}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{(1-q^{-1}t^{-1})}\left(\frac{q^{p}}{1-q^{r}}+\frac{t^{r-p}}{1-t^{r}}\right)

\displaystyle=\frac{-1}{(1-q^{-1}t^{-1})}\left(\frac{q^{p-r}}{1-q^{-r}}+\frac{t^{-p}}{1-t^{-r}}\right)

for
0\leq p\leq r-1
.
All-in-all, we have reduced (
4.46
) to showing

(4.49)

\mathbb{D}_{-\epsilon_{p}}^{*}(g\otimes e^{w_{0}\alpha})[\iota D_{\alpha}^{\bullet}]\overset{?}{=}\frac{-1}{(1-q^{-1}t^{-1})}\left(\frac{q^{p-r}}{1-q^{-r}}+\frac{t^{-p}}{1-t^{-r}}\right)g[\iota D_{\alpha}^{\bullet}].

We proceed by unpacking the left-hand-side of (
4.49
).
To make the notation easier, it suffices to consider the case where
g
can be factored according to its colors:

g[X^{\bullet}]=\prod_{i\in\mathbb{Z}/r\mathbb{Z}}g_{i}[X^{(i)}].

First, recall that in the definition of
\mathbb{D}^{*}
, all the rational functions are series expanded assuming

(4.50)

\displaystyle|z_{i}|

\displaystyle=1,

\displaystyle|q|,|t|

\displaystyle>1.

On the other hand, when we evaluate the
\Omega
-terms, we have

(4.51)

\Omega\left[D_{\alpha}^{(-i)}z_{i}\right]=\frac{\displaystyle\prod_{\square\in A_{-i}(\alpha)}(1-\chi_{\square}z_{i})}{\displaystyle\prod_{\square\in R_{-i}(\alpha)}(1-qt\chi_{\square}z_{i})}

For the above formula to hold, we need
|q^{a}t^{b}|<|z_{i}|
, which is in conflict with (
4.50
).
This creates a conflict in describing the expansion region, and thus we introduce auxiliary variables
\mathfrak{p}_{q}
and
\mathfrak{p}_{t}
in place of
q
and
t
where expansions are taken with
|q|,|t|>1
.
Let us also note that the action of
z_{i}^{H_{i,0}}
yields

\left(\prod_{i\in\mathbb{Z}/r\mathbb{Z}}z_{i}^{H_{i,0}}\right)(e^{w_{0}\alpha})=\left(\prod_{i\in\mathbb{Z}/r\mathbb{Z}}z_{i}^{c_{-i}-c_{-i-1}}\right)e^{\alpha}=\left(z_{0}\prod_{i\in\mathbb{Z}/r\mathbb{Z}}z_{i}^{\#R_{-i}(\alpha)-\#A_{-i}(\alpha)}\right)e^{\alpha},

by Proposition
2.2
(4).
As in the proof of Lemma
4.12
, these powers of
z_{i}
can be used to balance the binomials in (
4.51
).
We obtain

(4.52)

\displaystyle\mathbb{D}_{-\epsilon_{p}}^{*}(g\otimes e^{w_{0}\alpha})[\iota D_{\alpha}^{\bullet}]

\displaystyle=\left\{\left(\frac{z_{0}}{z_{p}}\right)\frac{(1-q^{-1}t^{-1})^{r-1}}{\prod_{i\in\mathbb{Z}/r\mathbb{Z}}(1-\mathfrak{p}_{t}^{-1}z_{i+1}/z_{i})(1-\mathfrak{p}_{q}^{-1}z_{i}/z_{i+1})}\right.

\displaystyle\quad\times\prod_{i\in\mathbb{Z}/r\mathbb{Z}}\frac{\displaystyle\prod_{\square\in A_{-i}(\alpha)}(z_{i}^{-1}-\chi_{\square})}{\displaystyle\prod_{\square\in R_{-i}(\alpha)}(z_{i}^{-1}-qt\chi_{\square})}

\displaystyle\quad\times\left.\left.\prod_{i\in\mathbb{Z}/r\mathbb{Z}}g_{i}\left[D_{\alpha}^{(-i)}+t^{-1}z_{i-1}^{-1}-(1+q^{-1}t^{-1})z_{i}^{-1}+q^{-1}z_{i+1}^{-1}\right]\right\}_{0}\,\right|_{(\mathfrak{p}_{q},\mathfrak{p}_{t})\mapsto(q,t)}

where all rational functions are expanded assuming

(4.53)

\displaystyle|z_{i}|

\displaystyle=1,

\displaystyle|q|,|t|

\displaystyle<1,

\displaystyle\text{ and }~|\mathfrak{p}_{q}|,|\mathfrak{p}_{t}|

\displaystyle>1.

It will also be convenient to make the variable switch
z_{i}\mapsto z_{-i}^{-1}
;
we also balance out the binomials in (
4.52
) and move things around:

(4.54)

\displaystyle\mathbb{D}_{-\epsilon_{p}}^{*}(g\otimes e^{w_{0}\alpha})[\iota D_{\alpha}^{\bullet}]

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{(1-q^{-1}t^{-1})}\left\{\frac{z_{-p}}{z_{0}}\prod_{i\in\mathbb{Z}/r\mathbb{Z}}\frac{(1-q^{-1}t^{-1})z_{i}^{2}}{(z_{i}-\mathfrak{p}_{t}^{-1}z_{i+1})(z_{i}-\mathfrak{p}_{q}^{-1}z_{i-1})}\right.

(4.55)

\displaystyle\quad\times\prod_{i\in\mathbb{Z}/r\mathbb{Z}}\frac{\displaystyle\prod_{\square\in A_{i}(\alpha)}(z_{i}-\chi_{\square})}{\displaystyle\prod_{\square\in R_{i}(\alpha)}(z_{i}-qt\chi_{\square})}

\displaystyle\quad\times\left.\left.\prod_{i\in\mathbb{Z}/r\mathbb{Z}}g_{i}\left[D_{\alpha}^{(-i)}+t^{-1}z_{-i+1}-(1+q^{-1}t^{-1})z_{-i}+q^{-1}z_{-i-1}\right]\right\}_{0}\,\right|_{(\mathfrak{p}_{q},\mathfrak{p}_{t})\mapsto(q,t)}

The functions are still expanded into series assuming (
4.53
).
Finally, like in the proof of Lemma
4.12
, we strengthen the conditions (
4.53
) to include

(4.56)

\displaystyle|q^{a}t^{b}\mathfrak{p}_{t}|,|q^{a}t^{b}\mathfrak{p}_{q}|

\displaystyle>1\hbox{ for all }(a,b)\in\mathbb{N}^{2}.

We are now ready to proceed with the computation.

The strategy
: We apply Lemma
4.1
to find the constant term of
z_{r-1}
, then proceed down in cyclic order to
z_{r-2}
, then
z_{r-3}
, and so on until we reach
z_{0}
.
At each step, poles of
z_{i}
will either give the evaluation of
z_{i}
at a box
\square_{i}
on the outer boundary of
\alpha
, or crucially, at the end, in terms of
z_{0}
.
We will show that the only surviving terms are of the latter kind, and the constant term is found by setting
z_{0}=0
.

We first note that there is a power of
z_{r-1}
in the numerator; and the only poles expanded in negative powers of
z_{r-1}
are given by the following terms:

(
t^{-1}
)

t^{-1}
-pole
: the pole
(z_{r-1}-\mathfrak{p}_{t}^{-1}z_{0})
in (
4.54
);

(
q^{-1}
)

q^{-1}
-pole
: the pole
(z_{r-1}-\mathfrak{p}_{q}^{-1}z_{r-2})
in (
4.54
);

(
\chi
)

\chi
-poles
: for some
\square\in R_{r-1}(\alpha)
, the pole
(z_{r-1}-qt\chi_{\square})
in (
4.55
).

Once these are factored out, the remainder is a series in nonnegative powers of
z_{r-1}
.
Upon evaluating at the
t^{-1}
-pole, the factor

\frac{(1-q^{-1}t^{-1})z_{0}}{z_{0}-\mathfrak{p}_{q}^{-1}z_{r-1}}\mapsto\frac{1-q^{-1}t^{-1}}{1-\mathfrak{p}_{q}^{-1}\mathfrak{q}_{t}^{-1}}

from (
4.54
) becomes
1
upon evaluation at
(\mathfrak{p}_{q},\mathfrak{p}_{t})\mapsto(q,t)
.
Likewise, upon evaluating at the
q^{-1}
pole, the factor

(4.57)

\frac{(1-q^{-1}t^{-1})z_{r-2}}{z_{r-2}-\mathfrak{p}_{t}^{-1}z_{r-1}}\mapsto\frac{1-q^{-1}t^{-1}}{1-\mathfrak{p}_{q}^{-1}\mathfrak{q}_{t}^{-1}}

from (
4.54
) becomes trivial as well.
Note that both of these cancellations remove a power of some variable in the numerator of (
4.54
).

Our computation proceeds
downward
in cyclic order.
For
z_{i}
with
i\not=0
, we have the three poles as in the case of
z_{r-1}
.
If
z_{i+1}
was evaluated at the
q^{-1}
-pole, then there are some additional considerations.
First, as in (
4.57
),
z_{i}
no longer has the
t
-pole.
Additionally, we encounter poles of the form
(\mathfrak{p}_{q}^{k}z_{i}-qt\chi_{\square})
for some
\square\in R_{i+k}(\alpha)
and
k>1
, but the stronger condition (
4.56
) means that this pole is not expanded in negative powers of
z_{i}
.
Thus, by the time we reach
z_{0}
, the variables are broken up into contiguous groups, each evaluated at:

•

a chain of
t^{-1}
-poles either
starting
at
z_{0}
or at a
\chi
-pole evaluation;

•

a chain of
q^{-1}
-poles either
ending
at
z_{0}
or at a
\chi
-pole evaluation.

At the final variable
z_{0}
, there are some slight differences, and one must consider how
z_{1}
and
z_{r-1}
were evaluated.
First, due to the
z_{0}
in the denominator of (
4.54
), one may be concerned that there is no leftover
z_{0}
in the numerator to implement Lemma
4.1
if
z_{1}
is evaluated at a
q^{-1}
-pole or
z_{r-1}
is evaluated at a
t^{-1}
-pole.
However, in the first case, the initial variable
z_{i}
in the chain of
q^{-1}
-poles will have a leftover
z_{i}
in the numerator that is evaluated to
\mathfrak{p}_{q}^{-i}z_{0}
, and in the second case, the last variable
z_{j}
in the chain of
t^{-1}
-poles will have a leftover
z_{j}
that is evaluated at
\mathfrak{p}_{t}^{-j+r}z_{0}
.

Thanks to the strengthened conditions (
4.56
), we avoid poles from (
4.55
) of the form

\displaystyle(\mathfrak{p}_{q}^{-k}z_{0}-qt\chi_{\square})

\displaystyle\text{ or }

\displaystyle(\mathfrak{p}_{t}^{-r+k}z_{0}-qt\chi_{\square})

for
k>0
and
\square\in R_{k}(\alpha)
.
Thus, the initial slate of options for
z_{0}
is the same as the other variables, where as before, we need to take into account the cancellations that result when
z_{1}
and
z_{r-1}
are evaluated at
t^{-1}
- or
q^{-1}
-poles.
There are three cases where the
t^{-1}
- and
q^{-1}
-poles are both removed, which creates novelties:

•

the variables all evaluated in one big chain of
t^{-1}
-poles starting at
z_{0}
;

•

the variables all evaluated in one big chain of
q^{-1}
-poles ending at
z_{0}
; or

•

z_{1}
is evaluated at the
q^{-1}
-pole and
z_{r-1}
is evaluated at the
t^{-1}
-pole.

The first two cases will yield our desired result– we address them at the end.
In the third case, if
z_{0}
does not have a
\chi
-pole, then as remarked in the previous paragraph, there is a power of
z_{0}^{2}
in the numerator.
After dividing by the
z_{0}
in the denominator, we obtain
z_{0}F(z_{0})
for some power series
F
and so the constant term is
0
.
Thus, this case results in either
0
or a
\chi
-pole evaluation.

Outside of the cases where we have one big chain of
t^{-1}
-poles or
q^{-1}
-poles, our variables are split into contiguous segments consisting of a (possibly empty) chain of
q^{-1}
-poles ending at a
\chi
-pole evaluation, which then initiates a (possibly empty) chain of
t^{-1}
-poles.
Because of the
\chi
-pole evaluation, every variable
z_{i}
in this segment specializes to a box of color
i
when
(\mathfrak{p}_{q},\mathfrak{p}_{t})\mapsto(q,t)
.
The boxes form a sequence of cells on the boundary of
\alpha
of shape

L

– we illustrate these segments in Figure
3
.
Our goal is to show that these terms vanish when
(\mathfrak{p}_{q},\mathfrak{p}_{t})\mapsto(q,t)
.
Vanishing occurs due to the zeros in (
4.55
) of the form
(z_{i}-\chi_{\square})
for some
\chi\in A_{i}(\alpha)
, and here, the fact that
\alpha
is a
r
-core plays a large role.

\alpha
{z_{i}}
z_{i+1}

Figure 3.
Two

L

-shaped segments that avoid the addable boxes of
\alpha
.
The black squares are the
\chi
-poles while the chains of
t^{-1}
- and
q^{-1}
-poles are indicated by the gray squares.
If a segment starting at
z_{i}
has
\chi
-pole northwest of the segment ending at
z_{i+1}
, then
\alpha
has a removable ribbon of length
nr
for some
n
, as illustrated by the dashed line.

First, it is impossible that all the variables are evaluated in one

L

shape that avoids the addable squares:
If the northwesternmost square in the

L

has color
i
, then the southeasternmost square has color
i+1
.
The
q^{-1}t^{-1}
-shifts of these two boxes are, respectively, the NW and SE endpoints of a removable ribbon of length
r
in
\alpha
, contradicting
\alpha
being an
r
-core.

If the variables are split into multiple

L

-shapes, then we can find one shape
\hbox{\rotatebox[origin={c}]{180.0}{L}}_{1}
with northwestermost box
\square_{i}
coming from evaluating some
z_{i}
and another shape
\hbox{\rotatebox[origin={c}]{180.0}{L}}_{2}
with southeasternmost
\square_{i+1}
box coming from evaluating
z_{i+1}
.
If both avoid addable squares, then we claim that
\hbox{\rotatebox[origin={c}]{180.0}{L}}_{1}
must be southeast of
\hbox{\rotatebox[origin={c}]{180.0}{L}}_{2}
.
Otherwise, as before, the
q^{-1}t^{-1}
-shifts of
\square_{i}
and
\square_{i+1}
are the start and endpoints of a removable ribbon of
\alpha
of length
kr
for some
k
, in which case either the last
r
cells or the first
r
cells give a removable
r
-ribbon, contradicting
\alpha
being an
r
-core.
We illustrate this in Figure
3
.
From this, we see that going down in cyclic order, later

L

-shapes must be to the southeast, which leads to a contradiction once we go around the cycle of
\mathbb{Z}/r\mathbb{Z}
.

Finally, we are left with the two cases of a single long
t^{-1}
-chain and a single long
q^{-1}
-chain.
We will only address in detail the former.
Here we have evaluated
z_{i}\mapsto\mathfrak{p}_{t}^{-r+i}z_{0}
for
1<i\leq r
.
This gives us

\displaystyle\frac{1}{(1-q^{-1}t^{-1})}\left\{\left(\frac{\mathfrak{p}_{t}^{-p}z_{0}}{z_{0}}\right)\frac{z_{0}}{(z_{0}-\mathfrak{p}_{t}^{-r}z_{0})}\right.\prod_{i=1}^{r}\frac{\displaystyle\prod_{\square\in A_{i}(\alpha)}(\mathfrak{p}_{t}^{-r+i}z_{0}-\chi_{\square})}{\displaystyle\prod_{\square\in R_{i}(\alpha)}(\mathfrak{p}_{t}^{-r+i}z_{0}-qt\chi_{\square})}

\displaystyle\quad\times\left.\left.\prod_{i\in\mathbb{Z}/r\mathbb{Z}}g_{i}\left[D_{\alpha}^{(-i)}+C_{(i)}z_{0}\right]\right\}_{0}\,\right|_{(\mathfrak{p}_{q},\mathfrak{p}_{t})\mapsto(q,t)}

(4.58)

\displaystyle=\frac{t^{-p}}{(1-q^{-1}t^{-1})(1-t^{-r})}\left\{\prod_{i=1}^{r}\frac{\displaystyle\prod_{\square\in A_{i}(\alpha)}(\mathfrak{p}_{t}^{-r+i}z_{0}-\chi_{\square})}{\displaystyle\prod_{\square\in R_{i}(\alpha)}(\mathfrak{p}_{t}^{-r+i}z_{0}-qt\chi_{\square})}\right.

\displaystyle\quad\times\left.\left.\prod_{i\in\mathbb{Z}/r\mathbb{Z}}g_{i}\left[D_{\alpha}^{(-i)}+C_{(i)}z_{0}\right]\right\}_{0}\,\right|_{(\mathfrak{p}_{q},\mathfrak{p}_{t})\mapsto(q,t)}

where
C_{(i)}\in\mathbb{C}[q^{\pm 1},t^{\pm 1},\mathfrak{p}_{t}^{\pm 1}]
.
If there exists
\square\in R_{0}(\alpha)
, then
qt\chi_{\square}
is the character of a box that is no more than
r
steps north of an addable box
\blacksquare
.
Otherwise, there would be a removable ribbon of length
r
in
\alpha
.
Supposing that
\blacksquare
has color
i
, we then have that if
z_{0}
is evaluated at
qt\chi_{\square}
, then
\mathfrak{p}_{t}^{-r+i}z_{0}-\chi_{\blacksquare}
will evaluate to zero when
(\mathfrak{p}_{q},\mathfrak{p}_{t})\mapsto(q,t)
.

Thus, nothing is lost if we expand the poles
(z_{0}-qt\chi_{\square})
in positive powers of
z_{0}
.
We then have a power series in
z_{0}
.
Setting
z_{0}\mapsto 0
to find the constant term, we are left with

(4.59)

\frac{t^{-p}g[\iota D_{\alpha}^{\bullet}]}{(1-q^{-1}t^{-1})(1-t^{-r})}\prod_{i\in\mathbb{Z}/r\mathbb{Z}}\frac{\displaystyle\prod_{\square\in A_{i}(\alpha)}(-\chi_{\square})}{\displaystyle\prod_{\square\in R_{i}(\alpha)}(-\chi_{\square})}.

The product of characters is
(-1)
:
every removable corner has exactly one addable corner in the same row, whereas every addable corner has exactly one removable corner in the same column. Therefore the
q
,
t
powers in the product of character weights between addable and removable corners is the same. Lastly, there is one more addable corner than removable corners.

After this cancellation, (
4.59
) becomes the
t
-summand of (
4.49
).
The
q
-summand comes from the case of a single long
q^{-1}
-chain.∎

5.
Variations

Here, we catalogue some variants of Theorem
4.18
.

5.1.
Shifted versions

The components of our operator
\mathsf{V}
in Theorem
4.18
are very much centered on color
0
: the eigenvalues of
\nabla
involve color
0
boxes, the
\Omega
-term only involves the plethystic evaluation at
X^{(0)}
, and the
\TT
-term only acts on
X^{(0)}
.
One can try to shift all these colors simultaneously and ask if an analogous formula holds.
Here, we present the correct way of doing so.
Surprisingly,
\nabla
does not change in the way outlined above.
Recall that in
2.1.4
, we defined the action of
\sigma
on partitions; we replace
\nabla_{\mathrm{core}(\lambda)}
with
\nabla_{\sigma^{-k}\mathrm{core}(\lambda)}
.

Theorem 5.1
.

Fix
k\in\mathbb{Z}/r\mathbb{Z}
and set

\mathbb{E}_{\lambda}^{(k)}\coloneq\Omega\left[\sum_{i\in\mathbb{Z}/r\mathbb{Z}}X^{(i)}\left(\frac{D_{\lambda}^{\bullet}}{(1-q)(t-1)}\right)^{(i+k)}\right].

We then have

(5.1)

\mathbb{E}_{\lambda}^{(k)}\otimes e^{\mathrm{core}(\sigma^{-k}\lambda)}=\nabla\Omega\left[\frac{X^{(-k)}}{(1-q\sigma^{-1})(t\sigma-1)}\right]\TT\left[X^{(-k)}\right]\left(H_{\lambda}[\sigma^{-k}X^{\bullet}]\otimes e^{\sigma^{-k}\mathrm{core}(\lambda)}\right).

Theorem
5.1
follows from combining Theorems
5.8
and
5.9
below.
As in Theorem
4.18
, the operator appearing on the right-hand-side of (
5.1
) satisfies commutation properties analogous to those of
\mathsf{V}
.
We will need to introduce more notation to discuss this in detail.
The series
\mathbb{E}_{\lambda}^{(k)}
also has a natural interpretation; the following is proven similarly to Proposition
4.9
and Corollary
4.10
.

Proposition 5.2
.

For any
r
-core
\alpha
, we have

\mathbb{E}_{\lambda}^{(k)}=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\mu\\
\mathrm{core}(\mu)=\alpha\end{subarray}}\frac{H_{\mu}[X^{\bullet}]H_{\mu}^{\dagger}[\sigma^{k}\iota D_{\lambda}^{\bullet}]}{N_{\mu}}=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\mu\\
\mathrm{core}(\mu)=\alpha\end{subarray}}\frac{H^{\dagger}_{\mu}[X^{\bullet}]H_{\mu}[\sigma^{k}\iota D_{\lambda}^{\bullet}]}{N_{\mu}}.

Therefore, for any
f\in\Lambda_{q,t}^{\otimes r}
,

\langle f,\mathbb{E}_{\lambda}^{(k)}\rangle_{q,t}^{\prime}=f[\sigma^{k}\iota D_{\lambda}^{\bullet}].

5.1.1.
Extending the
\sigma
-action

In our setup, we will need operators that act diagonally on
H_{\lambda}[\sigma^{-k}X^{\bullet}]
.
Obviously, we will want to twist
\Delta
and
\Delta^{\dagger}
by the plethysm
\sigma^{-k}
on
\Lambda_{q,t}^{\otimes r}
, but recall that the action of a Delta operator also depends on the tensorand
e^{\alpha}
.
For
\alpha=(c_{0},\ldots,c_{r-1})
, we define

\displaystyle\sigma\alpha=(c_{r-1},c_{0},c_{1},\ldots,c_{r-2})

\displaystyle\text{ and }\sigma(e^{\alpha})=e^{\sigma\alpha}.

Recall from
2.1.4
that we have defined an action of
\sigma
on partitions.
One can see that when we identify an
r
-core with its vector of charges, both actions of
\sigma
agree.
Note as well that we have
\sigma w_{0}\alpha=w_{0}\sigma^{-1}\alpha
.

We have the following compatibility with respect to the pairing:

Proposition 5.3
.

Fix
k\in\mathbb{Z}/r\mathbb{Z}
.
The shifted elements
\{H_{\lambda}[\sigma^{-k}X^{\bullet}]\otimes e^{\sigma^{-k}\mathrm{core}(\lambda)}\}
form a basis of
\Lambda_{q,t}^{\otimes r}\otimes\mathbb{C}[Q]
.
With respect to the extended pairing
\langle-,-\rangle_{q,t}^{\prime}
(cf.
3.2.6
), they have a dual orthogonal basis given by
\{H_{\lambda}^{\dagger}[\sigma^{k}X^{\bullet}]\otimes e^{w_{0}\sigma^{-k}\mathrm{core}(\lambda)}\}
, i.e.

\left\langle H_{\mu}^{\dagger}[\sigma^{k}X^{\bullet}]\otimes e^{w_{0}\sigma^{-k}\mathrm{core}(\mu)},H_{\lambda}[\sigma^{-k}X^{\bullet}]\otimes e^{\sigma^{-k}\mathrm{core}(\lambda)}\right\rangle_{q,t}^{\prime}=0

if and only if
\lambda\not=\mu
.

Finally, we have the following easy observation:

Proposition 5.4
.

\mathbb{E}_{\lambda}^{(k)}
is obtained from
\mathbb{E}_{\lambda}
via the
\sigma
-action:

\mathbb{E}_{\lambda}^{(k)}\otimes e^{\sigma^{-k}\mathrm{core}(\lambda)}=\mathbb{E}_{\lambda}[\sigma^{-k}X^{\bullet}]\otimes e^{\sigma^{-k}\mathrm{core}(\lambda)}.

5.1.2.
Shifted Delta operators

In light of Proposition
5.3
, for
k\in\mathbb{Z}/r\mathbb{Z}
and
f\in\Lambda_{q,t}^{\otimes r}
, we set:

\displaystyle\Delta_{(k)}[f]

\displaystyle\coloneq\sigma^{-k}\Delta[f]\sigma^{k},

\displaystyle\Delta_{(k)}^{\dagger}[f]

\displaystyle\coloneq\sigma^{k}\Delta^{\dagger}[f]\sigma^{-k}.

The following is by construction:

Proposition 5.5
.

For
k\in\mathbb{Z}/r\mathbb{Z}
and
f\in\Lambda_{q,t}^{\otimes r}
, we have:

\displaystyle\Delta_{(k)}[f]\left(H_{\lambda}[\sigma^{-k}X^{\bullet}]\otimes e^{\sigma^{-k}\mathrm{core}(\lambda)}\right)

\displaystyle=f[-D_{\lambda}^{\bullet}]H_{\lambda}[\sigma^{-k}X^{\bullet}]\otimes e^{\sigma^{-k}\mathrm{core}(\lambda)},

\displaystyle\Delta_{(k)}^{\dagger}[f]\left(H_{\lambda}^{\dagger}[\sigma^{k}X^{\bullet}]\otimes e^{w_{0}\sigma^{-k}\mathrm{core}(\lambda)}\right)

\displaystyle=f[-D_{\lambda}^{\bullet}]H_{\lambda}[\sigma^{k}X^{\bullet}]^{\dagger}\otimes e^{w_{0}\sigma^{-k}\mathrm{core}(\lambda)}.

Lemma 5.6
.

The operator obtained from
F_{0,n}
remains unchanged:

\Delta\left[e_{F,n}^{(0)}\right]=\Delta_{(k)}\left[e_{F,n}^{(0)}\right].

In particular,
\sigma^{-k}\nabla_{\mathrm{core}(\lambda)}\sigma^{k}=\nabla_{\sigma^{-k}\mathrm{core}(\lambda)}

Proof.

The components of (
3.14
) interact with
\sigma
as follows:

\displaystyle\sigma^{-1}\Omega[X^{(i)}z]\sigma

\displaystyle=\Omega[X^{(i-1)}],

\displaystyle\sigma^{-1}\TT[X^{(i)}z]\sigma

\displaystyle=\TT[X^{(i-1)}],

\displaystyle\sigma^{-1}z^{H_{i,0}}\sigma

\displaystyle=z^{H_{i-1,0}}.

Applying this and then making the change of variables
z_{i,a}\mapsto z_{i-k,a}
leaves (
3.14
) unchanged.
∎

5.1.3.
The map
\mathsf{V}^{(k)}

Let us set
\mathsf{V}^{(k)}\coloneq\sigma^{-k}\mathsf{V}\sigma^{k}
.
By Proposition
5.4
,
\mathrm{span}\{\mathbb{E}_{\lambda}^{(k)}\otimes e^{\sigma^{-k}\mathrm{core}(\lambda)}\}
is closed under the action of
\Delta^{\dagger}_{(k)}[f]
for any
f\in\Lambda_{q,t}^{\otimes r}
.

Proposition 5.7
.

The map
\mathsf{V}^{(k)}:\Lambda_{q,t}^{\otimes r}\rightarrow\mathrm{span}\{\mathbb{E}_{\lambda}^{(k)}\otimes e^{\sigma^{-k}\mathrm{core}(\lambda)}\}
satisfies the following:

•

\mathsf{V}^{(k)}(fg\otimes e^{\alpha})=\Delta_{(-k)}^{\dagger}\left[f[-\iota\sigma^{k}X^{\bullet}]\right]\left(\mathsf{V}^{(k)}(g\otimes e^{\alpha})\right)
,

•

\mathsf{V}^{(k)}(1\otimes e^{\sigma^{-k}\alpha})=\mathbb{E}_{\alpha}^{(k)}\otimes e^{\sigma^{-k}\alpha}
.

Theorem 5.8
.

For
k\in\mathbb{Z}/r\mathbb{Z}
, we have

\mathsf{V}^{(k)}\left(H_{\lambda}[\sigma^{-k}X^{\bullet}]\otimes e^{\sigma^{-k}\mathrm{core}(\lambda)}\right)=H_{\lambda}[\iota D_{w_{0}\mathrm{core}(\lambda)}^{\bullet}]\mathbb{E}_{\lambda}^{(k)}\otimes e^{\sigma^{-k}\mathrm{core}(\lambda)}.

Note that this simply follows from the fact that
V^{(k)}
is attained by conjugating
V
by
\sigma^{k}
, so then

\sigma^{k}V\sigma^{-k}\left(H_{\lambda}[\sigma^{-k}X^{\bullet}]\otimes e^{\sigma^{-k}\mathrm{core}(\lambda)}\right)=\sigma^{k}V\left(H_{\lambda}[\sigma X^{\bullet}]\otimes e^{\mathrm{core}(\lambda)}\right).

Theorem 5.9
.

The map
\mathsf{V}^{(k)}
is given by

\mathsf{V}^{(k)}=\nabla\Omega\left[\frac{X^{(-k)}}{(1-q\sigma^{-1})(t\sigma-1)}\right]\TT\left[X^{(-k)}\right]\nabla.

Note that combining Corollary
4.19
and Lemma
5.6
, we have

\nabla\left(\frac{H_{\lambda}[\sigma^{-k}X^{\bullet}]}{H_{\lambda}\left[\iota D_{w_{0}\mathrm{core}(\lambda)}^{\bullet}\right]}\otimes e^{\sigma^{-k}\mathrm{core}(\lambda)}\right)=H_{\lambda}[\sigma^{-k}X^{\bullet}]\otimes e^{\sigma^{-k}\mathrm{core}(\lambda)}.

Remark 5.10
.

We note here that, if one tries to deduce a reciprocity formula as we did in
4.2.2
, one will simply rederive Corollary
4.16
.

5.2.
Star versions

We present a version of our results involving inverted characters.

Theorem 5.11
.

For
k\in\mathbb{Z}/r\mathbb{Z}
, let

{}^{*}\mathbb{E}_{\lambda}^{(k)}\coloneq\Omega\left[\sum_{i\in\mathbb{Z}/r\mathbb{Z}}X^{(-i)}\left(\frac{-D_{\lambda}^{\bullet}}{(1-q)(t-1)}\right)^{(i+k)}_{*}\right]

We then have

\displaystyle{}^{*}\mathbb{E}_{\lambda}^{(k)}

\displaystyle\otimes e^{w_{0}\sigma^{-k}\mathrm{core}(\lambda)}

(5.2)

\displaystyle=\nabla^{-1}\Omega\left[\frac{-X^{(k)}}{(1-q^{-1}\sigma)(t^{-1}\sigma^{-1}-1)}\right]\TT[-X^{(k)}]\nabla^{-1}\left(\frac{H_{\lambda}^{\dagger}[\sigma^{k}X^{\bullet}]}{H_{\lambda}^{\dagger}\left[-(D_{w_{0}\mathrm{core}(\lambda)}^{\bullet})_{*}\right]}\otimes e^{w_{0}\sigma^{-k}\mathrm{core}(\lambda)}\right).

Proof.

We apply
\downarrow
to Theorem
5.1
.
By Proposition
4.2
, we get:

\displaystyle{}^{*}\mathbb{E}_{\lambda}^{(k)}\otimes e^{w_{0}\sigma^{-k}\mathrm{core}(\lambda)}

\displaystyle=\downarrow\mathbb{E}_{\lambda}^{(k)}\otimes e^{\sigma^{-k}\mathrm{core}(\lambda)}

\displaystyle=\downarrow\nabla\Omega\left[\frac{X^{(-k)}}{(1-q\sigma^{-1})(t\sigma-1)}\right]\TT\left[X^{(-k)}\right]\nabla\left(\frac{H_{\lambda}[\sigma^{-k}X^{\bullet}]}{H_{\lambda}[\iota D_{w_{0}\mathrm{core}(\lambda)}]}\otimes e^{\sigma^{-k}\mathrm{core}(\lambda)}\right)

\displaystyle=\nabla^{-1}\Omega\left[\frac{-X^{(k)}}{(1-q^{-1}\sigma)(t^{-1}\sigma^{-1}-1)}\right]\TT[-X^{(k)}]\nabla^{-1}\left(\frac{H_{\lambda}^{\dagger}[\sigma^{k}X^{\bullet}]}{H_{\lambda}\left[\iota(D_{w_{0}\mathrm{core}(\lambda)}^{\bullet})_{*};q^{-1},t^{-1}\right]}\otimes e^{w_{0}\sigma^{-k}\mathrm{core}(\lambda)}\right).

The result follows from the fact that

H_{\lambda}\left[\iota(D_{w_{0}\mathrm{core}(\lambda)}^{\bullet})_{*};q^{-1},t^{-1}\right]=H_{\lambda}^{\dagger}\left[-(D_{w_{0}\mathrm{core}(\lambda)}^{\bullet})_{*}\right].\qed

As before,
{}^{*}\mathbb{E}_{\lambda}^{(k)}
has a natural interpretation:

Proposition 5.12
.

For any core
\alpha
,

{}^{*}\mathbb{E}_{\lambda}^{(k)}=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\mu\\
\mathrm{core}(\mu)=\alpha\end{subarray}}\frac{H_{\mu}[X^{\bullet}]H_{\mu}^{\dagger}\left[-\sigma^{k}qt(D_{\lambda}^{\bullet})_{*}\right]}{N_{\mu}}=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\mu\\
\mathrm{core}(\mu)=\alpha\end{subarray}}\frac{H_{\mu}^{\dagger}[X^{\bullet}]H_{\mu}\left[-\sigma^{k}qt(D_{\lambda}^{\bullet})_{*}\right]}{N_{\mu}}.

Therefore, for any
f\in\Lambda_{q,t}^{\otimes r}
,

\langle f,{}^{*}\mathbb{E}_{\lambda}^{(k)}\rangle_{q,t}^{\prime}=f\left[-\sigma^{k}qt(D_{\lambda}^{\bullet})_{*}\right].

Proof.

We obtain
\mathbb{E}_{\lambda}^{*}
from
\Omega_{q,t}\left[X^{-\bullet}Y^{\bullet}\right]
by (1) applying
\sigma^{k}
to
Y^{(i)}
and then (2) evaluating
Y^{(i)}\mapsto-qt(D_{\lambda}^{(i)})_{*}
:

\displaystyle\Omega_{q,t}\left[X^{-\bullet}Y^{\bullet}\right]

\displaystyle\overset{(1)}{\mapsto}\Omega\left[\sum_{i\in\mathbb{Z}/r\mathbb{Z}}X^{(-i)}\left(\frac{Y^{(i+k)}}{(1-q\sigma^{-1})(t\sigma-1)}\right)\right]

\displaystyle=\Omega\left[\sum_{i\in\mathbb{Z}/r\mathbb{Z}}X^{(-i)}\left(\frac{q^{-1}t^{-1}Y^{(i+k)}}{(1-q^{-1}\sigma)(t^{-1}\sigma^{-1}-1)}\right)\right]

\displaystyle\overset{(2)}{\mapsto}\Omega\left[\sum_{i\in\mathbb{Z}/r\mathbb{Z}}X^{(-i)}\left(\frac{-D_{\lambda}^{\bullet}}{(1-q)(t-1)}\right)^{(i+k)}_{*}\right].\qed

5.2.1.
Delta-star operators

Next, we consider how the operator in (
5.2
) commutes with multiplication operators.
To that end, for any
f\in\Lambda_{q,t}^{\otimes r}
, we define

\displaystyle{}^{*}\Delta_{(k)}[f]\coloneq\downarrow\Delta_{(k)}^{\dagger}\left[\downarrow f[\iota X^{\bullet}]\right]\downarrow

\displaystyle\text{ and }

\displaystyle{}^{*}\Delta_{(k)}^{\dagger}[f]\coloneq\downarrow\Delta_{(k)}\left[\downarrow f[\iota X^{\bullet}]\right]\downarrow.

Combining Propositions
4.2
and
5.5
yields:

Proposition 5.13
.

For any
f\in\Lambda_{q,t}^{\otimes r}
,

\displaystyle{}^{*}\Delta_{(k)}[f]\left(H_{\lambda}[\sigma^{-k}X^{\bullet}]\otimes e^{\sigma^{-k}\mathrm{core}(\lambda)}\right)

\displaystyle=f\left[(D_{\lambda}^{\bullet})_{*}\right]H_{\lambda}[\sigma^{-k}X^{\bullet}]\otimes e^{\sigma^{-k}\mathrm{core}(\lambda)}

\displaystyle{}^{*}\Delta_{(k)}^{\dagger}[f]\left(H_{\lambda}^{\dagger}[\sigma^{k}X^{\bullet}]\otimes e^{w_{0}\sigma^{-k}\mathrm{core}(\lambda)}\right)

\displaystyle=f\left[(D_{\lambda}^{\bullet})_{*}\right]H_{\lambda}^{\dagger}[\sigma^{k}X^{\bullet}]\otimes e^{w_{0}\sigma^{-k}\mathrm{core}(\lambda)}.

5.2.2.
The map
\mathsf{V}_{*}^{(k)}

Setting
\mathsf{V}_{*}^{(k)}=\downarrow\mathsf{V}^{(k)}\downarrow
, we have

\mathsf{V}_{*}^{(k)}=\nabla^{-1}\Omega\left[\frac{-X^{(k)}}{(1-q^{-1}\sigma)(t^{-1}\sigma^{-1}-1)}\right]\TT[-X^{(k)}]\nabla^{-1}.

Proposition 5.14
.

\mathsf{V}_{*}^{(k)}
satisfies the following:

•

\mathsf{V}_{*}^{(k)}(fg\otimes e^{\alpha})={}^{*}\Delta_{(-k)}\left[f[-\sigma^{-k}X^{\bullet}]\right]\left(\mathsf{V}_{*}^{(k)}(g\otimes e^{\alpha})\right)
,

•

\mathsf{V}_{*}^{(k)}(1\otimes e^{w_{0}\sigma^{-k}\alpha})={}^{*}\mathbb{E}_{\alpha}^{(k)}\otimes e^{w_{0}\sigma^{-k}\alpha}
.

Proof.

Only the first condition is a bit tricky.
We have

\displaystyle\mathsf{V}_{*}^{(k)}f

\displaystyle=\downarrow\mathsf{V}^{(k)}(\downarrow f)\downarrow

\displaystyle=\downarrow\Delta_{(-k)}^{\dagger}\left[(\downarrow f)[-\iota\sigma^{k}X^{\bullet}]\right]\mathsf{V}^{(k)}\downarrow

\displaystyle=\downarrow\Delta_{(-k)}^{\dagger}\left[(\downarrow f)[-\iota\sigma^{k}X^{\bullet}]\right]\downarrow\mathsf{V}^{(k)}_{*}.

Now, note that

(\downarrow f)[-\iota\sigma^{k}X^{\bullet}]=f[\iota\sigma^{k}\iota X^{\bullet};q^{-1},t^{-1}]=f[\sigma^{-k}X^{\bullet};q^{-1},t^{-1}]=\downarrow\left(f[-\iota\sigma^{-k}X^{\bullet}]\right).\qed

6.
Consequences

The dependence of
\mathsf{V}^{(k)}
on the core tensorand
e^{\alpha}
only plays a role when we apply
\nabla
.
In what follows, we will work with
\Lambda_{q,t}^{\otimes r}
, instead of
\Lambda_{q,t}^{\otimes r}\otimes\mathbb{C}[Q]
, and encode the core-dependence into
\nabla_{\alpha}
.

6.1.
Reciprocal identities

We have already seen one instance of reciprocity (Corollary
4.16
).
Combining Theorem
5.1
with the pairing
\langle-,-\rangle_{q,t}^{\prime}
, we obtain other versions.

6.1.1.
A Macdonald–Koornwinder duality

We will use a variable
u
to record degrees.
We note that with respect to
\langle-,-\rangle_{q,t}^{\prime}
, if we insert a grading variable
u
, we have

\langle f[uX^{\bullet}],\TT[AX^{\bullet}]g[X^{\bullet}]\rangle_{q,t}^{\prime}=\langle f[X^{\bullet}],\TT[AX^{\bullet}u^{-1}]g[uX^{\bullet}]\rangle_{q,t}^{\prime}

We have the following strong form of reciprocity:

Theorem 6.1
.

For
k\in\mathbb{Z}/r\mathbb{Z}
and partitions
\lambda,\mu
with
\mathrm{core}(\mu)=w_{0}\sigma^{-k}\mathrm{core}(\lambda)
, we have

(6.1)

\displaystyle\frac{H_{\lambda}\left[1+u\sigma^{k}\iota D_{\mu}^{\bullet}\right]}{\displaystyle\prod_{\begin{subarray}{c}\square\in\lambda\backslash\mathrm{core}(\lambda)\\
\bar{c}_{\square}=k\end{subarray}}\left(1-u\chi_{\square}\right)}

\displaystyle=\frac{H_{\mu}\left[1+u\sigma^{k}\iota D_{\lambda}^{\bullet}\right]}{\displaystyle\prod_{\begin{subarray}{c}\square\in\mu\backslash\mathrm{core}(\mu)\\
\bar{c}_{\square}=k\end{subarray}}\left(1-u\chi_{\square}\right)}.

Proof.

By Proposition
5.2
,

(6.2)

\displaystyle\Omega\left[-u\left(\frac{D_{\lambda}^{\bullet}}{(1-q)(1-t)}\right)^{(k)}\right]

\displaystyle H_{\mu}\left[1+u\sigma^{k}\iota D_{\lambda}^{\bullet}\right]

(6.3)

\displaystyle=\left\langle\Omega\left[\frac{uX^{(0)}}{(1-q\sigma^{-1})(t\sigma-1)}\right]\TT\left[X^{(0)}u^{-1}\right]H_{\mu}[uX^{\bullet}],\mathbb{E}_{\lambda}^{(k)}\right\rangle_{q,t}^{\prime}.

By Theorem
5.1
, we can write

\mathbb{E}_{\lambda}^{(k)}=\nabla_{\sigma^{-k}\mathrm{core}(\lambda)}\Omega\left[\frac{X^{(-k)}}{(1-q\sigma^{-1})(t\sigma-1)}\right]\TT\left[X^{(-k)}\right]H_{\lambda}[\sigma^{-k}X^{\bullet}].

Putting this into (
6.3
), we use Corollary
3.32
to move
\nabla_{\sigma^{-k}\mathrm{core}(\lambda)}
to the other side, where it becomes
\nabla_{w_{0}\sigma^{-k}\mathrm{core}(\lambda)}
.
Moving the variable
u
to the other side, (
6.3
) is equal to

\displaystyle\left\langle\mathbb{E}_{\mu},\Omega\left[\frac{uX^{(-k)}}{(1-q\sigma^{-1})(t\sigma-1)}\right]\TT\left[X^{(-k)}u^{-1}\right]H_{\lambda}[u\sigma^{-k}X^{\bullet}]\right\rangle_{q,t}^{\prime}

\displaystyle=\left\langle\mathbb{E}_{\mu}[\sigma^{-k}X^{\bullet}],\Omega\left[\frac{uX^{(0)}}{(1-q\sigma^{-1})(t\sigma-1)}\right]\TT\left[X^{(0)}u^{-1}\right]H_{\lambda}[uX^{\bullet}]\right\rangle_{q,t}^{\prime}

\displaystyle=\left\langle\mathbb{E}_{\mu}^{(k)},\Omega\left[\frac{uX^{(0)}}{(1-q\sigma^{-1})(t\sigma-1)}\right]H_{\lambda}[1+uX^{\bullet}]\right\rangle_{q,t}^{\prime}

(6.4)

\displaystyle=\Omega\left[-u\left(\frac{D_{\mu}^{\bullet}}{(1-q)(1-t)}\right)^{(k)}\right]H_{\lambda}[1+u\sigma^{k}D_{\mu}^{\bullet}].

The
\Omega
-terms in (
6.2
) (
6.4
) are equal to

(6.5)

\displaystyle\Omega\left[-u\left(\frac{D_{\lambda}^{\bullet}}{(1-q)(1-t)}\right)^{(k)}\right]

\displaystyle=\Omega\left[-uB_{\lambda}^{(k)}\right]\Omega\left[u\left(\frac{1}{(1-q)(1-t)}\right)^{(k)}\right]\text{ and }

\displaystyle\Omega\left[-u\left(\frac{D_{\mu}^{\bullet}}{(1-q)(1-t)}\right)^{(k)}\right]

\displaystyle=\Omega\left[-uB_{\mu}^{(k)}\right]\Omega\left[u\left(\frac{1}{(1-q)(1-t)}\right)^{(k)}\right].

The common factors in (
6.5
) will cancel.
Finally, because
\mathrm{core}(\mu)=w_{0}\sigma^{-k}\mathrm{core}(\lambda)
, by Proposition
2.4
,
\mathrm{core}(\mu)
and
\mathrm{core}(\lambda)
have the same color
k
boxes, so their corresponding factors will cancel as well.
Equation (
6.1
) follows after rearranging the terms.
∎

6.1.2.
Evaluation formulas

In Theorem
6.1
, considering the case where
\mu=w_{0}\sigma^{-k}\mathrm{core}(\lambda)
yields a concrete evaluation formula:

Corollary 6.2
.

For
k\in\mathbb{Z}/r\mathbb{Z}
, we have:

(6.6)

H_{\lambda}\left[1+u\sigma^{k}\iota D_{w_{0}\sigma^{-k}\mathrm{core}(\lambda)}^{\bullet}\right]=\prod_{\begin{subarray}{c}\square\in\lambda\backslash\mathrm{core}(\lambda)\\
\bar{c}_{\square}=k\end{subarray}}(1-u\chi_{\square}).

In the limit, as
u\rightarrow\infty
, (
6.6
) becomes:

Corollary 6.3
.

For
k\in\mathbb{Z}/r\mathbb{Z}
, we have:

H_{\lambda}\left[\sigma^{k}\iota D_{w_{0}\sigma^{-k}\mathrm{core}(\lambda)}^{\bullet}\right]=\prod_{\begin{subarray}{c}\square\in\lambda\backslash\mathrm{core}(\lambda)\\
\bar{c}_{\square}=k\end{subarray}}(-\chi_{\square}).

6.1.3.
Shifted reciprocity

Taking the limit as
u\rightarrow\infty
in Theorem
6.1
and applying Corollary
6.3
to the denominators, we obtain a shifted analogue of Corollary
4.16
:

Corollary 6.4
.

For
k\in\mathbb{Z}/r\mathbb{Z}
and partitions
\lambda,\mu
with
\mathrm{core}(\mu)=w_{0}\sigma^{-k}\mathrm{core}(\lambda)
, we have:

\frac{H_{\lambda}\left[\sigma^{k}\iota D_{\mu}^{\bullet}\right]}{H_{\lambda}\left[\sigma^{k}\iota D_{w_{0}\sigma^{-k}\mathrm{core}(\lambda)}^{\bullet}\right]}=\frac{H_{\mu}\left[\sigma^{k}\iota D_{\lambda}^{\bullet}\right]}{H_{\mu}\left[\sigma^{k}\iota D_{w_{0}\sigma^{-k}\mathrm{core}(\mu)}^{\bullet}\right]}.

6.1.4.
An expansion of
e_{n}

For
r>1
, the single box partition
(1)
is an
r
-core.
Here, we will derive a curious expansion of
e_{n}[X^{(k)}]
in terms of
\{H_{\lambda}^{\dagger}\,|\,\mathrm{core}(\lambda)=w_{0}(1)\}
.
To make things concrete, note that
w_{0}(1)=(r^{1}1^{r-1})
.

Proposition 6.5
.

For
r\geq 3
, we have:

(6.7)

e_{n}[X^{(k)}]=(-1)^{n}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\lambda\\
\mathrm{core}(\lambda)=w_{0}(1)\\
|\mathrm{quot}(\lambda)|=n\end{subarray}}\frac{H_{\lambda}^{\dagger}[\sigma^{k}X^{\bullet}]}{N_{\lambda}}\prod_{\begin{subarray}{c}\square\in\lambda\backslash\mathrm{core}(\lambda)\\
\bar{c}_{\square}=0\end{subarray}}(1-\chi_{\square}).

Proof.

It suffices to consider the case
k=0
, as we can obtain the other cases by applying
\sigma^{k}
.
The first step is to take the limit as
u\rightarrow 1
in (
6.6
):
for
\lambda
with
\mathrm{core}(\lambda)=w_{0}(1)
,

(6.8)

H_{\lambda}\left[1+\iota D_{(1)}^{\bullet}\right]=\prod_{\begin{subarray}{c}\square\in\lambda\backslash w_{0}(1)\\
\bar{c}_{\square}=0\end{subarray}}(1-\chi_{\square}).

Now observe that for any partition
\mu
,

\displaystyle 1+uD_{\mu}^{\bullet}\Big|_{u=1}

\displaystyle=\left((1-q)(1-t)B_{\mu}^{\bullet}\right).

Equation (
6.8
) becomes:

(6.9)

H_{\lambda}\left[\iota(1-q)(1-t)B_{(1)}^{\bullet}\right]=\prod_{\begin{subarray}{c}\square\in\lambda\backslash w_{0}(1)\\
\bar{c}_{\square}=0\end{subarray}}(1-\chi_{\square}).

In the Cauchy kernel, we substitute
Y^{\bullet}
by
\iota(1-q)(1-t)B_{(1)}^{\bullet}
by (1) applying
\iota
, then (2) making the substitution of
Y^{(i)}
at
((1-q)(1-t)B_{(1)}^{\bullet})^{(i)}
:

\displaystyle\Omega_{q,t}[X^{-\bullet}Y^{\bullet}]

\displaystyle=\Omega\left[X^{(0)}\left(\frac{Y^{(0)}}{(1-q\sigma^{-1})(t\sigma-1)}\right)+\cdots+X^{(r-1)}\left(\frac{Y^{(1)}}{(1-q\sigma^{-1})(t\sigma-1)}\right)\right]

\displaystyle\overset{(1)}{\mapsto}\Omega\left[X^{(0)}\left(\frac{Y^{(0)}}{(1-q\sigma)(t\sigma^{-1}-1)}\right)+\cdots+X^{(r-1)}\left(\frac{Y^{(r-1)}}{(1-q\sigma)(t\sigma^{-1}-1)}\right)\right]

\displaystyle\overset{(2)}{\mapsto}\Omega\left[X^{(0)}\left(\frac{(1-q)(1-t)B_{(1)}^{\bullet}}{(1-q)(t-1)}\right)^{(0)}+\cdots+X^{(r-1)}\left(\frac{(1-q)(1-t)B_{(1)}^{\bullet}}{(1-q)(t-1)}\right)^{(r-1)}\right]

\displaystyle=\Omega\left[-X^{(0)}B_{(1)}^{(0)}-\cdots-X^{(r-1)}B_{(1)}^{(r-1)}\right]

\displaystyle=\Omega\left[-X^{\bullet}B^{\bullet}_{(1)}\right]

\displaystyle=\sum_{n\geq 0}(-1)^{n}\sum_{\vec{\lambda}\vdash n}e_{\vec{\lambda}}[X^{\bullet}]m_{\vec{\lambda}}[B_{(1)}^{\bullet}]

\displaystyle=\sum_{n\geq 0}(-1)^{n}e_{n}[X^{(0)}].

On the other hand, performing the same steps on

\Omega_{q,t}[X^{-\bullet}Y^{\bullet}]=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\lambda\\
\mathrm{core}(\lambda)=w_{0}(1)\end{subarray}}\frac{H_{\lambda}^{\dagger}[X^{\bullet}]H_{\lambda}[Y^{\bullet}]}{N_{\lambda}}

yields

\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\lambda\\
\mathrm{core}(\lambda)=w_{0}(1)\end{subarray}}\frac{H_{\lambda}^{\dagger}[X^{\bullet}]}{N_{\lambda}}\prod_{\begin{subarray}{c}\square\in\lambda\backslash w_{0}(1)\\
\bar{c}_{\square}=0\end{subarray}}(1-\chi_{\square})

by (
6.9
).
∎

Remark 6.6
.

Observe that we can take any eigenoperator for
\{H_{\lambda}^{\dagger}[\sigma^{k}X^{\bullet}]\,|\,\mathrm{core}(\lambda)=w_{0}(1)\}
and easily apply it to the right-hand-side of (
6.7
).
By Lemma
5.6
, one such operator is provided by
\nabla_{\sigma^{k}w_{0}(1)}
.
A more interesting operator would be some version of the “geometric nabla” defined by Haiman (cf.
[
OS23
]
).
It would also be interesting to remove the restriction on
\mathrm{core}(\lambda)
.

In the
r=1
case,
\nabla e_{n}
has a well-known expansion in terms of
\{H_{\lambda}\}
.
One familiar with modified Macdonald polynomials will note a big difference in (
6.7
) compared to the case when
r=1
.
This stems from the fact that when
r=1
,
H_{(1)}=e_{1}
, but for
r>1
,
(1)
is a core, meaning that
H_{(1)}=1
.

This forces, for instance, Theorem
6.1
to specialize differently when
r=1
and
\mu=1
:

H_{\lambda}[1-u+u(1-q)(1-t)]=(1-u+u(1-q)(1-t)B_{\lambda})\prod_{\square\in\lambda\backslash(1)}\left(1-u\chi_{\square}\right)

At
u=1
, this then gives

H_{\lambda}[(1-q)(1-t)]=(1-q)(1-t)B_{\lambda}\prod_{\square\in\lambda\backslash(1)}(1-\chi_{\square}),

and therefore

e_{n}[X]=(-1)^{n}\sum_{\lambda\vdash n}\frac{H_{\lambda}[X](1-q)(1-t)B_{\lambda}}{N_{\lambda}}\prod_{\square\in\lambda\backslash(1)}(1-\chi_{\square}).

6.2.
Interpolation

For an
r
-core
\alpha
, and any
f\in\Lambda_{q,t}^{\otimes r}
, define

(6.10)

f^{\mathfrak{F}}_{\alpha}\coloneq\nabla_{w_{0}\alpha}^{-1}\TT[-X^{(0)}](f).

This is the analogue of
\mathbf{\Pi}^{\prime}_{f}
from
[
GHT99
]
.
The following is an analogue of (I.16) from
loc. cit.

Lemma 6.7
.

Let
\lambda
be a partition with
\mathrm{core}(\lambda)=\alpha
.
For
f\in\Lambda_{q,t}^{\otimes r}
, we have

(6.11)

\displaystyle f_{\alpha}^{\mathfrak{F}}\left[\iota D_{\lambda}^{\bullet}\right]

\displaystyle=\left\langle f,H_{\lambda}[1+X^{\bullet}]\right\rangle_{q,t}^{\prime}

(6.12)

\displaystyle=\left\langle\Omega\left[\frac{X^{(0)}}{(1-q\sigma^{-1})(t\sigma-1)}\right]f,H_{\lambda}[X^{\bullet}]\right\rangle_{q,t}^{\prime}.

Proof.

We begin with Theorem
5.1
:

\displaystyle f_{\alpha}^{\mathfrak{F}}\left[\iota D_{\lambda}^{\bullet}\right]

\displaystyle=\left\langle\nabla_{w_{0}\alpha}^{-1}\TT[-X^{(0)}](f),\mathbb{E}_{\lambda}\right\rangle_{q,t}^{\prime}

(6.13)

\displaystyle=\left\langle\nabla_{w_{0}\alpha}^{-1}\TT[-X^{(0)}](f),\nabla_{\alpha}\Omega\left[\frac{X^{(0)}}{(1-q\sigma^{-1})(t\sigma-1)}\right]\TT[X^{(0)}]H_{\lambda}[X^{\bullet}]\right\rangle_{q,t}^{\prime}.

Applying adjoints, (
6.13
) becomes

(6.14)

\displaystyle\left\langle f,\TT[X^{(0)}]H_{\lambda}[X^{\bullet}]\right\rangle_{q,t}^{\prime}

\displaystyle=\left\langle f,H_{\lambda}[1+X^{\bullet}]\right\rangle_{q,t}^{\prime}

On the other hand, applying adjunction to the left-hand-side of (
6.14
) yields (
6.12
).
∎

6.2.1.
Fourier pairing

To motivate the definition (
6.10
), we present an identity generalizing a result in
[
CNO14
, 5.5]
.
In
loc. cit.
, the authors show that their identity is an infinite-variable analogue of
[
Che97
, Theorem 1.2]
.

Theorem 6.8
.

For an
r
-core
\alpha
, consider the pairing
\langle-,-\rangle^{\mathfrak{F}}_{\alpha}
on
\Lambda_{q,t}^{\otimes r}
given by

\langle f,g,\rangle^{\mathfrak{F}}_{\alpha}\coloneq\left\langle\TT[X^{(0)}]\nabla_{w_{0}\alpha}(f),\TT[X^{(0)}]\nabla_{\alpha}(g)\right\rangle_{q,t}^{\prime}.

Then for partitions
\lambda
and
\mu
with
\mathrm{core}(\lambda)=\alpha
and
\mathrm{core}(\mu)=w_{0}\mathrm{core}(\lambda)
, we have:

(6.15)

\displaystyle\left\langle H_{\mu}[X^{\bullet}],H_{\lambda}[X^{\bullet}]\right\rangle^{\mathfrak{F}}_{\alpha}

\displaystyle=H_{\mu}\left[\iota D_{\alpha}^{\bullet}\right]H_{\lambda}\left[\iota D_{\mu}^{\bullet}\right]

(6.16)

\displaystyle=H_{\lambda}\left[\iota D_{w_{0}\alpha}^{\bullet}\right]H_{\mu}\left[\iota D_{\lambda}^{\bullet}\right].

Remark 6.9
.

Observe that

\left\langle f_{\alpha}^{\mathfrak{F}},g_{w_{0}\alpha}^{\mathfrak{F}}\right\rangle_{\alpha}^{\mathfrak{F}}=\langle f,g\rangle_{q,t}^{\prime}.

Proof.

Taking adjoints, we have:

\displaystyle\left\langle H_{\mu}[X^{\bullet}],H_{\lambda}[X^{\bullet}]\right\rangle^{\mathfrak{F}}_{\alpha}

\displaystyle=\left\langle\TT[X^{(0)}]\nabla_{w_{0}\alpha}H_{\mu}[X^{\bullet}],\TT[X^{(0)}]\nabla_{\alpha}H_{\lambda}[X^{\bullet}]\right\rangle_{q,t}^{\prime}

\displaystyle=\left\langle H_{\mu}[X^{\bullet}],\nabla_{\alpha}\Omega\left[\frac{X^{(0)}}{(1-q\sigma^{-1})(t\sigma-1)}\right]\TT[X^{(0)}]\nabla_{\alpha}H_{\lambda}[X^{\bullet}]\right\rangle_{q,t}^{\prime}

\displaystyle=H_{\lambda}[\iota D_{w_{0}\alpha}^{\bullet}]\left\langle H_{\mu}[X^{\bullet}],\mathbb{E}_{\lambda}\right\rangle_{q,t}^{\prime}

\displaystyle=H_{\lambda}[\iota D_{w_{0}\alpha}^{\bullet}]H_{\mu}\left[\iota D_{\lambda}^{\bullet}\right]

This yields (
6.16
).
The proof of (
6.15
) is similar.
∎

6.2.2.
Skew functions

We take a little interlude to introduce skew wreath Macdonald polynomials.
Because we have
H_{\lambda}
and
H_{\lambda}^{\dagger}
, there are actually four flavors of wreath Macdonald skew functions; we will only focus on one.
For partitions
\lambda,\mu,\nu
with equal core
\alpha
, define the coefficients
c_{\mu^{\dagger}\nu^{\dagger}}^{\lambda^{\dagger}}
by

\displaystyle H_{\mu}^{\dagger}H_{\nu}^{\dagger}=\sum_{\mathrm{core}(\lambda)=\alpha}c_{\mu^{\dagger}\nu^{\dagger}}^{\lambda^{\dagger}}H_{\lambda}^{\dagger}.

By Lemma
4.3
, these coefficients are nonzero only if
\lambda\supset\mu,\nu
.
Note that on partitions, the embellishment
(-)^{\dagger}
is only being used to mark which version of wreath Macdonald the partition is attached to.

Definition 6.10
.

The
wreath Macdonald skew functions

H_{\lambda/\mu^{\dagger}}
are defined to be

\displaystyle H_{\lambda/\mu^{\dagger}}

\displaystyle\coloneq N_{\lambda}\sum_{\nu}\frac{c_{\mu^{\dagger}\nu^{\dagger}}^{\lambda^{\dagger}}}{N_{\nu}}H_{\nu}.

We have that
H_{\lambda/\mu^{\dagger}}\not=0
only if
\lambda\supset\mu
and
\mathrm{core}(\lambda)=\mathrm{core}(\mu)
.
By construction,
H_{\lambda/\mu\dagger}
satisfies

\langle H_{\mu}^{\dagger}f,H_{\lambda}\rangle_{q,t}^{\prime}=\langle f,H_{\lambda/\mu^{\dagger}}\rangle_{q,t}^{\prime},

which can be checked for
f=H_{\nu}^{\dagger}
.
The following is an analogue of
[
GHT99
, 1.53]
:

Lemma 6.11
.

Given two alphabets
X^{\bullet}
and
Y^{\bullet}
, we have

H_{\lambda}\left[X^{\bullet}+Y^{\bullet}\right]=\sum_{\mu}\frac{H_{\mu}[X^{\bullet}]H_{\lambda/\mu^{\dagger}}[Y^{\bullet}]}{N_{\mu}}.

Proof.

Let
\alpha=\mathrm{core}(\lambda)
.
As in
loc. cit.
, the proof proceeds by considering

(6.17)

\Omega_{q,t}\left[\left(X+Y\right)^{-\bullet}Z^{\bullet}\right]=\Omega_{q,t}\left[X^{-\bullet}Z^{\bullet}\right]\Omega_{q,t}\left[Y^{-\bullet}Z^{\bullet}\right].

Expanding both sides of (
6.17
) using Proposition
2.14
, we have

\displaystyle\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\lambda\\
\mathrm{core}(\lambda)=\alpha\end{subarray}}\frac{H_{\lambda}[X^{\bullet}+Y^{\bullet}]H_{\lambda}^{\dagger}[Z^{\bullet}]}{N_{\lambda}}

\displaystyle=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\mu,\nu\\
\mathrm{core}(\mu)=\mathrm{core}(\nu)=\alpha\end{subarray}}\frac{H_{\mu}[X^{\bullet}]H_{\nu}[Y^{\bullet}]H_{\mu}^{\dagger}[Z^{\bullet}]H_{\nu}^{\dagger}[Z^{\bullet}]}{N_{\mu}N_{\nu}}

\displaystyle=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\lambda,\mu,\nu\\
\mathrm{core}(\lambda)=\mathrm{core}(\mu)=\mathrm{core}(\nu)=\alpha\end{subarray}}\frac{c_{\mu^{\dagger}\nu^{\dagger}}^{\lambda^{\dagger}}H_{\mu}[X^{\bullet}]H_{\nu}[Y^{\bullet}]H_{\lambda}^{\dagger}[Z^{\bullet}]}{N_{\mu}N_{\nu}}

\displaystyle=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\lambda,\mu\\
\mathrm{core}(\lambda)=\mathrm{core}(\mu)=\alpha\end{subarray}}\frac{H_{\mu}[X^{\bullet}]H_{\lambda/\mu^{\dagger}}[Y^{\bullet}]H_{\lambda}^{\dagger}[Z^{\bullet}]}{N_{\mu}N_{\lambda}}.

The result follows from applying
\langle-,H_{\lambda}[Z^{\bullet}]\rangle_{q,t}^{\prime}
to both sides.
∎

6.2.3.
Wreath Macdonald interpolation polynomials

Finally, we present wreath analogues of (modified) Macdonald interpolation polynomials
[
Sah96
,
Kno97
]
.
Our approach and results are as in
[
GHT99
, Theorem I.4]
.

Definition 6.12
.

The
wreath Macdonald interpolation polynomial

\mathfrak{H}_{\mu}\in\Lambda_{q,t}^{\otimes r}
is defined to satisfy the following conditions:

•

\mathfrak{H}_{\mu}
has highest degree
|\mathrm{quot}(\mu)|
;

•

\mathfrak{H}_{\mu}[\iota D_{\lambda}^{\bullet}]=0
if
\mathrm{core}(\lambda)=\mathrm{core}(\mu)
,
|\lambda|\leq|\mu|
, and
\lambda\not=\mu
;

•

\mathfrak{H}_{\mu}[\iota D_{\mu}^{\bullet}]=1
.

Lemma 6.13
.

If
\mathfrak{H}_{\mu}
exists, then it is unique.

Proof.

Set
\alpha=\mathrm{core}(\mu)
.
Let
\mathfrak{H}_{\mu}
and
\mathfrak{H}_{\mu}^{\prime}
both satisfy the conditions in Definition
6.12
.
We then have that
\mathfrak{D}_{\mu}\coloneq\mathfrak{H}_{\mu}-\mathfrak{H}_{\mu}^{\prime}
satisfies
\mathfrak{D}_{\mu}[\iota D_{\lambda}^{\bullet}]=0
for all
\lambda
with
\mathrm{core}(\lambda)=\alpha
and
|\lambda|\leq|\mu|
.
By Theorem
6.8
, this implies that

\langle\mathfrak{D}_{\mu},H_{\lambda}\rangle_{\alpha}^{\mathfrak{F}}=\left\langle\TT[X^{(0)}]\nabla_{w_{0}\alpha}\mathfrak{D}_{\mu},\TT[X^{(0)}]\nabla_{\alpha}H_{\lambda}\right\rangle_{q,t}^{\prime}=0

for all such
\lambda
.
This implies that
\mathfrak{D}_{\mu}=0
because:

•

\TT[X^{(0)}]\nabla_{w_{0}\alpha}\mathfrak{D}_{\mu}
has highest degree at most
|\mathrm{quot}(\mu)|
;

•

the set

\left\{\TT[X^{(0)}]\nabla_{\alpha}H_{\lambda}\,\middle|\,\mathrm{core}(\lambda)=\alpha,\,|\lambda|\leq|\mu|\right\}

is a basis for the subspace of
\Lambda_{q,t}^{\otimes r}
consisting of elements with degree no larger than
|\mathrm{quot}(\mu)|
;

•

\TT[X^{(0)}]\nabla_{w_{0}\alpha}
is invertible.∎

Theorem 6.14
.

The interpolation polynomial
\mathfrak{H}_{\mu}
exists and is given by

\mathfrak{H}_{\mu}=\frac{1}{N_{\mu}}\left(H_{\mu}^{\dagger}\right)_{\alpha}^{\mathfrak{F}}=\frac{1}{N_{\mu}}\nabla_{w_{0}\mathrm{core}(\mu)}^{-1}H_{\mu}^{\dagger}[X^{\bullet}-1].

Moreover, it satisfies

\mathfrak{H}_{\mu}\left[\iota D_{\lambda}^{\bullet}\right]=\frac{H_{\lambda/\mu^{\dagger}}[1]}{N_{\mu}}

for all
\lambda
with
\mathrm{core}(\lambda)=\mathrm{core}(\mu)
.
In particular, the evaluation above is nonzero only if
\lambda\supset\mu
.

Proof.

By (
6.11
) and Lemma
6.11
, we have

\displaystyle\mathfrak{H}_{\mu}[\iota D_{\lambda}^{\bullet}]

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{N_{\mu}}\langle H_{\mu}^{\dagger},H_{\lambda}[1+X^{\bullet}]\rangle_{q,t}^{\prime}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{N_{\mu}}\left\langle H_{\mu}^{\dagger},\sum_{\nu}\frac{H_{\nu}[X^{\bullet}]H_{\lambda/\nu^{\dagger}}[1]}{N_{\nu}}\right\rangle_{q,t}^{\prime}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{N_{\mu}}H_{\lambda/\mu^{\dagger}}[1].

Finally, note that
H_{\mu/\mu^{\dagger}}=N_{\mu}
.
∎

Remark 6.15
.

Note that
\{\mathfrak{H}_{\mu}\,|\,\mathrm{core}(\mu)=\alpha\}
is not an orthogonal basis for
\langle-,-\rangle_{\alpha,0}^{\mathfrak{F}}
.
A dual orthogonal basis is given by

\mathfrak{H}_{\mu}^{\dagger}\coloneq\nabla_{\alpha}^{-1}H_{\mu}\left[X^{\bullet}-1\right].

6.3.
Kostka coefficients

The
wreath
(q,t)
-Kostka coefficients
are the coefficients
K_{\vec{\gamma},\mu}=K_{\vec{\gamma},\mu}(q,t)
appearing in the expansion

H_{\mu}=\sum_{\vec{\gamma},\mu}K_{\vec{\gamma},\mu}s_{\vec{\gamma}}[X^{\bullet}].

For
\vec{\gamma}=(\gamma^{(0)},\gamma^{(1)},\ldots,\gamma^{(r-1)})
, let

\iota\vec{\gamma}=(\gamma^{(0)},\gamma^{(r-1)},\gamma^{(r-2)},\ldots,\gamma^{(1)}).

Because

(6.18)

s_{\vec{\gamma}}=s_{\gamma^{(0)}}[X^{(0)}]\cdots s_{\gamma^{(r-1)}}[X^{(r-1)}],

we have
s_{\vec{\gamma}}[\iota X^{\bullet}]=s_{\iota\vec{\gamma}}
.
Thus, in terms of the
\iota
-twisted Hall scalar product, we can write:

K_{\vec{\gamma},\mu}=\langle s_{\iota\vec{\gamma}},H_{\lambda}\rangle^{*}.

Finally, in terms of the wreath Macdonald pairing, we have

(6.19)

\displaystyle K_{\vec{\gamma},\mu}

\displaystyle=\left\langle s_{\iota\vec{\gamma}}\left[\frac{X^{\bullet}}{(1-q\sigma^{-1})(t\sigma-1)}\right],H_{\mu}[X^{\bullet}]\right\rangle_{q,t}^{\prime}

Here, we will give a plethystic formula for
K_{\vec{\gamma},\mu}
analogous to
[
GHT99
, Theorem I.1]
.

6.3.1.
Bernstein operators

Recall the following creation operator for Schur functions.

Proposition 6.16

(
[
Mac15
, Example I.5.29]
)
.

Suppose that
\lambda=(\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2},\ldots)
and set
\lambda_{>1}=(\lambda_{2},\lambda_{3},\ldots)
.
We then have

s_{\lambda}=\left\{z^{-\lambda_{1}}\Omega\left[Xz\right]s_{\lambda_{>1}}[X-z^{-1}]\right\}_{0}.

Because
s_{\vec{\gamma}}
is factored according to color as in (
6.18
), we can apply these creation operators for each color to add a longest row.
We will only do this for color zero.
Taking the plethysm in (
6.19
) into account, we have

Corollary 6.17
.

For
\vec{\gamma}=(\gamma^{(0)},\gamma^{(1)},\ldots,\gamma^{(r-1)})
, let
\gamma_{>1}^{(0)}=(\gamma_{2}^{(0)},\gamma_{3}^{(0)},\ldots)
.
Then

(6.20)

\displaystyle s_{\iota\vec{\gamma}}\left[\frac{X^{(0)}}{(1-q\sigma^{-1})(t\sigma-1)}\right]

\displaystyle=\prod_{i=1}^{r-1}s_{\gamma^{(i)}}\left[\frac{X^{(-i)}}{(1-q\sigma^{-1})(t\sigma-1)}\right]

\displaystyle\times\left\{z^{-\gamma_{1}^{(0)}}\Omega\left[\frac{X^{(0)}z}{(1-q\sigma^{-1})(t\sigma-1)}\right]s_{\gamma^{(0)}_{>1}}\left[\frac{X^{(0)}}{(1-q\sigma^{-1})(t\sigma-1)}-z^{-1}\right]\right\}_{0}.

6.3.2.
Plethystic formula

Recall the operation in (
6.10
).
Given an
r
-core
\alpha
, for
\vec{\gamma}=(\gamma^{(0)},\ldots,\gamma^{(r-1)})
, set

k_{\vec{\gamma}}^{\alpha}\coloneq\left(s_{\gamma^{(0)}}\left[\frac{X^{(0)}}{(1-q\sigma^{-1})(t\sigma-1)}-1\right]\prod_{i=1}^{r-1}s_{\gamma^{(i)}}\left[\frac{X^{(-i)}}{(1-q\sigma^{-1})(t\sigma-1)}\right]\right)_{\alpha}^{\mathfrak{F}}.

Let
\vec{\gamma}_{>1}=(\gamma^{(0)}_{>1},\gamma^{(1)},\ldots,\gamma^{(r-1)})
, i.e.
\vec{\gamma}
minus its longest row in color zero.
If
\gamma^{(0)}=\varnothing
, then we set
\vec{\gamma}_{>1}=\vec{\gamma}
.

Theorem 6.18
.

Let
\mu
be a partition with
\mathrm{core}(\mu)=\alpha
.
Given
\vec{\gamma}
with
|\mathrm{quot}(\mu)|=|\vec{\gamma}|
, we have

K_{\vec{\gamma},\mu}=k_{\vec{\gamma}_{>1}}^{\alpha}\left[\iota D_{\mu}^{\bullet}\right].

Proof.

Applying (
6.12
), we have:

\displaystyle k_{\vec{\gamma}_{>1}}^{\alpha}[\iota D_{\mu}^{\bullet}]

(6.21)

\displaystyle=\left\langle\Omega\left[\frac{X^{(0)}}{(1-q\sigma^{-1})(t\sigma-1)}\right]s_{\gamma^{(0)}_{>1}}\left[\frac{X^{(0)}}{(1-q\sigma^{-1})(t\sigma-1)}-1\right]\prod_{i=1}^{r-1}s_{\gamma^{(i)}}\left[\frac{X^{(-i)}}{(1-q\sigma^{-1})(t\sigma-1)}\right],H_{\mu}\right\rangle_{q,t}^{\prime}

Note that
H_{\mu}
is homogeneous of degree
|\mathrm{quot}(\mu)|
.
The summand of the same degree in the left argument of (
6.21
) is exactly the right-hand-side of (
6.20
).
The theorem follows from (
6.19
).
∎

6.4.
Global eigenfunctions

Finally, we describe an analogue of Cherednik’s
global spherical function

[
Che97
]
(called the
basic hypergeometric function
in
[
Sto14
]
).
Our analogy is imperfect because we work in the infinite-variable rather than the finite-variable case.
Nonetheless, we wish to initiate the study of such an object.

We will work with two alphabets of variables,
X^{\bullet}
and
Y^{\bullet}
.
When it is unclear, we will embellish an operator with
(-)^{X}
or
(-)^{Y}
to specify which family of variables it is acting on.

Definition 6.19
.

For
k\in\mathbb{Z}/r\mathbb{Z}
and an
r
-core
\alpha
, we define

(6.22)

\displaystyle\mathcal{H}^{(k)}_{\alpha}\left[X^{\bullet},Y^{\bullet}\right]

\displaystyle\coloneq\left(\nabla_{w_{0}\sigma^{-k}\alpha}^{Y}\right)^{-1}\TT\left[-Y^{(0)}\right]\Omega\left[\frac{-Y^{(0)}}{(1-q\sigma^{-1})(t\sigma-1)}\right]

\displaystyle\times\left(\nabla_{\sigma^{-k}\alpha}^{X}\right)^{-1}\TT\left[-X^{(-k)}\right]\Omega\left[\frac{-X^{(-k)}}{(1-q\sigma^{-1})(t\sigma-1)}\right]\left(\nabla_{\sigma^{-k}\alpha}^{X}\right)^{-1}\Omega_{q,t}[X^{-\bullet}Y^{\bullet}].

Remark 6.20
.

Notice that we only introduce shifts in the
X
-variables.
If we wish to shift the
Y
-variables by
\sigma^{-j}
for
j\in\mathbb{Z}/r\mathbb{Z}
, then by Lemma
5.6
, that is just

\mathcal{H}^{(j+k)}_{\alpha}[\sigma^{j}X^{\bullet},\sigma^{-j}Y^{\bullet}].

6.4.1.
Symmetry

\mathcal{H}^{(k)}_{\alpha}[X^{\bullet},Y^{\bullet}]
exhibits an
X\leftrightarrow Y
symmetry, but we must be careful when accounting for the cores and shifts.

Theorem 6.21
.

For
k\in\mathbb{Z}/r\mathbb{Z}
and an
r
-core
\alpha
, we have

\mathcal{H}_{\alpha}^{(k)}[X^{\bullet},Y^{\bullet}]=\mathcal{H}_{w_{0}\sigma^{-k}\alpha}^{(k)}\left[\sigma^{k}Y^{\bullet},\sigma^{-k}X^{\bullet}\right].

Proof.

By Corollary
3.32
, we have

\displaystyle\left(\nabla_{\sigma^{-k}\alpha}^{X}\right)^{-1}\Omega_{q,t}[X^{-\bullet}Y^{\bullet}]

\displaystyle=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\lambda\\
\mathrm{core}(\lambda)=\sigma^{-k}\alpha\end{subarray}}\frac{\left(\nabla_{\sigma^{-k}\alpha}^{X}\right)^{-1}H_{\lambda}[X^{\bullet}]H_{\lambda}^{\dagger}[Y^{\bullet}]}{N_{\lambda}}

\displaystyle=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\lambda\\
\mathrm{core}(\lambda)=\sigma^{-k}\alpha\end{subarray}}\frac{H_{\lambda}[X^{\bullet}]\left(\nabla_{w_{0}\sigma^{-k}\alpha}^{Y}\right)^{-1}H_{\lambda}^{\dagger}[Y^{\bullet}]}{N_{\lambda}}

\displaystyle=\left(\nabla_{w_{0}\sigma^{-k}\alpha}^{Y}\right)^{-1}\Omega_{q,t}[X^{-\bullet}Y^{\bullet}].

Thus, we also have

(6.23)

\displaystyle\mathcal{H}^{(k)}_{\alpha}\left[X^{\bullet},Y^{\bullet}\right]

\displaystyle=\left(\nabla_{\sigma^{-k}\alpha}^{X}\right)^{-1}\TT\left[-X^{(-k)}\right]\Omega\left[\frac{-X^{(-k)}}{(1-q\sigma^{-1})(t\sigma-1)}\right]

\displaystyle\times\left(\nabla_{w_{0}\sigma^{-k}\alpha}^{Y}\right)^{-1}\TT\left[-Y^{(0)}\right]\Omega\left[\frac{-Y^{(0)}}{(1-q\sigma^{-1})(t\sigma-1)}\right]\left(\nabla_{w_{0}\sigma^{-k}\alpha}^{Y}\right)^{-1}\Omega_{q,t}[X^{-\bullet}Y^{\bullet}].

We use Lemma
5.6
to appropriately change the nablas when computing
\mathcal{H}_{w_{0}\sigma^{-k}\alpha}^{(k)}[\sigma^{k}Y^{\bullet},\sigma^{-k}X^{\bullet}]
, which yields (
6.23
).
∎

Remark 6.22
.

Should we have chosen to define
\mathcal{H}_{\alpha}^{(k)}
with a shift on the
Y
-variables as well, then by Remark
6.20
, the symmmetry becomes

\mathcal{H}_{\alpha}^{(j+k)}\left[\sigma^{j}X^{\bullet},\sigma^{-j}Y^{\bullet}\right]=\mathcal{H}_{w_{0}\sigma^{j+k}\alpha}^{(j+k)}\left[\sigma^{k}Y^{\bullet},\sigma^{-k}X^{\bullet}\right].

6.4.2.
Eigenfunction equations

The following follows from applying
\sigma^{-k}
Proposition
4.17
.

Lemma 6.23
.

We have

h_{n}^{\perp}[X^{(i-k)}]\mathsf{V}^{(k)}=\mathsf{V}^{(k)}\Delta_{(k)}\left[h_{n}\left[\frac{X^{(i)}}{(1-q\sigma)(1-t\sigma^{-1})}\right]\right].

Recall that our Delta operators depend on a core
\alpha
.
Let
\Delta_{(k)}[f]_{\alpha}
denote the operator on
\Lambda_{q,t}^{\otimes r}
such that

\Delta_{(k)}[f]\left(g\otimes e^{\sigma^{-k}\alpha}\right)=\Delta_{(k)}[f]_{\alpha}(g)\otimes e^{\sigma^{-k}\alpha}.

Theorem 6.24
.

\mathcal{H}_{\alpha}^{(k)}[X^{\bullet},Y^{\bullet}]
solves the bispectral problem: for any
f\in\Lambda_{q,t}^{\otimes r}
,

(6.24)

\displaystyle\Delta_{(k)}\left[f\right]^{X}_{\alpha}\left(\mathcal{H}_{\alpha}^{(k)}[X^{\bullet},Y^{\bullet}]\right)

\displaystyle=\left(f\left[-\sigma^{k}\iota Y^{\bullet}\right]\right)_{\sigma^{-k}\alpha}^{\mathfrak{F}}\mathcal{H}_{\alpha}^{(k)}[X^{\bullet},Y^{\bullet}],\text{ and }

(6.25)

\displaystyle\Delta_{(0)}\left[f\right]^{Y}_{w_{0}\sigma^{-k}\alpha}\left(\mathcal{H}_{\alpha}^{(k)}[X^{\bullet},Y^{\bullet}]\right)

\displaystyle=\left(f\left[-\iota X^{\bullet}\right]\right)_{w_{0}\sigma^{-k}\alpha}^{\mathfrak{F}}\mathcal{H}_{\alpha}^{(k)}[X^{\bullet},Y^{\bullet}].

where
f_{\alpha}^{\mathfrak{F}}
was defined in (
6.10
).

Proof.

First consider (
6.24
).
Using Lemma
6.23
, we have:

\displaystyle\Delta_{(k)}\left[h_{n}\left[\frac{X^{(i)}}{(1-q\sigma)(1-t\sigma^{-1})}\right]\right]^{X}_{\alpha}\left(\mathcal{H}_{\alpha}^{(k)}[X^{\bullet},Y^{\bullet}]\right)

(6.26)

\displaystyle=\left(\nabla_{w_{0}\sigma^{-k}\alpha}^{Y}\right)^{-1}\TT\left[-Y^{(0)}\right]\Omega\left[\frac{-Y^{(0)}}{(1-q\sigma^{-1})(t\sigma-1)}\right]

(6.27)

\displaystyle\quad\times\left(\nabla_{\sigma^{-k}\alpha}^{X}\right)^{-1}\TT\left[-X^{(-k)}\right]\Omega\left[\frac{-X^{(-k)}}{(1-q\sigma^{-1})(t\sigma-1)}\right]\left(\nabla_{\sigma^{-k}\alpha}^{X}\right)^{-1}h_{n}^{\perp}[X^{(i-k)}]\Omega_{q,t}[X^{-\bullet}Y^{\bullet}].

Notice that

\TT[X^{(i-k)}u]\left(\Omega_{q,t}[X^{-\bullet}Y^{\bullet}]\right)=\Omega_{q,t}[X^{-\bullet}Y^{\bullet}]\Omega\left[\frac{Y^{(k-i)}u}{(1-q\sigma^{-1})(t\sigma-1)}\right].

Therefore, in (
6.27
),

\displaystyle h_{n}^{\perp}[X^{(i-k)}]\Omega_{q,t}[X^{-\bullet}Y^{\bullet}]

\displaystyle=h_{n}\left[\frac{Y^{(k-i)}}{(1-q\sigma^{-1})(t\sigma-1)}\right]\Omega_{q,t}[X^{-\bullet}Y^{\bullet}]

\displaystyle=h_{n}\left[\sigma^{k}\iota\left(\frac{Y^{(i)}}{(1-q\sigma)(t\sigma^{-1}-1)}\right)\right]\Omega_{q,t}[X^{-\bullet}Y^{\bullet}].

Commuting past (
6.26
) yields

\Delta_{(k)}\left[h_{n}\left[\frac{X^{(i)}}{(1-q\sigma)(1-t\sigma^{-1})}\right]\right]^{X}_{\alpha}\left(\mathcal{H}_{\alpha}^{(k)}[X^{\bullet},Y^{\bullet}]\right)=\left(h_{n}\left[\sigma^{k}\iota\left(\frac{Y^{(i)}}{(1-q\sigma)(t\sigma^{-1}-1)}\right)\right]\right)_{\sigma^{-k}\alpha}^{\mathfrak{F}}\mathcal{H}_{\alpha}^{(k)}.

Because these modified
h_{n}
generate
\Lambda_{q,t}^{\otimes r}
, (
6.24
) follows for general
f
.
The proof of (
6.25
) is similar, after applying (
6.23
).
∎

Remark 6.25
.

In the spirit of Remark
6.20
, we have:

\displaystyle\Delta_{(k)}\left[f\right]^{X}_{\alpha}\left(\mathcal{H}_{\alpha}^{(j+k)}[\sigma^{j}X^{\bullet},\sigma^{-j}Y^{\bullet}]\right)

\displaystyle=\left(f\left[-\sigma^{k}\iota Y^{\bullet}\right]\right)_{\sigma^{-k}\alpha}^{\mathfrak{F}}\mathcal{H}_{\alpha}^{(j+k)}[\sigma^{j}X^{\bullet},\sigma^{-j}Y^{\bullet}]

\displaystyle\Delta_{(j)}\left[f\right]^{Y}_{w_{0}\sigma^{-k}\alpha}\left(\mathcal{H}_{\alpha}^{(j+k)}[\sigma^{j}X^{\bullet},\sigma^{-j}Y^{\bullet}]\right)

\displaystyle=\left(f\left[-\sigma^{j}\iota X^{\bullet}\right]\right)_{w_{0}\sigma^{-k}\alpha}^{\mathfrak{F}}\mathcal{H}_{\alpha}^{(j+k)}[\sigma^{j}X^{\bullet},\sigma^{-j}Y^{\bullet}].

Remark 6.26
.

In the finite-variable setting of
[
Che97
]
, the analogue of
\mathsf{V}
is written in terms of the
gaussian
, which commutes with multiplication operators.
Here, we can drop the left two nabla operators in the definition of
\mathcal{H}_{\alpha}^{(k)}
, and then
f
would not be modified by
(-)_{\alpha}^{\mathfrak{F}}
as in Theorem
6.24
.

6.4.3.
Kernel of the Fourier pairing

Finally, let us mention a simpler variant, which is the reproducing kernel for the Fourier pairing
\langle-,-\rangle_{\alpha}^{\mathfrak{F}}
defined in
6.2.1
.

Definition 6.27
.

For an
r
-core
\alpha
, we set

\mathcal{F}_{\alpha}[X^{\bullet},Y^{\bullet}]\coloneq(\nabla_{\alpha}^{X})^{-1}\TT[-X^{(0)}](\nabla_{w_{0}\alpha}^{Y})^{-1}\TT[-Y^{(0)}]\Omega_{q,t}[X^{-\bullet}Y^{\bullet}].

Theorem 6.28
.

\mathcal{F}_{\alpha}
satisfies the following properties:

(1)

Symmetry:

\mathcal{F}_{\alpha}[X^{\bullet},Y^{\bullet}]=\mathcal{F}_{w_{0}\alpha}[Y^{\bullet},X^{\bullet}].

(2)

Eigenfunction equations: for
f\in\Lambda_{q,t}^{\otimes r}
,

\displaystyle\Delta[f]_{\alpha}^{X}\mathcal{F}_{\alpha}[X^{\bullet},Y^{\bullet}]

\displaystyle=f[-\iota Y^{\bullet}]\mathcal{F}_{\alpha}[X^{\bullet},Y^{\bullet}]~\text{ and }

\displaystyle\Delta[f]_{w_{0}\alpha}^{Y}\mathcal{F}_{\alpha}[X^{\bullet},Y^{\bullet}]

\displaystyle=f[-\iota X^{\bullet}]\mathcal{F}_{\alpha}[X^{\bullet},Y^{\bullet}].

(3)

Specialization to Macdonald: for
\lambda
with
\mathrm{core}(\lambda)=\alpha
,

\displaystyle\mathcal{F}_{\alpha}[X^{\bullet},\iota D_{\lambda}^{\bullet}]

\displaystyle=\frac{H_{\lambda}[X^{\bullet}]}{H_{\lambda}[\iota D_{w_{0}\alpha}^{\bullet}]}~\text{ and }

\displaystyle\mathcal{F}_{\alpha}[\iota D_{w_{0}\lambda}^{\bullet},Y^{\bullet}]

\displaystyle=\frac{H_{w_{0}\lambda}[Y^{\bullet}]}{H_{w_{0}\lambda}[\iota D_{\alpha}^{\bullet}]}.

Proof.

Property (1) is obvious.
The other two follow from manipulations similar to those done in the proof of Theorem
6.21
.
Namely,

\displaystyle\mathcal{F}_{\alpha}[X^{\bullet},Y^{\bullet}]

\displaystyle=(\nabla_{\alpha}^{X})^{-1}\TT[-X^{(0)}](\nabla_{w_{0}\alpha}^{Y})^{-1}\TT[-Y^{(0)}]\Omega_{q,t}[X^{-\bullet}Y^{\bullet}]

\displaystyle=(\nabla_{\alpha}^{X})^{-1}\TT[-X^{(0)}]\Omega\left[\frac{-X^{(0)}}{(1-q\sigma^{-1})(t\sigma-1)}\right](\nabla_{w_{0}\alpha}^{Y})^{-1}\Omega_{q,t}[X^{-\bullet}Y^{\bullet}]

\displaystyle=(\nabla_{\alpha}^{X})^{-1}\TT[-X^{(0)}]\Omega\left[\frac{-X^{(0)}}{(1-q\sigma^{-1})(t\sigma-1)}\right](\nabla_{\alpha}^{X})^{-1}\Omega_{q,t}[X^{-\bullet}Y^{\bullet}],

which is
\mathsf{V}^{-1}
at
e^{\alpha}
applied to the
X
-variables.
We prove the first eigenfunction equation as in Theorem
6.24
, using the second property in Proposition
4.17
. The first specialization to Macdonalds is the fourth property of Proposition
4.17
.
A similar conclusion for the
Y^{\bullet}
can be made from the following equalities:

\displaystyle\mathcal{F}_{\alpha}[X^{\bullet},Y^{\bullet}]

\displaystyle=(\nabla_{\alpha}^{X})^{-1}\TT[-X^{(0)}](\nabla_{w_{0}\alpha}^{Y})^{-1}\TT[-Y^{(0)}]\Omega_{q,t}[X^{-\bullet}Y^{\bullet}]

\displaystyle=(\nabla_{w_{0}\alpha}^{Y})^{-1}\TT[-Y^{(0)}]\Omega\left[\frac{-Y^{(0)}}{(1-q\sigma^{-1})(t\sigma-1)}\right](\nabla_{w_{0}\alpha}^{Y})^{-1}\Omega_{q,t}[X^{-\bullet}Y^{\bullet}].\qed

7.
